Audit 1/72 · Agent: `/root/audit01_host_overengineering` · Lens: overengineering

### Overengineering

Module: Host composition (audit 01/72).

Source: `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, verified at `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e`.

Scope inspected: root `Cargo.toml`, `build.rs`, `src/lib.rs`, the Boot Profile and world-ingestion composition in `src/boot/mod.rs`, its profile/ingestion tests, `src/server_app/mod.rs`, `src/presentation_adapters.rs`, compatibility modules under `src/server/` and `src/workshop/`, and the headless app builder. Native app assembly and browser bridge call sites were sampled to establish real consumers. Repository-wide Rust searches covered `src/`, `tests/`, and `examples/` for the dependency and marker candidates below; extracted package manifests and implementation sites were checked where ownership matters. Read the wiki schema, domain vocabulary, wiki index, reusable-layer guide and `pasm/spec/architecture/reusable-layers.yaml`. The tracked file search found only the root `AGENTS.md`; `docs/adr/` is absent.

No builds or tests were run, as required for this read-only audit. Findings are opportunities for subsequent changes, not claims that an edited configuration has passed compilation.

#### 1. Remove inherited root dependency declarations that no longer have root consumers

**Evidence and callers.** Root `Cargo.toml:256` declares `gltf`, `Cargo.toml:258` declares `ktx2`, and `Cargo.toml:365`, `Cargo.toml:367`, and `Cargo.toml:368` declare `noise`, `toml_edit`, and `strum`. Searches across all root Rust sources, integration tests and examples found no executable references to these five crate names. There are comments mentioning noise, GLTF and strum, but these are not uses. In particular, `src/headless/report.rs:42` describes the origin of a discriminant; consuming a discriminant generated in another package does not require that package's derive dependency at the consumer.

The implementation consumers and their dependency declarations now live below the host:

- KTX decoding and GLTF validation are in `crates/phoenix-content/src/pack_asset_validation.rs:33` and `:124`, with direct dependencies in `crates/phoenix-content/Cargo.toml:15` and `:16`.
- Perlin sampling is in `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/asteroids/spawner.rs:851`, with `noise` declared in `crates/phoenix-simulation/Cargo.toml:43`.
- Workshop document editing imports `toml_edit` at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/workshop/document.rs:6` and `workshop/entity.rs:50`, with its declaration at `crates/phoenix-simulation/Cargo.toml:45`.
- Wire discriminants derive strum traits in `crates/phoenix-model/src/messages.rs:1954` and `:2435`, and that owner declares the dependency at `crates/phoenix-model/Cargo.toml:11`.

**Why this is overengineering.** These are configuration carried forward from a larger root implementation. They make the host appear responsible for libraries whose work now belongs to extracted owners, and give dependency updates additional version/feature declarations to inspect. This is a concrete maintenance cost, even though it is small. They also leave those libraries available for accidental new root imports without a manifest change that would expose the ownership decision in review.

**Concrete opportunity / deletion test.** Delete these five root dependency entries, preserving their owner declarations. No substitute abstraction, dependency facade or new crate is needed. Their used features already have declarations in actual consumer packages. Keep the compatibility exports in `src/lib.rs:5` and `src/server/mod.rs:1`; re-exporting owner APIs is a separate requirement and does not require the root to redeclare every implementation dependency of those owners.

**Invariants and verification.** Preserve all native/WASM feature behavior and the established deterministic math configuration. This finding deliberately does not extend to `glam`, `nalgebra`, `parry3d` or `rapier3d`: an otherwise unused direct dependency can intentionally select determinism features. Likewise, `indexmap` has root test and headless graph consumers, `rand` has the native join-code generator, and `rhai`/`vellum-script`/`vellum-rng` have integration-test consumers; a blanket unused-looking dependency purge is not justified.

Existing layer checks (`scripts/check-layers.mjs`, named by the reusable-layer PASM slice) and the normal native/WASM compile configurations are appropriate follow-up evidence. Verify root examples and integration targets as well as the library. A static text search cannot establish the absence of every procedural-macro or generated-code dependency. Compilation after removal is the missing verification; no new unit test is needed for deleting redundant manifest entries.

**Tradeoffs and confidence.** High confidence that the five names have no handwritten root Rust consumer; medium-high confidence that removal is behavior-neutral pending compilation and feature-graph verification. Do not promise smaller binaries, fewer downloaded packages or faster builds: these libraries remain transitively used. The gain is five fewer dependency declarations and less misleading host configuration, not removal of the implementations.

#### 2. Keep boot-path test markers out of production ECS registration

**Evidence and callers.** `src/boot/mod.rs:403` and `:407` define private `RenderSurrogateApplied` and `RenderStackApplied` resources. `render_surrogate` inserts the former at `:673`; `render_stack` inserts the latter at `:708`. Their declared purpose is to reveal the chosen composition branch when GPU-less tests cannot distinguish the two paths by their shared asset/message floor. The only behavioral readers found are the unit assertions in `src/boot/tests.rs:282`, especially `:296` and `:312`. Production callers do not read them.

The runtime registration is observable outside those tests: `tests/authoritative_state_enumeration.rs:258` carries an explicit `project_phoenix::boot::RenderSurrogateApplied` exemption. Thus a marker introduced for a private branch test also occupies the root app's production resource inventory and requires knowledge in the independent authoritative-state enumeration guard.

**Why this is overengineering.** The markers themselves are reasonable test probes; carrying them in every ordinary app is unnecessary machinery. The concrete cost is a resource registration/insertion and cross-file census exception, not a meaningful performance bottleneck. The extra authoritative-state exception is the stronger maintenance concern: a test implementation detail leaks into a separate test's inventory policy.

**Concrete opportunity / deletion test.** Restrict the private marker definitions and their insertions to unit-test builds, leaving the existing unit assertions intact. Remove the now-unneeded integration census exception after confirming its ordinary library build no longer registers that type. Do not remove the profile parity tests, replace contract assertions with markers, or infer that a marker proves a real GPU was initialized. The current comments correctly distinguish a profile choosing rendering from the target actually creating a renderer.

**Invariants and verification.** The production plugin graph, render assets/messages, ingestion order and all simulation schedules must remain identical. Unit tests at `src/boot/tests.rs:193` verify the common asset/message floor; `:282` verifies branch choice. Both remain useful. The authoritative-state enumeration integration test should see only the meaningful runtime inventory after the adjustment. These tests were inspected, not executed. No additional test framework is warranted.

**Tradeoffs and confidence.** High confidence about the reader inventory and low priority overall. A test-only cfg makes the unit-test resource inventory intentionally differ by these two probes; it should remain confined to the probes and must not guard actual plugin registration. If future runtime diagnostic tooling starts consuming the markers, retaining them could be justified, but no such consumer was found in this revision.

#### Suspected complexity retained because current requirements justify it

- **Boot profiles and ingestion axes:** `src/boot/mod.rs:90`, `:197`, and `:244` encode real differences: browser automation and GM hosts without rendering, native window/offscreen/contract surfaces, and reader/preloaded/deferred content. The headless production caller builds a reader plan at `src/headless/app.rs:225`; native assembly selects NativeHost at `src/native_host/app.rs:365`; browser bridge and disposable browser Workshop paths also call this seam. Replacing the axes with a single generic boolean or deleting the surrogate would lose shipped or tested configurations. `src/boot/tests.rs:488`, `:529`, and `:589` protect meaningfully different preload/freeze behavior.
- **Prepared ingestion:** `src/boot/mod.rs:1021` retains prepared data before installation. This is not merely a builder wrapper: preparation/installation separates validation from live world mutation, with an explicit test at `src/boot/tests.rs:628`. No deletion is proposed that would change ledger reset, compilation, freeze or materialization order.
- **Host composition wrapper:** `src/server_app/mod.rs:11` does more than forward simulation registration. It installs host-owned state declarations, admission/debug draining, visibility and endpoint adapters, and optional presentation plugins. Its existence maintains the one-way package boundary recorded in PASM. Flattening it back into Simulation would make the renderer-independent package depend on host concerns.
- **Compatibility facades:** `src/lib.rs:5`, `src/server/mod.rs:1`, and `src/workshop/mod.rs:1` preserve historical API paths while owners move. Their small indirection is explicitly required by the reusable-layer design and is not evidence of unnecessary duplication.
- **Windows SDK staging:** `build.rs:61` and following code address DLL discovery before process startup; runtime staging cannot run before the Windows loader loads the executable. The build-time and runtime mechanisms therefore cannot simply be deleted as equivalent copies. The retry stamp also has a stated cold-build dependency-order purpose. This audit did not establish a simpler compatible staging mechanism.

#### Coverage limitations

This is a host-composition audit, not an exhaustive review of every native adapter under the large root `src/` tree. The detailed native audio engine, device handling, pane-thread queues, multi-monitor layout/recovery, native Workshop jobs, complete browser bridge export inventory, replay/report internals and binary CLI implementations were not reviewed line by line. Caller searches through these areas support the specific findings above, but do not establish that all their abstractions are necessary or unnecessary. Other extracted simulation/presentation implementations were inspected only to trace ownership, not audited independently here. Build-script SDK freshness/error handling was read but not exercised on a cold SDK download or a Windows launch. No runtime or compile-time performance savings were measured.


Audit 2/72 · Agent: `/root/audit02_host_reuse` · Lens: reuse

### Reuse

Module: Host composition (`src/`, root `Cargo.toml` and `build.rs`). Source inspected: `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, verified HEAD `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e`.

Scope and orientation: read the workspace instructions, wiki schema, vocabulary, index and reusable-layers guide; inspected the reusable-layers architecture slice and relevant portions of native-delivery and deterministic-simulation. No nested AGENTS.md was found; `docs/adr` does not exist in this checkout. Inspection concentrated on boot profiles and world ingestion, native/headless hull setup, root compatibility facades, native transport adapters, demo-build configuration, and selected native presentation/preferences helpers. Followed calls into Simulation, Content, Model, Platform and Transport where needed to distinguish reuse from parallel implementations. Reviewed relevant integration-test source, without executing tests or builds.

#### 1. Headless hull setup can reuse the native path's resolve-once preparation instead of resolving and parsing the same hull twice

**Evidence and actual callers.** `src/headless/app.rs:151` is the production implementation behind `build_headless_app`, its test-override variant and the external-logging variant. After shared boot ingestion it selects the RNG at `src/headless/app.rs:308`, then at `src/headless/app.rs:326` performs an explicit readability check, calls `load_entity_config`, calls `FsTemplateLoader.load_template` solely for its ledger side effects, freezes the ledger, extracts `ship_config` and installs the two selected-hull resources. The duplicate resolution is concrete: `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/entities/include_resolve.rs:41` implements `load_entity_config` as `resolve_from_disk(path)?.parse()`, while `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/entities/loader.rs:39` resolves that same path again, records the composed TOML, parses it again, and records its primary model sidecar. The second return value is discarded by the host.

An existing host implementation already does the more reusable preparation: `src/native_host/app.rs:506` (`prepare_world_selection`) resolves once at line 582, parses that retained resolution, captures the sidecar, and returns a `PreparedWorldSelection` carrying both the parsed ship configuration and content records. Its `install` at line 486 applies the captured records, freezes the ledger and inserts `PendingShipConfig` and `SelectedShipResource`. It serves boot through `install_world_selection` at line 499 and deferred loading through `src/native_host/world_load.rs:820`. This is therefore an actual working implementation, not a proposed new content subsystem.

**Why it matters.** Headless maintains a second version of selected-hull preparation and repeats filesystem/include work after the bulk preload. Its station configuration comes from the first resolution while its ledger records come from the second. With stable files these agree; a file or overlay changing between resolutions could make the declared content identity differ from the configuration installed. That last scenario is a hypothesis, not a reproduced bug. The certain costs are duplicated resolution/parsing and two host sites that must preserve the same hull-plus-sidecar freeze rules. The identical CLI/world/random seed precedence at `src/headless/app.rs:308` and `src/native_host/app.rs:513` is an additional small overlap in this same setup seam, not a separate significant finding.

**Concrete change opportunity.** Reuse the resolved-template/captured-record portion of the native preparation for headless setup, retaining the rich error mapping at the adapter boundary. A narrow common preparation operation in host composition can be consumed by both adapters; it need not import native window/pane policy into the headless runner. At minimum, headless can use the same existing `resolve_from_disk`, retained `ResolvedTemplate`, and `capture_primary_sidecar_from_fs` approach rather than re-entering `FsTemplateLoader` just to obtain side effects. The latter helper is already implemented at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/entities/model_markers.rs:295`; no new resolver or ledger is needed.

**Compatibility differences and invariants.** Do not blindly call the entire native selection routine. Native chooses an authored default under a curated allowlist, canonicalizes the selected key, checks template-cache membership, and rejects participant pane names shadowing Stations. Headless chooses its explicit/default CLI hull and supports `--side-a` rewriting. Its explicit first read preserves the established `could not read ship` error. These are different policies. Preserve headless path semantics unless separately reviewed, OS RNG draw timing, CLI-over-world seed precedence and `SeedSource`, insertion before lobby consumption, included-fragment and sidecar identity, and the ledger's declared-content scope. Bulk preloading must still not become a hash of every template in the repository. Deferred native preparation must remain free of live-world installation until all refusal gates pass.

**Existing verification and missing evidence.** `tests/headless_runner.rs:79` checks a fully backfilled player ship, line 188 checks selected-hull world overrides, line 339 checks deterministic repeatability, and line 569 checks that an unseeded run reports its seed. `tests/native_host_sim.rs:313` checks the chosen hull reaches client configuration, line 517 checks the missing-cache refusal, and line 594 checks curated hull selection. `tests/native_host_lobby.rs:668` checks runtime/boot world identity; its materialization sibling at `tests/native_host_lobby/materialization.rs:97` checks composed worlds after different lobby delays. Those are useful integration boundaries, but they do not directly demonstrate that headless parses a selected included hull once and records exactly the bytes used. A focused preparation test using an included hull plus primary sidecar, and comparison of installed configuration and captured ledger keys/digests, would close that gap. Keep independent host integration tests after consolidating production preparation.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limitations.** High confidence in the duplicate resolve/parse and available replacement mechanism; medium confidence that extracting the whole preparation object is worth more than a focused headless rewrite. Do not expand this into a universal host configuration abstraction. No runtime performance measurement or mutable-file race reproduction was performed.

#### 2. The demo-build gate no longer has the single literal its reuse contract claims

**Evidence and consumers.** Root `src/demo_build_value.rs:23` declares `DEMO_VALUE = "true"`. Root `build.rs:25` includes it, and its lines 30–43 emit the environment invalidation, cfg registration and enabled cfg. Each extracted build script also includes that root file: `crates/phoenix-model/build.rs:1`, `crates/phoenix-presentation/build.rs:1`, and `crates/phoenix-simulation/build.rs:1`. However, `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/demo_build_value.rs:23` contains another copy of the same declaration and explanatory comments. `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/build_flags.rs:21` includes this local copy, and `demo_flag_from_env` at line 35 compares against it. Thus compilation gates and the runtime flag exposed to the page are not structurally coupled to one literal anymore.

The three extracted build scripts also duplicate the same small cfg-emission function. Separate build-script execution is necessary because Cargo cfg flags are crate-local; that necessity does not require copying the literal or policy implementation. The existing `include!` mechanism already solves the build-script-cannot-import-its-own-library constraint.

**Why it matters.** There is no present value mismatch: both copies are `"true"`. The problem is a lost prevention mechanism at a meaningful boundary. A future edit can change the constant used to remove debug/upload routes without changing the value used by `is_demo_build`, or vice versa. Both copies explicitly document that sharing one literal was chosen to prevent exactly that drift. The refactor retained the documentation while duplicating the source of truth.

**Concrete change opportunity.** Keep one canonical small, dependency-free flag source and make the runtime accessor and all four build scripts include it. If consolidating the repeated cfg emission at the same time, expose only a build-support function and keep each script responsible for invoking it; root build.rs must still run SDK staging. Update the relevant `rerun-if-changed` paths for the actual shared source. This can use the existing include-based technique, without introducing a runtime dependency from a reusable package onto the root host or creating another package just for this constant.

**Behavior to preserve.** Match only the exact value `true`; do not reinterpret `TRUE`, `1`, an empty value or the size-optimization flag. The public-demo route must remain absent via `#[cfg]`, not merely guarded at runtime. Every crate containing cfg-gated code still needs its own build-script cfg output. Environment changes and edits to the shared file must invalidate the affected build. Root-only Windows Ultralight staging remains a separate responsibility.

**Existing verification and missing evidence.** `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/build_flags_tests.rs:5` onward exercises exact flag spelling; line 48 compares the runtime answer with the simulation crate's compiled cfg. Its preceding comment at lines 42–43 describes the literal as structurally shared, which is no longer accurate. A test run with the variable unset alone cannot detect divergence between two different nonempty literals; the configured demo test path must remain part of verification. These unit tests also do not alone verify the root, Model and Presentation cfg behavior. After consolidation, verify representative gated APIs in demo and ordinary configurations and incremental invalidation when the environment changes. No such checks were run during this read-only audit.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limitations.** High confidence: the duplicate source files and different include paths are directly visible. Consolidating only the literal is a small, clear improvement. Sharing the eight-line build script body is lower value and optional; preserve easy-to-read crate entry points. This is a maintainability/prevention finding, not a claim that the current demo build exposes forbidden routes.

#### Reviewed similarities that do not justify deduplication

- **Root compatibility paths are already reuse.** `src/lib.rs:5` re-exports Simulation; `src/lib.rs:26` aliases Platform's native-file mechanism. `src/server_app/mod.rs:11` calls Simulation's registration before adding host/presentation adapters. The wrapper has real ordering and classification responsibilities, including the debug gate at line 29 and endpoint input adapter at line 57. Removing it as a duplicate would remove host behavior rather than consolidate two plugin inventories.
- **World startup and runtime loading now share their materialization implementation.** `src/native_host/world_load.rs:185` invokes `world::materialization::register` for the runtime schedule; `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/world/server.rs:876` invokes it for Startup. Runtime loading also shares `boot::prepare_world_ingest` at `src/native_host/world_load.rs:775`. The distinct schedules, mint parking and native tail are required lifecycle adaptations. `tests/native_host_lobby/materialization.rs:260` checks membership parity, and line 230 checks restoration of the live mint sequence. A recommendation to merge copied spawn chains would describe older code, not this checkout.
- **Native transport already consumes the extracted transport.** `src/native_host/transport.rs:40` binds the Phoenix vocabulary through `PhoenixProtocol`; `src/native_host/relay_transport.rs:10` aliases the reusable relay and implements only Phoenix stamp/codec/identity policy at line 12. `src/native_host/relay_socket.rs:2` is a compatibility re-export. These are not parallel implementations of connections or relay supervision. The native transport integration at `tests/native_host_sim.rs:399` and reserved-token refusal at line 449 retain host Admission coverage independently of package tests.
- **Settings stores have different semantics but share the critical file write.** `src/native_host/viewscreen_locale.rs:35` validates and serializes a locale before calling `native_file::write_preferences`; the presentation store at `src/native_host/viewscreen_presentation.rs:346` preserves audio sections and has different reset/default rules. Platform already owns replacement through `crates/phoenix-platform/src/native_file.rs:33` and preference writing at line 58. Similar constructors and TOML reads alone do not justify replacing these with a generic persistence framework.
- **Build-time and runtime SDK staging are not interchangeable.** Root `build.rs:64` runs before the Windows loader can start the executable; `src/native_host/panes/ultralight.rs:206` runs in an already started process and uses `vellum_ultralight::staging`, including executable/deps-relative search. Deleting build-time staging in favor of runtime reuse would reintroduce the missing-DLL bootstrap failure. This audit did not inspect the external dependency's full staging API, so it does not claim the build-time search can or cannot reuse a narrower upstream primitive.
- **The duplicated scale formatter is real but too small to warrant another mechanism.** `src/native_host/viewscreen_presentation.rs:281` and `src/native_host/panes/os_prefs.rs:185` have almost identical bodies, but take f64 and f32 respectively. Their callers sanitize the values before injecting JS numbers. Reuse must preserve each type's display representation; indiscriminate widening can change text. This is minor local cleanup, not grounds for a new generic rendering/serialization layer.

#### Inspection limits

This is not exhaustive coverage of every file under `src/`. Unexplored in depth: audio playback and mixing, pane-thread/frame-pool integration, monitor/layout persistence and recovery, native Workshop job/process orchestration, native GM surfaces, headless report/performance/replay internals, most browser bridge exports and queue policies, and the capture/tune/performance CLI tools. Cargo features and extracted-owner dependencies were inspected for composition context, not audited for every unused direct dependency. External Vellum implementations and browser JS counterparts were not comprehensively read. Test names and assertions provide static evidence only; no test-pass claim is made. No code, documentation or specifications were modified.


Audit 3/72 · Agent: `/root/audit03_host_architecture` · Lens: architecture

### Architecture

Module: Host composition. Source revision inspected: `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e`, in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`.

Scope: root `src/`, root `Cargo.toml` and `build.rs`, with focused exploration of Boot Profile composition, native/headless/browser adapters, runtime World loading, presentation adapters, browser fleet staging and their tests. Read the architecture skill and its LANGUAGE.md, repository instructions, wiki schema/index, domain vocabulary, reusable-layers guide and PASM reusable-layers slice; consulted native-delivery and host-mesh-lockstep declarations. No nested `src/AGENTS.md` was found; `docs/adr/` does not exist in this checkout. This is a read-only source audit; no builds or tests were run.

#### 1. Deepen host composition so Boot Profile callers do not repeat the shared registration sequence and renderer decision

**Files:** `src/boot/mod.rs`, `src/server_app/mod.rs`, `src/headless/app.rs`, `src/native_host/app.rs`, `src/server/bridge.rs`, `src/boot/tests.rs`.

**Problem and evidence:** The boot module already provides substantial depth: profiles select renderer policy, reader-based versus preloaded versus deferred ingestion, template-cache requirements and error policy. However, its callers still jointly implement another ordered composition protocol:

- `src/headless/app.rs:318` explains that the chosen hull's configuration must precede LobbyPlugin; lines 344–366 insert the hull resources, install AsteroidLifecyclePlugin, ModifierCoordinationPlugin, LobbyPlugin and its broadcaster, install simulation with `render: false`, install the selected RNG and then install WorldPlugin.
- `src/native_host/app.rs:696` delegates the World/hull selection to `install_world_selection`, but lines 747–773 repeat the same plugin sequence, select `render: true` and install the selected RNG after plugin initialization. Deferred loading is an intentional extra case at line 778.
- `src/server/bridge.rs:953` repeats the plugin inventory around a browser-only ConfigCachePlugin, inserts PendingShipConfig before LobbyPlugin at line 959, and independently computes `render: !(is_automation || is_browser_gm)` at line 971. The adjacent comment explicitly records the consequence of getting that decision wrong: an asset preloader which never finishes and fleet start validation which remains false.
- `src/boot/mod.rs:148` already expresses the profile's renderer policy in `has_render_stack`; the caller must nevertheless reconstruct the applicable presentation decision. These decisions are related, although the native Contract surface deliberately has presentation plugins without a GPU and must retain that distinction.
- `src/server_app/mod.rs:11` joins simulation registration to presentation and host adapters, but does not own the surrounding lobby/World registration protocol.

This is interface complexity, not merely repeated lines. A caller must know the chosen hull's resource timing, default RNG overwrite behavior, shared plugin inventory and the relationship between its Boot Profile and later presentation installation. A new host or a profile change has several places where those facts can diverge.

**Solution:** Concentrate the common host composition protocol in the existing host composition module, with its implementation owning the shared registration sequence and the applicable presentation policy. Keep browser preload, native deferred selection, headless report collection and OS/window wiring as real adapters. This is a candidate for deepening an existing module, not a proposal for another general plugin framework or for moving host work into the reusable simulation siblings. No replacement interface is prescribed here.

**Deletion test:** Deleting boot would spread renderer and ingestion complexity across real adapters, so boot earns its keep. Deleting the compatibility wrapper in server_app would also spread presentation and host-adapter registration. The opportunity is to bring the currently repeated protocol behind that existing seam; merely extracting each plugin call into its own helper would increase the interface without producing depth.

**Benefits:** Greater locality for composition changes and leverage for new Boot Profiles: a caller chooses host behavior without reconstructing shared inventory and ordering. Tests could exercise the same completed composition interface as production adapters rather than partially recreating it.

**Existing tests and missing verification:** `src/boot/tests.rs:193` tests the asset/message floor for profiles, but explicitly adds simulation itself with `render: false` at line 200; it is intentionally not a test of every production adapter's final inventory. `tests/native_headless_digest.rs:165` exercises authoritative digest equivalence through real native/headless construction; its offscreen GPU case at line 187 is separately ignored for hardware reasons. `tests/native_host_sim.rs:126` covers complete native mission construction and line 313 checks selected-hull configuration. `tests/native_host_lobby.rs:668` compares entity identities across boot and runtime load, and line 773 covers a failed load leaving a pickable lobby. Preserve these independent proofs. Additional focused verification should cover the complete profile-to-presentation decision and actual shared registration through the production composition seam, especially BrowserAutomation and BrowserGameMaster, without treating a native surrogate build as proof of browser initialization.

**Invariants:** Preserve system registration slots, explicit ordering, deferred flushes, RNG initialization and UUID mint timing. Browser preloading must remain distinct from filesystem ingestion; NativeWorkshop must remain free of live simulation. Headless must not acquire a renderer or playback. A native Contract host must retain its intentional presentation classification without acquiring a GPU. Keep pure-JavaScript Consoles and current Admission unchanged.

**Tradeoffs and confidence:** High confidence in the distributed interface obligation; medium confidence in net benefit without designing the resulting composition. The common sequence is not large in executable lines, and some caller differences are essential. A change that merely relocates a large conditional without reducing caller knowledge would not be worthwhile. No current runtime failure is claimed.

#### 2. Give browser fleet staging one lifecycle test surface instead of testing only pieces of its state transition

**Files:** `src/server/browser_edge.rs`, `src/server/bridge.rs`, `src/server/bridge_tests.rs`.

**Problem and evidence:** Browser-only storage is correctly isolated, but the fleet staging lifecycle crosses that storage module and its parent in both directions:

- `src/server/bridge.rs:162` onwards defines the pending fleet and GM records; the bounded fleet-lobby queue rule is at line 395 and projection rebinding is at line 425.
- `src/server/browser_edge.rs:4` imports the parent wholesale. Its `queue_fleet_lobby_input` at line 891 reads a thread-local generation and calls the parent helper with a separate thread-local queue.
- `src/server/browser_edge.rs:961` owns the larger join transition: increment generation, decode/refuse a roster, cancel a pending join, rebind remembered managed/validation values, preserve a preceding Leave, queue adoption, and publish Pending status. `leave_fleet` immediately following that implementation increments the generation, replaces the adoption queue with Leave and publishes status.
- These operations use independently stored generation, pending adoptions, pending lobby inputs, latest projections and published status (`src/server/browser_edge.rs:71`, `:73`, `:74`, `:125`, `:131`, `:132`).
- The exported browser operations in `src/server/bridge.rs:1309`, `:1322`, `:1435` and `:1449` reach this machinery. The eventual Bevy drains remain separate (`src/server/bridge.rs:1549` and `:1848`), as they should: a synchronous browser call does not own a Bevy World.

The architectural friction is that the queue helper's interface asks callers to supply the correct generation and latest projection values, while correctness actually belongs to the whole join/leave lifecycle. The storage extraction hides RefCell access, but does not make that lifecycle directly exercisable by ordinary native tests. It is not evidence that any particular generation transition is currently wrong.

**Solution:** Deepen a fleet-staging module around the existing pending lifecycle: generation changes, supersession, remembered projections, bounded queuing and owned draining should be one implementation that the browser storage adapter invokes. Keep JavaScript values, thread-local storage and callbacks in the target adapter, and keep simulation adoption/Admission in their current owner. Avoid a generic event bus or a reusable foundation abstraction for Phoenix-specific fleet rules. No replacement interface is prescribed.

**Deletion test:** Removing browser_edge outright would put storage and borrow-lifetime concerns back in the parent bridge, so it earns its keep. The useful concentration is smaller: move the coherent lifecycle behind a seam rather than exposing its constituent mutable queue and generation to separate helper calls. A pure module here would earn its depth by owning those transitions, not merely wrapping VecDeque operations.

**Benefits:** Locality for changes involving join generations and queued lobby state; leverage for tests that execute the same complete transition used by the browser adapter. Tests would no longer have to manually supply a generation and remembered values to simulate the critical rebinding step.

**Existing tests and missing verification:** `src/server/bridge_tests.rs:310` tests absolute-sample coalescing and edge preservation, line 358 tests refusal instead of displacing an edge, and line 389 tests projection rebinding before a new generation's grant. These are valuable independent behavior checks. However, the rebinding test directly calls `rebind_fleet_lobby_projections` with supplied state; it does not drive the full browser_edge join operation that increments the generation and coordinates all the stores. The proposed test surface should cover setters-before-join, join supersession, leave-then-join within one frame, malformed replacement joins, queue refusal and subsequent owned drain/status behavior. Browser integration coverage was not exhaustively inspected, so this is a gap in the sampled native test surface, not a claim that no end-to-end test exists.

**Invariants:** Preserve generation-scoped adoption and the existing order of Leave versus Join; preserve absolute-sample coalescing without dropping meaningful edges; reject overflow honestly; keep invalid roster outcomes and status semantics unchanged. Do not retain a Bevy World in browser storage, introduce callback reentrancy under active RefCell borrows, or move authoritative decisions into JavaScript. Fleet roster validation and command Admission remain distinct from local staging.

**Tradeoffs and confidence:** Medium confidence. This adds value only if the complete lifecycle becomes easier to test through the production interface; splitting browser_edge into many shallow per-field modules would worsen locality. Its single browser storage adapter does not justify an abstraction for hypothetical native/browser interchangeability. Browser storage itself is a necessary target-specific adapter, not duplication to eliminate.

#### Inspected structures that do not justify removal

- `src/lib.rs:5` and `:21` retain compatibility re-exports after extraction. PASM explicitly requires these facades. Their small implementations do not imply low leverage: deleting them propagates path changes across host code and integration tests.
- `src/server_app/mod.rs:11` is not a pass-through: it adds state declarations, debug routing, test visibility, optional presentation and ordered readback adapters. Keep that host-owned joining role outside independently buildable simulation siblings.
- `src/native_host/world_load.rs:185` already calls `world::materialization::register(app, RuntimeWorldLoad)`. The old concern that native runtime loading hand-maintains a second World spawn inventory is not supported by this revision. Runtime selection, preparation and mint parking still have real native lifecycle responsibilities.
- Root Cargo target/feature selection and `build.rs` were inspected. Native-only binary gates, explicit Ultralight opt-in, resolver v2, and separate build-time DLL staging versus runtime SDK resource staging have stated operational purposes. Their mere existence is not an architecture finding. This audit did not validate cold-build SDK discovery or package distribution behavior.

#### Coverage limitations

This is not an exhaustive audit of every module under `src/`. Native audio/media, pane threading/upload/recovery, display/layout law, Workshop authoring and test-process internals, native GM lifecycle, transport socket implementations, capture/performance binaries, save catalogue behavior and headless report/replay internals were inventoried or encountered but not traced in sufficient depth for findings. Browser bridge functionality outside the sampled fleet staging and composition paths was not exhaustively reviewed. Cross-package implementation was followed only where needed to understand composition ownership; sibling-domain architecture is outside this report. Hardware behavior, WASM execution and test success are unverified in this read-only pass.

#### 1. Delete the second implementation of the checkpoint preflight wire shape

**Files and observed callers.** `src/server/bridge.rs:2525` implements `candidate_preflight_js`, manually constructing the JavaScript object for every `CandidateBlock` variant. Its caller is the `preflight` field in `save_slot_js` at `src/server/bridge.rs:2509`; catalogue enumeration calls that row conversion at `src/server/bridge.rs:2365`. This is an active browser interface, not dead code.

The existing model already supplies precisely this serialization: `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_checkpoint.rs:247` derives `Serialize` for `CandidateBlock` and declares `#[serde(tag = "kind", rename_all = "kebab-case")]`; `gm_checkpoint.rs:287` derives it for `CandidatePreflight { eligible, blocks }`. The native GM uses that model directly as its `SaveRow.preflight` at `src/native_host/native_gm/saves.rs:15` and serializes its reply through `core::codec::to_json` at `saves.rs:130`. Comparing the definitions with the browser match shows the same variant names, fields, optional hull strings, numeric `u32` slots and station arrays.

**Opportunity.** Replace the browser's variant-by-variant object builder with serialization through the existing `crate::core::codec::to_json` followed by `js_sys::JSON::parse`. An existing host implementation of exactly this boundary conversion is `ship_entry_to_js`, `src/server/bridge.rs:3788`, and the scenario catalogue also uses JSON parsing at `bridge.rs:4185`. Keep the named preflight adapter if it aids readability, but it should no longer enumerate variants or field names.

This is genuine deletion: one independently maintained serialization match disappears, and the surviving serialization is already used in production. It does not move the 70-plus lines to a utility or replace them with a macro hiding the same field inventory. The current function occupies approximately 72 lines; an adapter similar to the existing ship conversion needs approximately 8–12. **Rough production-source saving: 60 lines**, before any additional boundary regression test. Keep `save_js_field`, which has many other callers.

**Behavior to preserve.** Preserve kebab-case `kind` values; explicit `null` for absent `candidate`/`live` hulls; numeric slots; ordering of blocks and stations; and the outer catalogue's `preflight: null` when live seating is unavailable. There is no `u64` precision concern in this particular object: slots are `u32`, and the rest is strings, booleans, optional strings and vectors. Do not broaden this into blanket serialization of the whole save row: that row deliberately emits seed and capture tick as decimal strings and the content digest as hexadecimal, and native save rows are a different projection.

The comment at `bridge.rs:2521` says save metadata avoids serialization because `serde_json` is confined to the codec. That constraint remains valid, but calling the existing codec and parsing its result does not import `serde_json` into the bridge. This is not a proposal to change the RON save format or the checkpoint compatibility decision.

**Verification and tradeoffs.** Model tests in `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_checkpoint_tests.rs:78`, `:110`, `:130`, `:151` and `:173` cover eligibility and concrete refusal data. `tests/client/gm-checkpoint-panel.test.js:46` and `:236` exercise the published preflight shape and rendering. Native save tests (`src/native_host/native_gm/saves_tests.rs:5`, `:35`) cover request refusal and receipts, not equivalence of every serialized variant. Add a boundary fixture covering every variant, especially optional hull nulls and non-ASCII strings, and check the actual browser return shape. Existing JS fixtures alone would not catch a Rust marshalling mismatch.

The tradeoff is an intermediate JSON string and parse in a catalogue/UI path in exchange for deleting its duplicated schema. No profiling was performed; retain manual marshalling if evidence establishes this path as hot. Error handling must be explicit and should follow the established object-boundary convention without presenting a failed conversion as an eligible save. **Confidence: high** in the duplicate shape and deletion opportunity; implementation validation is still required.

Native additionally waits for `WorldConfig`, supporting deferred lobby selection. Both are intentionally scheduled on the fixed path; native/headless digest equivalence and native lobby tests protect this behavior. - **HTML stripping is similar but not equivalent.** `src/native_host/native_gm/document.rs:11` removes every script from a built page before installing the native adapter. `src/native_host/panes/document.rs:497` already has a flat-element stripper. Native GM document tests at `document_tests.rs:4` cover a valid real page, not this malformed-input distinction. Meanwhile `src/native_host/host_lobby/document.rs` uses depth-counted extraction for nested div/section elements; that cannot be replaced by the flat-element helper. Consolidation is a hypothesis requiring an explicit parsing contract, not a proven deletion.
- **Build-time and runtime SDK staging cover different moments.** `build.rs:62` stages Windows SDK DLLs before the executable can start; its comments and stamp retry logic describe why a runtime staging call cannot bootstrap a missing import library dependency. Removing it in favor of runtime staging would trade source lines for a broken cold launch. The demo-build include likewise has compile-time consumers (`src/server_app/mod.rs:30` and `src/server/bridge.rs` cfg gates); it is not obsolete merely because gameplay moved crates.
- **Tests and target adapters are not expendable line count.** Root composition tests check integration across owners; independent sibling-crate tests establish independence. Areas inspected: package manifest and build script; crate-root composition and selected compatibility facades; `server_app` registration, registration options, registrar dispatch and physics registration; `ship_plugin` scheduling; `sim_sets/order` and its custom schedule build pass; delivery's HTTP/WebSocket upgrade boundary; root host feature forwarding and native direct-join implementation; determinism and schedule-pass test callers. #### 1. Remove the unused WebSocket implementation dependency from the simulation host feature

**Observed implementation and callers.** `crates/phoenix-simulation/Cargo.toml:14` makes its `host` feature activate `dep:tungstenite`; the dependency at `crates/phoenix-simulation/Cargo.toml:61` enables both handshake and rustls/WebPKI support. Searching the complete simulation crate for `tungstenite` finds the manifest and explanatory comments, but no implementation reference. The package's actual native HTTP server uses `httparse` and hands an upgraded stream to the abstract `ConnectionUpgrade` boundary (`crates/phoenix-simulation/src/delivery/serve.rs:613`, trait at line 625). Its own documentation explicitly says that the serving module must not name the WebSocket implementation.

The real concrete caller is `impl ConnectionUpgrade for DirectJoinGate` in `src/native_host/direct_join.rs:1166`; it constructs the handshake response at line 1207 and the WebSocket at line 1255. Root `Cargo.toml:219` activates its own `dep:tungstenite`, declared independently at line 529, alongside forwarding `phoenix-simulation/host`. Consequently, the actual host does not need simulation to activate this otherwise unused direct dependency.

**Why this is unnecessary machinery.** The package boundary already does the useful work: simulation serves and validates HTTP, while the host supplies the socket protocol. The extra dependency undermines the clarity of that boundary and enables a native networking/TLS dependency graph for a standalone `phoenix-simulation --features host` build without providing any corresponding simulation behavior. This is a concrete configuration and dependency-maintenance cost, not a claim about measured runtime overhead or a claim that the integrated host currently compiles two copies. Cargo can unify the integrated host's matching dependency.

**Deletion test and change opportunity.** Remove `dep:tungstenite` from this package's `host` feature and remove this package's target-specific dependency declaration. Retain `host = ["server"]` if preserving the existing feature spelling is desired. Keep the root host's dependency and the `ConnectionUpgrade` interface. No replacement abstraction, protocol implementation or caller migration is needed for this bounded change.

**Behavior and invariants to preserve.** Native direct joining must still own the socket after the HTTP handoff, receive prefetched bytes, reject malformed upgrades before handing over, and preserve the existing admission path. Delivery-only operation must remain available without the concrete socket implementation. Native/WASM simulation behavior, schedule order, UUID/RNG order and phone protocol must remain unchanged.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/delivery/serve_tests.rs:185` discusses rejecting invalid upgrades before the concrete WebSocket handler; `src/native_host/direct_join_tests.rs:9` identifies the real WebSocket integration harness. These remain useful behavior coverage and should not be deleted. A follow-up change needs the standalone simulation dependency-tree check with `host` enabled and the real native host build/direct-join tests. Workspace-only checks can conceal accidental reliance on feature unification. This audit performed source inspection only and does not claim those checks passed.

**Tradeoff, confidence and limitations.** High confidence that the direct dependency is unused in the inspected package: the crate-wide reference search found no executable use, and the concrete implementation is in the host. Build-time savings are not quantified and may be negligible in an already-built full workspace. The value is a truthful package feature and a smaller independently selected dependency graph. This recommendation does not require removing the compatibility feature name.

#### 2. Scheduling machinery passes the deletion test: retain it

**Observed implementation and callers.** `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/sim_sets/order.rs:10` records fixed-step owner sequences and installs `DeclaredOrder` after Bevy's existing automatic deferred pass near the end of the function. `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/sim_sets/order/pass.rs:17` models endpoints with singleton/optional constraints; its `members` function at line 59 resolves actual nested set membership, and its `ScheduleBuildPass` implementation at line 99 adds execution edges to the flattened graph. This looks like a substantial bespoke alternative to ordinary `.before`/`.after` declarations, but it has a specific semantic purpose.

`pasm/spec/architecture/deterministic-simulation.yaml:442` explicitly distinguishes execution order from deferred publication: adding ordinary edges before automatic flush insertion can suppress an existing flush or move publication. The neighboring rationale at lines 438–443 grounds the contract in an observed Captain/Command scheduling regression and hidden writes through deferred Commands. Replacing the pass with a conventional chain is therefore not a behavior-preserving simplification.

The implementation has focused tests: `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/sim_sets/order/pass_tests.rs:25` checks preservation of an existing publication boundary; line 47 checks that ordering does not create a new one; line 70 checks contradictory ordering remains an ordinary schedule error. These test externally meaningful behavior rather than just reproducing the algorithm. The optional headless owner is also explicitly permitted by the design, not evidence of a speculative generic scheduler.

**Lens judgment and change opportunity.** No deletion is recommended. The custom pass adds maintenance coupling to Bevy's schedule graph, but removes ambiguity without changing the saved publication contract. Any future simplification would first need evidence that a Bevy-supported mechanism preserves both existing and absent publication boundaries, as well as the integrated schedule proof. No such replacement was established in this audit. Preserve singleton assertions, required-owner checks, cycle detection, original registration slots and deferred frontiers; weakening them for fewer lines would remove safeguards rather than remove overengineering.

**Confidence and limitations.** High confidence in the current justification from implementation, narrow tests and explicit design. This is not a claim that every individual sequence edge was independently proven necessary; the complete graph and all behavioral goldens were not re-derived or executed here.

#### 3. Registration perturbation controls are active verification seams, not unused runtime configurability

**Observed implementation and callers.** `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/server_app/registration.rs:27` defines `SimPluginOptions`. Its `physics_last`, `registration_order` and `extra_registration_probes` fields feed the registration procedure; `register_sim_set_plugins` at line 157 assembles the fixed registrar list, optionally appends probe registrars, and shuffles it under a seeded test setting. These controls expose more configuration than a running mission requires and put a small amount of test-enabling machinery into the library.

However, real callers exercise all three. `tests/common/mod.rs:48` exposes the physics-order perturbation and line 54 the registration-order setting through the fixture; `tests/registration_order_determinism.rs:164` compares shuffled registration behavior, and lines 297–307 install probe registrars in opposite orders. `tests/authoritative_declaration_order_determinism.rs:85` also consumes the seam. The physics-first/last comparison is used at `tests/headless_runner.rs:6313`. Deleting these controls would remove current mutation and determinism verification, not just an unused generalization. The registrar allocation occurs during app assembly; no per-tick dispatch overhead was identified here.

**Lens judgment and change opportunity.** Retain the perturbation mechanisms and independent tests. Restricting them behind a test-support feature could be explored only if production dependency/size evidence makes it worthwhile, while ensuring integration tests still exercise the exact production registration path. That is a hypothesis, not a demonstrated present improvement: adding more feature gates and alternative construction paths may cost more than these small controls save. No new test-only registration implementation is warranted.

**Behavior and invariants.** Canonical registration, fixed phase membership, deterministic seeded permutations and the ability to show that probes detect an unordered mutation must survive. The native/WASM numerical agreement and registration/deferred-flush invariants remain stronger requirements than reducing the public options count.

**Confidence and limitations.** High confidence that the controls have current callers. The full long-running perturbation suite was not executed or audited assertion by assertion, so this report makes no independent claim that it proves every order-sensitive system.

#### 1. Reuse the existing whole-fixed-step-debt helper across simulation pause and restore paths

**Evidence and observed callers.** `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/mod.rs:1719` already defines `discard_whole_fixed_overstep`. It calculates the remainder of `Time<Fixed>::overstep()` modulo the timestep with integer nanosecond arithmetic, constructs an exact `Duration`, and discards only whole-step debt. The helper is used at lines 1616, 1691 and 1716, including the GM-boundary hold. The boundary system is registered at `lockstep/mod.rs:502`.

The same arithmetic is independently maintained in:

- `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_action.rs:4475`: after applying due GM actions, a paused simulation discards accumulated unbegun fixed steps. The preceding reducer also consumes the virtual-time delta.
- `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_join.rs:1071`: `set_join_pause` engages the technical join hold, pauses virtual time, then drops the same whole fixed-step debt.
- `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/entities/model_markers.rs:342`: `discard_blocked_model_rig_overstep` performs exactly this operation when model readiness blocks another simulation step.
- `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/snapshot.rs:5361`: restoring a paused snapshot repeats it after replacing bootstrap overstep with the stored duration.
- External composition caller `src/headless/replay.rs:461`: reconstructing a paused GM replay frontier repeats the operation again.

These are identical mechanisms with different reasons for invoking them. The existing helper already demonstrates that the arithmetic can be separated from its policy. This is not a recommendation to unify GM, model-readiness and lockstep state machines.

**Why it matters.** The duplicated mechanism protects an important determinism boundary: a pause may occur after a frame has accumulated several fixed steps. Removing all debt would lose the interpolation fraction, while merely pausing virtual time permits already accumulated steps to leak past the hold. Maintaining the same subtle arithmetic in six locations increases the chance that a future correction reaches some holds but not others. No present disagreement in the inspected arithmetic was found; this is a concrete maintenance opportunity, not a diagnosed runtime defect.

**Concrete change opportunity.** Expose or relocate the existing helper into an appropriate simulation clock utility and call it from the equivalent paths. Keep it Bevy-aware and renderer-free. There is no reason to put this into the Bevy-free reusable runtime package or to introduce a configurable clock-policy framework. The root headless caller would need an accessible simulation export if included; consolidating the five simulation implementations is useful independently of that follow-up.

**Behavior that must survive.** Preserve exact integer `Duration` arithmetic, fractional overstep, optional-resource behavior, and all original invocation points and predicates. In particular, retain the snapshot restore operation that first *replaces* bootstrap debt. Do not move virtual-time pause/unpause, join-hold engagement, cadence rederivation or schedule registration into the helper. These callers must retain their distinct policy and order.

There are superficially similar operations that must **not** adopt the helper: `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/workshop/test_clock.rs:138` and `:168` deliberately discard the entire accumulator to make a requested Step exactly one authored tick; snapshot replacement at `snapshot.rs:5358` also deliberately clears the whole bootstrap accumulator. Those are compatibility differences, not missed reuse.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/tests.rs:601` (`a_multi_participant_frame_commits_one_step_before_egress`) drives a frame with five-and-a-half periods and asserts exactly one committed step plus the retained half-period. The same file at `:675` covers a solo GM pause boundary and at `:802` covers consuming the current frame delta when applying Pause. `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/entities/model_markers_tests.rs:399` and `:425` distinguish an unblocked three-step frame from a blocked one-step frame and assert the same fractional remainder. Preserve these adapter-level tests even if the arithmetic has one owner. A focused helper test should additionally cover zero debt, debt less than a timestep, an exact multiple, and a nontrivial nanosecond remainder. The inspected snapshot tests include paused-frontier round-trip coverage (`snapshot_tests.rs:396`), but this audit did not establish coverage for malformed paused snapshots carrying several whole steps of debt; add or locate that before consolidating the restore call.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limits.** High confidence in mechanism equivalence: the implementations and the already-existing helper were compared directly. Savings are modest; the principal value is one owner for a subtle operation. An exported helper adds a small public surface for the headless caller, so a crate-local consolidation is also reasonable. This audit does not claim that all clock holds can or should share policy.

#### 2. Build the runtime entity snapshot once for initial seeding and later spawn publication

**Evidence and observed callers.** `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/server_app/broadcast_publish.rs:799` defines `reconcile_runtime_entities`, installed in the existing reconciliation slot by `server_app/registration.rs:1162`. Within that one system, the unseeded branch constructs an `EntitySnapshot` at `broadcast_publish.rs:893`, and the newly-seen branch constructs the same projection at `:1019`.

Both branches independently:

- Compute hull and shield fractions with the same zero-maximum fallback rules (`:876` and `:1002`).
- Copy identity, name, transform, tags and live infrastructure state.
- Map Sphere, Box and Torus geometry, including the X/Z box radius and torus inner radius (`:912` and `:1038`).
- Apply the asteroid-field torus fallback only when a shape has not already been supplied.
- Copy optional radar colour, region colour, size and icon, guarding short colour arrays (`:941` and `:1067`).
- Derive the active Objective marker and copy target tags, threat level and description.

The initial branch upserts the snapshot into `WorldResource` at `:965`, marks the registry seeded, and returns. The runtime branch upserts the same value and additionally emits a reliable `EntitySpawned` to all recipients at `:1091`. A local `shape_to_wire` helper at `:845` already shares the smallest piece while leaving the much larger projection repeated.

**Why it matters.** Both snapshots describe the same live entity to the same client model. Duplicated construction means every new projected field or fallback rule must be changed twice or clients joining through Welcome can see a different representation from clients watching a runtime spawn. The current blocks inspected agree; this report does not claim that they already diverge.

**Concrete change opportunity.** Factor the existing component-to-`EntitySnapshot` construction into a local projection helper used by both branches. Keep the seed and incremental traversal around it. It can remain private to publication; neither an independent crate nor a generic serialization abstraction is necessary. This removes one maintained copy of roughly a hundred lines of projection logic, rather than merely moving both copies elsewhere.

**Behavior that must survive.** Initial seeding must populate reconnect world data without emitting duplicate spawn messages. Later spawns must still emit reliably to all, and only newly seen UUIDs must retain the existing sorted publication order (`:983`). Preserve the existing query filter excluding Asteroids, registry insertion timing, shape precedence, health defaults, infrastructure read-back from live state, and Objective lookup by authored entity ID. Leave the removal pass and delta-cache pruning at `:1097` onward alone. Do not combine this projection with save-snapshot capture: save continuation and client-facing entity descriptions have different contracts.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/server_app/mod_tests.rs:6009` checks the initial seeded latch; `:6021` checks runtime spawn emission; `:6044` verifies position and authored ID; `:6070` covers despawn; `:6102` proves Asteroids do not use `EntitySpawned`. The tests at `:6294` and `:6317` cover all-recipient lifecycle delivery. These establish real callers and important lifecycle differences, but the inspected cases do not compare complete initial and incremental payloads for the same component set. Add that parity case with region geometry, radar appearance, health, infrastructure and target metadata. Keep a separate assertion that seed produces no `EntitySpawned`; equal projection does not imply equal emission policy.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limits.** High confidence: this duplication is inside one live system using the same query and destination type. Passing a large collection of optional components to a helper can become awkward; a local typed query item or narrowly scoped closure may be preferable to a new wide public API. Choose the smallest local extraction that preserves borrowing and lifecycle control. No change to package boundaries or schedule ordering is required.

#### Investigated similarities that are justified

- `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/ship/helm_ai/impulse.rs:14` repeats the Gameplay operator's ECS signature, but its body at `:40` delegates to `phoenix_sim_gameplay::ship::helm_ai::impulse::ai_helm_impulse`, injecting the live parent `AiHostEnv`. This is an adapter across independent domains, not a second impulse algorithm. Removing it by making Gameplay import World or Session would violate the declared architecture.
- `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lobby/handler.rs:35` forwards Welcome construction to Session while supplying active-pack string catalogues from the parent's content context. The shared implementation is already reused; the different signature is meaningful.
- `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/native_file.rs:2`, `lockstep/transfer.rs:2`, and `entities/include_resolve.rs:4` reuse Platform, Runtime and Content through compatibility exports. The filesystem/overlay adapter beside the include-resolver export adds Phoenix-specific resolution precedence. These are not copied implementations to delete.
- The two Coordination capture blocks in `snapshot.rs:3023` and `:4038` share sender metadata mapping but preserve different state: the unread enqueue suffix depends on a cloned authoritative cursor and source identity, whereas pending queues store relative due ticks. Their whole capture routines should not be merged merely because the field lists resemble each other. A small shared origin-tag mapping may be possible, but was not promoted over the substantially clearer findings above.

#### Coverage limits

This is not an exhaustive semantic audit of the substantial parent crate. Repeated-source scans are candidate discovery, not proof that unmatched code has no duplication. The complete GM action families, all command admission paths, weapon/coupling systems, Workshop authoring flows, complete world script application, the full snapshot schema/digest traversal, and native/browser storage implementations were not traced end to end. Materialization and selected GM/Workshop code were sampled; no removal is recommended from those samples. Cross-package files were read only to establish concrete existing implementations and distinguish facades from duplicated behavior. Independent branch test suites and deliberate native/JavaScript codec counterparts were not treated as duplication.


Audit 7/72 · Agent: `/root/audit07_simulation_architecture` · Lens: architecture

### Architecture

Module: Simulation composition (`crates/phoenix-simulation/`). Code baseline: `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e`; navigation documents subsequently advanced in the documented, docs-only `2de7d532` commit. All source inspection used `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, not the older output checkout.

Scope and inspection: crate manifest and public module directory; simulation registration and host wrapper; World plugin registration and shared materialization; entity spawning; Ship plugin registration; AI Host Spine environment and its consumers in Weapons, Navigation, Comms and Sensors; Ship continuation declarations; startup-restore entry points and selected tests. External callers inspected were the root browser, native and headless composition adapters and native deferred World loading. Read `AGENTS.md`, `wiki/SCHEMA.md`, `CONTEXT.md`, `wiki/index.md`, the reusable-layers guide, architecture skill and vocabulary, and the reusable-layers and deterministic-simulation PASM slices. No nested `AGENTS.md` was found; `docs/adr/` is absent. PASM therefore supplies the relevant recorded architecture decisions.

This is a focused composition audit, not exhaustive coverage of the 602 source files listed beneath the crate. No builds, tests, network research or source modifications were performed.

#### 1. Deepen the AI mission-context interface around target-name resolution

**Files.** `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/ai/host.rs:53`, `:111`, `:122`; `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/console/weapons/server.rs:1710`, `:2385`; `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/console/weapons/beam.rs:226`; `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/console/navigation/server.rs:935`, `:1155`; `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/console/comms/server.rs:1833`, `:2700`; `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/ship/sensors.rs:1271`, `:1377`.

**Problem and observed callers.** `AiHostEnv` already gives the Host Spine useful depth: it privately borrows the live World runtime, layer map and Session state, and resolves layered flags through `flag_chain` and `flag_chain_from`. Its target-name escape hatch is much wider: `content_runtime()` returns the entire `WorldContentRuntime`. Consumers then understand that target names live specifically in `name_to_uuid`, and either access that field directly or forward the whole runtime to another resolver.

Weapons' `resolve_objective_target_uuid` reads the authoritative map first, then searches live entities for matching UUID or `EntityName`. Its ordinary policy caller and beam caller share that helper. Sensors reimplements that lookup twice, for Scan directives and Destroy-objective candidates. Navigation passes the same whole-runtime reference into its own resolver, but also needs a live position. Comms passes it into a resolver whose fallback deliberately uses known Comms contacts and finally syntactically valid UUID strings. These are real, existing consumers, not a hypothetical reason to introduce a seam.

The consequence is more than repeated map syntax: the interface makes several System hosts responsible for interpreting a shared World naming policy. Navigation's implementation at `console/navigation/server.rs:1160` illustrates the maintenance risk. It calculates `mapped`, then uses one `find` predicate accepting the mapped UUID **or** a directly matching UUID/name. Weapons and Sensors instead return the map hit before trying fallback. Consequently, if a different live entity with a matching display name precedes the mapped entity, Navigation's predicate can choose that other entity while Weapons selects the mapped one. This code-level difference is confirmed. Whether valid authored/dynamic content can produce that collision, and whether Navigation intentionally permits it, is an **unverified hypothesis**, not a reported runtime defect.

**Solution opportunity.** Deepen the existing mission-context module so the shared authoritative name lookup and its documented precedence live behind its interface, without returning the full World runtime merely to read one index. Keep the differing live-entity, position and Comms-contact policies explicit in their owning adapters. Before consolidating precedence, establish whether the Navigation difference is intentional and preserve it or correct it under a separate behavior decision. This proposal does not prescribe a replacement interface or generic resolver framework.

Keep the implementation in the Simulation parent where live cross-domain context belongs. Do not move World access into Gameplay, make siblings import each other, introduce a copied name cache, or make Contracts own live scenario state. A borrowed read is sufficient and consistent with the PASM architecture.

**Benefits and deletion test.** The existing `AiHostEnv` earns its keep: deleting it would redistribute layered flag traversal and registration knowledge among numerous AI hosts. Retain it. The opportunity is to increase its depth by replacing raw-store knowledge in callers with the behavior they actually need. This improves locality when naming rules change and gives leverage across four System families while retaining their genuinely different fallback rules. Merely moving all current helpers into one file without reducing caller knowledge would not achieve that benefit.

**Behavior that must survive.** Preserve exact live-context reads; the map remains the current World runtime's map, not a delayed projection. Preserve Control Source gating, fixed AI cadence and ordinary Admission. A resolved name must not bypass the System's visibility, range or capability gates. Navigation needs a live position, whereas a map hit in the Weapons helper currently returns an identity without requiring that helper's live query to contain it. Comms supports contact-name resolution and syntactically valid UUID fallback; these differences prevent a blanket mechanical merger. Preserve query candidate ordering unless a separately verified requirement changes it. Keep layer-qualified flag semantics separate from the base name table: the environment explicitly documents that they are different concepts.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** `ai/host_tests.rs:264` checks that an unregistered environment fails loudly; earlier tests exercise Control Source and policy behavior. Consumer-level coverage already exists: `console/comms/server_tests.rs:284`, `:350`, `:430`, `:461` exercise directive-driven hail, human operation, range and unresolved names; `ship/sensors_tests.rs:1093`, `:1118`, `:1261` exercise named Destroy targets, range and a missing live target; the Weapons test suite repeatedly constructs `name_to_uuid` fixtures (for example `console/weapons/server_tests.rs:7186` and `:7278`). Those tests should remain independent consumer evidence. The inspected tests did not establish a shared precedence contract covering a map/display-name collision, a stale map entry, direct identity lookup, and System-specific fallback differences. Verification should exercise both the mission-context interface and resulting admitted commands or Navigation candidates, rather than testing only an extracted map helper. No test result is claimed from this read-only audit.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limitations.** Confidence is high that callers currently know more World storage detail than their target-resolution task needs; the improvement is modest and suitable for a focused change. Confidence is medium that a common behavioral seam will simplify all four families, because their fallback rules differ and should not be flattened. The Navigation discrepancy merits a characterization test before any production change. This finding does not claim the crate split itself is defective or that every direct World runtime read should be hidden.

#### Inspected structures that already justify their complexity

- **Shared World materialization is already a deep module.** `world/materialization.rs:17` registers the same ordered pass into a supplied schedule. `world/server.rs:876` uses it for Startup, and `src/native_host/world_load.rs:185` uses it for deferred load. It retains function-identity ordering and chained deferred-command flushes rather than hiding them in an exclusive runner. `tests/native_host_lobby/materialization.rs:97`, `:230`, `:260` cover materialization after different lobby delays, mint restoration, and identical members in both schedules. Deleting this seam would spread the ordering and UUID-assignment knowledge back across callers. Do not propose another materialization consolidation based on older AGENTS commentary that describes the historical duplicated schedule.

- **Registration controls are a real verification interface.** `server_app/registration.rs:28` exposes physics placement, shuffled registrar order and optional probe registrations. These are not speculative production configurability: `tests/registration_order_determinism.rs:164` exercises shuffled composition, `:284` checks an intentionally order-sensitive pair, and `:508` verifies schedule membership. Their implementation preserves the normal inventory while permitting tests to attack hidden order dependencies. Removing them would reduce the ability to verify determinism through the composition interface.

- **Simulation/Presentation host composition is deliberate.** The parent `server_app/registration.rs:22` explains that `render` is retained for compatibility but not consumed by simulation. `src/server_app/mod.rs:11` delegates simulation installation and `:51` selects presentation. The duplication in entry-point names is a compatibility facade with real host work behind it, not sufficient evidence of a pass-through worth deleting. Renderer-free independent simulation and pure-JS phone clients remain constraints.

- **Cross-domain adapters belong in this crate.** `world/server.rs:814` composes Comms, infrastructure and capability plugins with World state; `ship_plugin.rs:51` joins helm dispatch, GM operations and simulation ordering. PASM explicitly assigns live integration to the parent while Gameplay, World and Session remain independent siblings. File size alone is not evidence for moving these adapters into a sibling or creating imports between siblings.

- **Spawner sections are private implementation seams.** `entities/spawner.rs:33` is the caller-facing operation; the private section ladder at `:81` and stable list at `:86` concentrate insertion order behind it. Ship capability installation follows the sections at `:67`. Replacing the ladder with another spelling would not by itself deepen the caller interface. Preserve component insertion order and test independence rather than treating the number of private structs as an architecture finding.

- **Continuation already separates projection from orchestration.** `ship/continuation.rs:1` explains the owner-local saved frontier and why snapshot orchestration retains identity matching, prerequisite checks and cross-System restore order. Its explicit payloads preserve save layout and distinguish desired controls, last-applied controls and target memory. The deterministic-simulation PASM slice records this as intentional. This inspection did not establish a reason to replace it with runtime-type serialization or a generic save registry.

#### Unexplored areas and evidence limits

The following substantial areas were not reviewed in depth: complete snapshot capture/restore and digest folds; lockstep/recovery state machines; complete command admission and GM action pipelines; all broadcaster projections; composed content validation; Workshop internals; all World script effects and layer mutation paths; all entity ship-seed installation; capability-specific runtime systems; delivery and persistence backends. The continuation and restore inspection was limited to public shape and relevant design records, not a continuation correctness audit. Some very large orientation files were read in excerpts; conclusions above rest on the specific implementation and caller locations listed. No claim is made that unexamined modules have no deepening opportunities.

### Reducing maintained lines

Module: Simulation composition. Scope: `crates/phoenix-simulation/` in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, source baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e` (the subsequent HEAD contains the documented navigation-only changes).

Inspected areas: package manifest and crate surface; composition registration and materialization entry points; Gameplay re-export/test attachment examples; helm adapter shape; codec entry points and their debug/headless callers; Live Inspector descriptor construction and World/Ship traversal; samples of GM projection publishers, faction state and health; snapshot function inventory; fixture access.
