No nested `AGENTS.md` was found; `docs/adr` does not exist in this worktree. This is a read-only source audit: no builds or tests were run.

1. **Consolidate the six Live Inspector descriptor defaults; retain the domain-specific field constructors.**

   **Observed code and callers.** `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/inspector.rs:22` already owns `FieldDescriptor`, including kind, default source, mutability, origin and validation. The same baseline value is constructed in:

   - `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_entity_inspector.rs:94`;
   - `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_ship_inspector.rs:86`;
   - `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_world_inspector.rs:52`;
   - `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_region_inspector.rs:93`;
   - `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_presentation_inspector.rs:60`;
   - `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_npc/inspector.rs:43`.

   All six set the supplied kind and schema path, supplied mutability, `default_source: None`, and `document`, `line` and `layer` to `None`. Entity, Ship, World and NPC begin with empty validation. Region and Presentation attach their distinct recreation explanations. NPC subsequently attaches its own named-action validation in `gm_npc/inspector.rs:69`.

   These are live paths, not abandoned constructors: `gm_projection.rs:726`, `:789`, `:857`, `:1303` and `:1381` build the domain projections; `gm_npc/inspector.rs:69`, `:77` and `:82` construct the NPC descriptors. The local field constructors also supply different IDs, labels, groups and optional action links. Those differences belong at the call sites.

   **Concrete reduction.** Put the common runtime descriptor construction beside the type in `inspector.rs` and replace the repeated baseline struct literals with calls. Preserve each adapter's field wrapper and append Region, Presentation and NPC validation where it is currently chosen. The existing NPC `descriptor` function demonstrates essentially the entire common operation; reuse its behavior without making the other domains depend on the NPC adapter. If that public path needs compatibility, a re-export can preserve it.

   **Why it matters for maintained lines.** Five additional copies each repeat roughly a dozen initialization lines for a shared value. Replacing those copies with calls, retaining the special validation branches, and retaining one implementation should remove approximately **40–55 production lines net**, depending on call formatting and any compatibility re-export. This is removal of repeated construction logic, not moving the same volume into another file. It also means adding a baseline descriptor property has one initialization owner.

   **Invariants and limits.** Metadata must remain descriptive, never grant mutation authority. Unknown source locations must remain absent rather than acquiring guessed provenance. Exact schema paths, labels, field ordering, action links and validation String Ids must survive. Do not fold `workshop/document.rs:171` into a runtime-only constructor without preserving its real source-span-derived line number and source-document validation; it has evidence that the runtime inspectors deliberately lack. Nor should this change replace explicit Region/Presentation field inventories with generic serialization: their coverage ratchets are separate contracts.

   **Existing verification and missing checks.** `gm_entity_inspector_tests.rs:39` checks schema-path identity and `:49` checks absent source location. `gm_region_inspector_tests.rs:38` and `gm_presentation_inspector_tests.rs:31` pin their different recreation explanations. `gm_ship_inspector_tests.rs:8` exercises classification without generic mutation; `gm_world_inspector_tests.rs:3` checks derived Flags and named-action links. Retain all these tests. A focused equality check of complete descriptors for the common baseline, Region/Presentation recreation cases and NPC named action would catch accidentally defaulted mutability, lost validation or fabricated provenance; existing sampled assertions do not pin every field of every constructor.

   **Tradeoff, confidence and limitations.** High confidence in the repetition and modest savings. One short constructor is warranted; a builder hierarchy, descriptor registry or macro framework would likely spend the savings. This finding does not assert that the inspector projections themselves should be combined.

2. **A smaller, conditional reduction exists in World's two TOML leaf walks, but not in merging World and Ship inspectors.**

   **Observed code and callers.** `gm_world_inspector.rs:68` recursively traverses TOML tables to produce readings. `gm_world_inspector.rs:105` repeats the table recursion and path construction to produce descriptors. Both treat arrays as leaves and trim a trailing dot from the same path. `definition` at `:151` invokes the reading walk; `projection` at `:234` invokes the descriptor walk. Root and active supporting-layer readings are built at `:534` and `:549` respectively.

   **Concrete opportunity.** A small private leaf visitor in this module could own table recursion and path formation, while reading formatting and descriptor production remain its two consumers. This eliminates the second copy of that traversal rule. It should not introduce a cross-domain serialization abstraction. The duplicated recursion is only several lines: after adding the visitor and closures, **net savings are not established and may be negligible**. Treat this as a conditional maintenance simplification, not a committed LOC win. If an implementation is not shorter and clearer, retain the current two functions.

   **Behavior to preserve.** World arrays are one comma-separated reading (`:75`), not recursively expanded. Scalar TOML rendering strips surrounding quote characters (`:88`). Descriptor production happens from the root global config; supporting layers supply separate readings. Absent global Options receive explicit discoverability fields at `:236`; a visitor must not remove those. World field ordering and duplicate handling must remain unchanged.

   Ship's superficially similar walker is intentionally different: `gm_ship_inspector.rs:102` handles JSON null as `not-authored`, `:119` keys array elements by `id`, `system_id`, `name` or index, and `:132` recursively expands arrays and objects. Combining those walkers merely because both recurse would require policy machinery and could increase maintained lines. That broader consolidation is not recommended.

   **Tests, tradeoffs and confidence.** `gm_world_inspector_tests.rs:3` covers global discoverability and mutation classification; `:31` covers supporting-layer provenance and float rendering (`120.0`). A traversal refactor needs explicit nested-table, empty-array, nonempty-array and quoted-string output checks, plus field-order comparison. None were run for this audit. Confidence is high in the shared traversal mechanics, low that extracting this tiny overlap is worthwhile on LOC alone.

3. **Several apparent deletion targets are justified and should not be counted as savings.**

   **Compatibility and independently owned mechanics.** `entities/config.rs:1` re-exports Gameplay's parser and attaches the composition-level tests at `:3`. `entities/mod.rs:16` still compiles the authored AI behavioral pins. The large `entities/config_tests.rs` file therefore is not an orphan just because the implementation moved. Likewise, `ship/helm_ai/mod.rs:503` re-exports mechanics while its adapter around `:384` supplies live composed inputs. Removing those paths or merging siblings would violate the reusable-layer contract rather than reduce an unnecessary implementation. This audit did not prove any large test file redundant.

   **Codec fallback wrappers.** `core/codec.rs:202`, `:231`, `:244`, `:255`, `:263`, `:271`, `:279` and `:293` all serialize a typed debug payload and return an empty string on failure. Actual callers include `debug/station_activity.rs:338`, `debug/ai_state.rs:352`, `debug/scenario.rs:493` and root `src/headless/report.rs:411`. Ordinary fallible serialization already has the generic `to_json` seam at `core/codec.rs:4`. The named debug functions express an intentional fallback contract, as the codec guide records. Replacing them with caller-side `unwrap_or_default()` just relocates that policy; replacing each body with another helper saves no meaningful production code. Keep the typed wire-shape tests, including `core/codec_tests.rs:3631` through `:3912`. No deletion recommendation or savings claim follows from these matching bodies.

   **Materialization schedule.** `world/materialization.rs:17` already registers one ordered pass for the caller-supplied schedule. Its chain preserves script compilation before spawning and anonymous-before-named minting (`:26`). This is existing reuse, not a second schedule to delete. Any further attempt to shorten registration must preserve system identities, deferred boundaries and mint order; apparent registration verbosity is not enough evidence.

Unexplored areas: this is a large composition crate, and the audit is not exhaustive. Most command admission reducers, the full GM canonical action/undo pipeline, full capture/restore bodies, lockstep recovery, all console systems, world dispatch/script application, authored configuration validation, Workshop editing/transaction paths and the complete test suite were not read end to end. No estimate is offered for those areas. The one established positive LOC opportunity above is deliberately modest; it does not imply a broader deletion target for the crate.

#### 1. Restrict the retired AI-declaration migration switch to its remaining test use

**Evidence and callers.** `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/entities/config.rs:346` implements the ordinary parser by passing `AiDeclarationMode::DEFAULT` to a public `from_toml_in_mode` at line 359. The mode enum at `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/entities/ai_declaration_manifest.rs:683` exposes `Lenient` and `Strict`, while line 695 fixes the default to `Strict`. At `entities/config.rs:1315`, that parameter decides whether the final missing-declaration check runs.

The entry-point documentation at `entities/config.rs:352` explicitly says leniency survives for incomplete test fixtures and has no production callers. A repository-wide Rust search corroborated that distinction: non-test code calls the ordinary parser; calls selecting `Lenient` occur in fixture and test files. Concrete users include `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/entities/marker_validate_tests.rs:41`, `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/entities/config_tests.rs:143`, `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/entities/spawner_tests.rs:50`, `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/entities/include_resolve_tests.rs:897`, and `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/server_app/world_setup_ship_spawn_tests.rs:7`. This is several test consumers, not literally one call site.

**Why this is unnecessary machinery.** Missing AI intent is now a content error, not a supported production configuration. Yet the public production parser still presents the completed migration as a selectable load policy, with a separate default constant and a bypass-capable public method. Readers must determine whether leniency is a legitimate host/editor mode before understanding the invariant. The stale comment immediately before the branch, at `entities/config.rs:1310`, still describes the pre-switch world in which synthesizers fill missing policies; the entry-point comment describes the opposite, current behavior. That is an observable maintenance cost of retaining the migration framing, although it is not evidence of a current gameplay bug.

**Concrete opportunity and deletion test.** Keep the existing shared parsing implementation and the fixture capability, but expose lenient loading only through the established test-support surface. Make the ordinary production entry point unconditionally enforce complete AI declarations. Retire the production-facing mode/default-switch framing where it is no longer needed; do not replace it with a new validation strategy abstraction. This removes an unsupported choice from the normal interface without forcing isolated schema fixtures to author unrelated AI policy blocks. The parent already activates `phoenix-sim-gameplay/test-support` in `crates/phoenix-simulation/Cargo.toml:78`, and forwards that feature at line 8, so there is an existing boundary to use.

**Invariants and tests.** Preserve the full validation sequence and error precedence: the missing-declaration check presently runs after validation of authored blocks. Strict parsing must continue accepting scenery and intentional idle declarations, reject omitted AI on capable systems, and name the same missing slots. Preserve the single parser body so strict and test-fixture paths do not drift. Existing coverage includes `strict_mode_is_on_by_default_and_every_shipped_hull_still_loads` at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/entities/ai_declaration_manifest_tests.rs:297`, shipped-hull acceptance at line 327, missing-policy rejection at line 361, absent-idle-field guidance at line 395, and scenery acceptance at line 411. The fixture callers above also need to remain usable. Verification after such a change should cover ordinary builds without test-support and the parent tests with it; unit-test feature unification alone would not prove the production surface is restricted.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limitations.** Low-priority API cleanup, not a performance finding. The underlying lenient behavior remains justified by many real tests; deleting it altogether would add irrelevant fixture complexity. Restricting a public method is a source compatibility change for any consumers outside the inspected repository. Confidence is high that current repository production callers do not need the switch, and medium that narrowing it is worthwhile relative to the small amount of code saved. Do not add elaborate feature machinery merely to remove an enum; use the already-existing test-support convention. No runtime savings are claimed.

#### 2. Remove target selection around the identical faction-resource dereference implementation

**Evidence and callers.** `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/entities/config_cache.rs:6` selects a WASM-only `Deref` implementation for `FactionRegistryResource`. Line 14 selects its non-WASM counterpart. Both implementations have exactly the same target type and return `&self.0`; the resource itself is unconditionally declared at line 4. There is no platform-specific operation, dependency, or storage in either body.

The wrapper is live and should remain: it is inserted by `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/server_app/registration.rs:1039` and the preload path in `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/entities/config_cache.rs:1176`, with a headless insertion at `src/headless/app.rs:278`. Consumers include faction-aware sensors (`crates/phoenix-simulation/src/ship/sensors.rs:530`), weapons (`crates/phoenix-simulation/src/console/weapons/server.rs:1350`), and GM projection (`crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_projection.rs:802`). Its identity is also recorded in the determinism symbol aliases at `tests/fixtures/determinism/layer-symbol-renames.json:28`.

**Why this is unnecessary machinery.** Two mutually exclusive compilation branches imply a platform distinction that no longer exists in this owner. A maintainer must compare both bodies and keep both updated. This is a small but definite case of target configuration carrying no behavior. It is distinct from the real native/filesystem versus browser/preload split elsewhere in entity loading.

**Concrete opportunity and deletion test.** Retain one unconditional `impl Deref for FactionRegistryResource` and remove the redundant body and both target conditions. Deleting the distinction leaves exactly the same implementation selected on each target. Keep the resource newtype, module path and underlying `FactionRegistry` unchanged.

**Invariants and tests.** Preserve Bevy Resource identity and borrowed access to the same registry; do not fold the registry into a different resource or alter faction ordering/state. Existing parent AI tests define a `Res<FactionRegistryResource>` reader at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/ai/server_tests.rs:1294`, and assert native resource availability at line 2011. Those are resource-wiring checks rather than direct evidence about this `Deref` body. Native and WASM compilation would be sufficient targeted verification for the deletion; no new behavior test is warranted for two identical implementations becoming one. Tests were inspected, not executed.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limitations.** High confidence, low impact. This removes only a handful of maintained lines and false configuration complexity. It should not be advertised as a performance improvement or as grounds to eliminate genuine target adapters.

#### Suspected overengineering that current requirements justify

- **Independent package and compatibility facades.** The module re-exports in `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/lib.rs:5`, the `world::flags` compatibility path at line 25 and the component facade at line 33 are not independently sufficient evidence of needless abstraction. `pasm/spec/architecture/reusable-layers.yaml` explicitly requires independent Gameplay/World/Session branches and compatibility surfaces. Reintroducing parent or sibling imports to shorten paths would violate the boundary being audited.
- **Live world context for AI.** `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/ai/host.rs:146` has an `AiWorldView` trait implemented by the parent at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/ai/host.rs:165`. Its borrowed flag-chain and name access provide the required cross-domain seam without a copied mission cache. The `emitter()` factory at gameplay line 153 and parent line 131 is thin and could be cosmetically shortened, but `AiEmitter::emit` still centralizes the call to Admission. The domain vocabulary explicitly names that seam. I found no meaningful present cost sufficient to recommend dismantling it, and no evidence that replacing it with a generic event bus or injectable command strategy would help.
- **Helm axis polymorphism.** `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/ship/helm_ai/mod.rs:391` has six current `HelmAxisHost` implementations, not an unused extension mechanism. The interface preserves stateful versus stateless policy resolution, the Lateral pre-override, axis-specific fact seeding, and on-change actuation. `run_helm_axis` at line 523 gives these callers a common decision path. Deleting the trait would spread this sequencing into six hosts; shortening the interface without preserving those differences risks behavior changes. This was a targeted inspection of the abstraction, not a full proof of every axis rule.
- **Manual providers.** `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/ship/manual.rs:66` initially looks like a trait-object registry for one provider because of its historical comment. The implementation at line 105 registers thirteen current providers, whose implementations span lines 257–653; the aggregator at line 158 consumes them by authored System kind. The old single-provider wording is stale, but the current extensibility has multiple concrete users. Converting stateless providers to function pointers might remove some syntax, yet would largely exchange representations rather than remove the need for per-kind dispatch. No performance problem was established.
- **Template loader and extension traits.** `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/entities/loader.rs:59` intentionally distinguishes unavailable browser-preload data from final native absence. `absence_is_final` is not an optional knob without a use case; the comments describe why a missing required override must fail to compile. `ParseEntityTemplate` at `entities/include_resolve.rs:6` and `SystemDepthRuntime` at `ship/rating.rs:10` add domain behavior to types owned by other packages. Their thinness alone does not pass the deletion test: they retain ergonomic compatibility while keeping game rules out of lower layers.
- **Modifier tables and derived cache.** `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/modifiers/cache.rs:67` keeps an ordered float table, derived per-slot cache, source-backed flags and an integer table. These are not four interchangeable representations: source identity supports replacement/removal, ordered float folding preserves determinism, and cached multipliers serve frequent reads. Existing tests at `modifiers/cache_tests.rs:544`, line 630 and line 647 cover stacking, independence and region-exit cleanup. The cache may still rebuild after an unchanged write, but that is a narrower optimization question; it does not establish that the cache or ordered source table is unnecessary. No profile was collected.

#### Coverage limits

This was not an exhaustive line-by-line review of this large package. The strongest conclusions concern the parser's public configuration surface and the identical target branches. I did not deeply audit each weapon's damage/trajectory implementation, region geometry and effects, ship damage/physics math, or every docking, transporter, tractor, umbilical, security, demolition, infrastructure, civilian and debris mechanic. I inspected the AI policy and selector abstractions for purpose and callers, not every evaluator branch or authored asset. Parent integration code was followed only far enough to establish relevant consumers and test boundaries. No scenario pacing, authored balance, schedule registration changes, network behavior or client implementation is proposed. Absence of additional findings in those areas is not an assertion that they contain no simplification opportunities.


Audit 10/72 · Agent: `/root/audit10_gameplay_reuse` · Lens: reuse

### Reuse

Module: Simulation Gameplay (`crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/`). Source inspected in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, against the audit's source baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e` and documentation-only successor HEAD specified in the audit instructions. This is a read-only source audit; no builds or tests were run.

Scope and inspected areas: crate manifest and public facade; entity include/override/loader adapters and selected weapon schema/marker validation; weapon geometry, phaser, torpedo and blaster helpers and pattern validation; SystemHull/ShipArcHull damage and repair distribution; weapon-console shared helpers and fact producers; AI host gate and selector; selected capability APIs and test inventories for Dock, Tractor, Transporter, Demolition and Umbilical. I traced the damage and geometry implementations into their actual Simulation adapters and inspected their corresponding owner-local tests. Orientation included `AGENTS.md`, `CONTEXT.md`, wiki schema/index/reusable-layers/shared-behaviour pages and the reusable-layers, weapons, engineering-damage and deterministic-simulation PASM slices. No nested crate `AGENTS.md` was found; `docs/adr/` does not exist in this source tree.

#### 1. The two hull owners maintain the same damage-tier calculation and weighted damage walk

**Evidence and live callers.** `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/ship/damage.rs:278` (`SystemHull::tier_for`) and `:688` (`ShipArcHull::tier_for`) implement the same decision: unknown entry is Operational; exactly zero current HP is Destroyed; otherwise compute current/max with the same non-positive-max fallback and compare the same configurable Disabled and Damaged thresholds. These are live rules, not obsolete counterparts. `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/ship/damage_sync.rs:48` and `:65` call both to decide which Systems are offline. System tiers also drive repair selection (`crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/modifiers/repair_teams.rs:895`) and coordination modifiers (`crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/modifiers/coordination.rs:200`).

The larger duplication is `SystemHull::apply_damage_within` at `ship/damage.rs:320` versus `ShipArcHull::apply_damage` at `:712`. Both repeatedly sum positive current HP in authored order, consume one `unit_f32` draw, subtract weights in that order, select the first negative residual, fall back to the last live entry on floating-point residue, absorb the available HP, and repeat on spill. Their comments explicitly describe the same policy. The former has an additional allow-list predicate and uses `SystemId`; the latter uses arc strings. Both retain separate ordered vectors alongside maps.

These two walks can execute for the same hit. For example, `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/console/weapons/beam.rs:1739`, `console/weapons/torpedo.rs:1398`, `regions/server.rs:283` and `server_app/collision.rs:414` apply absorbed hull damage to the arc hull after ordinary system damage. The GM path does so at `gm_effect.rs:647` and uses the scoped repair path at `:656`. Therefore merging the algorithms must not merge the two damage operations: their distinct mutations and RNG draws are part of the existing result.

**Reuse opportunity.** Share the exact tier calculation locally in `ship/damage.rs`, with each owner retaining its lookup and missing-entry behavior. For weighted damage, extract the common ordered selection/spill machinery behind the two existing owner methods. The purpose is one implementation of an existing policy, not a new universal health-component framework. Keep the hull types, public methods and domain-specific metadata distinct. SystemHull's scope must be supplied to every weight, choice and fallback step; an unscoped arc hull is not an excuse to weaken that rule.

`SystemHull::restore_distributed_within` at `ship/damage.rs:478` repeats much of the selection walk as well, but its semantics differ: weights are nonnegative missing HP, mutation adds HP and the result reports the amount restored. It is a secondary reuse candidate for the ordered-choice primitive, not a reason to force damage and repair into one public operation. Start with the two identical damage walks and only incorporate repair if the resulting code makes those differences explicit.

**Why it matters.** Fixes to threshold boundaries, draw handling, spill behavior or rounding currently require parallel edits. The duplicated tier rule directly feeds authority availability; the weighted walk directly feeds deterministic state. Removing these specific duplicate implementations makes agreement structural while keeping the existing public surfaces comprehensible.

**Behavior to preserve.** Authored iteration order; one RNG draw per successful weighted-selection iteration; exact accumulation/subtraction order; last-positive-entry fallback; depleted-entry exclusion; no draws when no positive weight exists; scope filtering during every spill; zero-HP classification and strict threshold comparisons; unknown IDs remaining Operational; independent System and arc hull mutations. Keep `unit_f32`'s high-24-bit conversion unchanged (`ship/damage.rs:27`). No new dependency on World, Session, the parent Simulation crate or rendering is necessary.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** `ship/damage_tests.rs:274` and `:283` cover total reduction and depleted entries; `:441` covers weighted preference. The scope-overrun regression at `:345` exercises both damage and repair and checks that spills never escape the allow-list. Tier tests start at `:475`, with configurable thresholds at `:510`. Arc tests cover damage at `:683`, tier changes at `:696`, unknown arcs at `:718`, authored order at `:732`, and weighted preference at `:739`. Preserve both domain test groups: sharing implementation does not make their different entry points redundant. The inspected tests do not establish an exact pre-refactor draw-sequence/next-RNG-value contract across both owners. Before extraction, add a fixed-seed regression with unequal HP, at least one exhausted entry, multiple spills and reordered declarations; assert each resulting entry and subsequent RNG output. Include empty and restrictive scopes, exact threshold equality and non-positive max cases for the tier helper. This is stronger than checking only total HP or a statistical preference.

**Tradeoff and confidence.** High confidence that the tier and damage algorithms duplicate one another. Tier sharing is a small, low-risk change. Weighted-walk sharing has medium implementation risk: generic entry access or allocation-heavy adapters could obscure a clear loop or change floating-point order. Keep the helper private, avoid building a sorted or normalized weight distribution, and do not replace the walk with a library sampler merely because it produces the same probability distribution. Statistical equivalence is insufficient for this deterministic simulator. No existing repository-wide weighted sampler with proven identical semantics was identified in the inspected areas.

#### 2. Phaser and torpedo signed-angle differences are identical, but nearby angle helpers are not interchangeable

**Evidence and live callers.** `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/weapons/phaser.rs:299` and `weapons/torpedo.rs:1220` each define the same private `angle_diff(a, b)`: subtract, repeatedly subtract `2*PI` while above `PI`, and repeatedly add `2*PI` while below `-PI`. Both preserve signed endpoints in `[-PI, PI]`.

The phaser copy feeds `in_arc` at `phaser.rs:256` and `target_geometry` at `:271` (difference calculation at `:288`). These are authoritative/readiness calculations, with real fire gates in `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/console/weapons/beam.rs:523`, `:912` and `:1211`. The torpedo copy feeds `Torpedo::tick`'s signed turn at `torpedo.rs:145` and tube arc membership at `:409`. Simulation's torpedo lifecycle invokes that machinery at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/console/weapons/torpedo.rs:1131` and `:1185`, through `TorpedoSystem::tick` and its per-projectile call at `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/weapons/torpedo.rs:1068`.

**Reuse opportunity.** Put the existing exact signed-radian difference implementation in a shared weapons-local geometry helper and have both families use it. This is a narrow reuse of existing code; it does not require moving all weapon geometry into a new framework or making torpedo mechanics depend on phaser state.

**Compatibility differences that limit the change.** `weapons/blaster.rs:615` normalizes a heading to `(-PI, PI]`, using `<= -PI`, whereas these two difference functions use `< -PI`. `weapons/arc_geometry.rs:213` normalizes degrees to `(-180, 180]` and first uses remainder. Those are not drop-in replacements. Torpedo steering consumes the sign, so changing which direction wins at an exact half-turn changes movement; converting to degrees or using a different modulo formula can also change rounding. The blaster helper is used for its fallback firing heading (`blaster.rs:799`) and should retain that contract. The degree helper serves world-bearing sectors and arc-exposure reduction, not the signed-radian turn contract. Do not consolidate them solely on similar names.

**Why it matters.** The duplicated code is small, but it carries a subtle signed-boundary contract. One implementation avoids a later correctness fix or normalization change reaching only firing geometry or only homing. Phaser's public geometry already gets reused by other fire/readiness paths; this extends that existing reuse at the exact arithmetic boundary rather than introducing another calculation.

**Behavior to preserve.** Exact comparison operators and subtraction sequence, radian units, signed half-turn endpoints, and existing deterministic `simmath` trigonometry at call sites. This finding is not a recommendation to change non-finite input behavior or optimize arbitrary-angle normalization as part of a mechanical reuse edit. Those would be separate behavior changes.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** `weapons/phaser_tests.rs:91` onward covers directional arc membership; its yaw-swept broad-arc regressions are at `:233`, `:257` and `:281`. `weapons/torpedo_tests.rs:384` covers homing, `:404` rate limiting, `:427` loss of target, and `:446` launch target retention. These owner tests remain useful after extraction. Add exact positive and negative half-turn cases and wrapped multi-turn inputs to the common calculation, plus a homing assertion at an exact opposite bearing to pin turn direction. Existing broad arc tests alone can hide a sign change because they compare an absolute difference.

**Tradeoff and confidence.** High confidence: the two function bodies are identical and both have live users. Benefit is modest because each copy is short. The helper must not become an opportunity to unify all angle conventions; doing that would make this straightforward reuse change materially riskier.

#### Examined similarities that do not establish additional findings

- Content composition is already reused. `entities/entity_override.rs:2` re-exports Content's implementation. `entities/include_resolve.rs:3` re-exports its include machinery; the small `ParseEntityTemplate` adapter at `:6` supplies `EntityConfig::from_toml` to that machinery. The gameplay parser owns full entity semantics by the explicit package design. Deleting this adapter or importing sibling World validation would undo that boundary.
- Blaster and torpedo authored firing patterns already share `weapons/pattern.rs:52`; the configuration adapters call it at `entities/config/weapons.rs:460` and `:499`. Their distinct launch/load/volley state machines do not become duplicates merely because both consume pattern steps. Ammo reservation, timed loading and homing make the torpedo lifecycle materially different.
- The AI Host Spine already centralizes the Control Source gate and declaration/resolve sequence (`ai/host.rs:96`, `:140`). The selector uses additive utility ranking, not the hull damage sampler's weighted random selection. Reusing that selector for hull damage would change semantics.
- Phaser and blaster fact builders (`console/weapons/beam.rs:368`, `console/weapons/blaster.rs:39`) seed a similar handful of facts, with Phaser additionally seeding frequency. Their live producers call these at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/console/weapons/beam.rs:854` and `console/weapons/blaster.rs:452`. A common initializer could remove a few lines, but this inspection did not establish a meaningful maintenance problem warranting another abstraction. Family-specific fact declaration remains valuable, so this is not raised as a finding.
- Crate-local compatibility modules and re-exports in `lib.rs` are not second runtime implementations. Independent owner tests and pure-JavaScript editor counterparts are not candidates for deletion as duplicate production code.

#### Coverage limits

This is a focused implementation audit, not an exhaustive duplication census of this large crate. The two findings above are grounded in both implementations, their live callers and existing test bodies. I did not fully inspect every Helm policy/fact branch, AI declaration manifest, complete EntityConfig validation path, shield state machine, repair-team sweep lifecycle, power allocator, region-effect processor, security-team lifecycle, sensor implementation or capability adapter. Capability function/test inventories were used for orientation rather than asserted as full coverage. Cross-module callers were read only far enough to establish the referenced contracts; this report does not claim a full schedule, native/WASM numerical or integration-test audit. Proposed verification is not a claim that tests were executed or passed.


Audit 11/72 · Agent: `/root/audit11_gameplay_architecture` · Lens: architecture

### Architecture

Module: Simulation Gameplay (`crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/`). Source inspected in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, against source baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e`; the audit instructions identify HEAD `2de7d5329fba55e06ad00fbd8ca13f0cb4e8c73c` as a documentation-only successor.

Scope and inspection: package manifest and public module surface; AI Host Spine and live-world input seam; Helm axis driver, Engines and Lateral implementations and their parent adapters; phaser state, cooldown, and parent beam callers; weapons shared query grouping; supporting admission, ship state and damage interfaces. Followed parent callers and sampled their existing tests. Read the audit instructions, architecture skill and LANGUAGE.md, root AGENTS.md constraints, wiki schema/index, domain vocabulary, reusable-layer guide, AI Helm guide, reusable-layer PASM slice and relevant Helm/AI/weapons PASM material. No nested AGENTS.md was found in the repository file inventory; `docs/adr/` was absent. This is a source inspection, not executed validation.

#### 1. Deepen the phaser lifecycle module so a completed cycle owns its cooldown transition

**Files and evidence.**

- `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/console/weapons/beam.rs:67` exposes the active slot's remaining duration, damage accumulator and pending cooldown. The comment at line 72 explicitly says the cooldown belongs to the same jittered cycle as the firing duration.
- The same file's `ActiveBeam` implementation at lines 187, 208, 216 and 222 exposes separate start, pending-cooldown lookup, removal and mutable-slot operations. `PhaserCooldown::start_bank` is separate again at line 263.
- Real callers in `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/console/weapons/beam.rs:1165`, `:1224`, `:2052` and `:2082` each implement the sequence: read pending cooldown, use an authored fallback if absent, remove the active bank, start that bank's cooldown, and trigger a beam-ended event. These paths cover vanished targets, arc rejection, destruction and ordinary expiry.
- At `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/console/weapons/beam.rs:1251`, the parent mutates the slot's fractional damage debt directly. At `:2074`, it mutates remaining lifetime directly and decides whether to perform the transition.

**Problem.** The gameplay module hides the map but only part of the lifecycle invariant. Its interface requires callers to know that reading pending cooldown must precede removing the slot, that zero represents old snapshot state and therefore requires a fallback, and that removal also obliges a cooldown update. This is gameplay knowledge repeated across the parent implementation, beyond the parent's legitimate job of collecting live targets, applying damage and publishing events. There is no demonstrated current malfunction: all four inspected endings use the correct sequence. The architectural friction is that a future ending path or cooldown rule needs the same knowledge in several places.

**Solution.** Concentrate ordinary cycle completion and its pending-cooldown fallback rule in the gameplay phaser module. Let the parent retain target/geometry decisions, damage orchestration and event publication, while relying on the gameplay module to perform a coherent bank transition. Fractional damage accumulation and lifetime advancement are adjacent candidates for the same module when that produces a smaller caller interface. Do not move the entire ECS damage system into gameplay or invent a generic weapon lifecycle abstraction: those would exceed the demonstrated opportunity.

**Benefits.** This increases depth by placing a meaningful transition behind the interface rather than presenting map manipulation primitives. It improves locality for jitter/resume behavior and provides leverage across the four existing ordinary ending paths. Focused tests could exercise expiry, severance, legacy fallback and fractional carry through the same module interface callers use, without constructing an entire combat app.

**Deletion test.** Deleting `ActiveBeam` would scatter ordered storage and per-bank state across callers; it earns its keep. Conversely, merely renaming its accessors or combining the two cooldown setter spellings would not solve this finding. The opportunity is to deepen the existing module, not remove its useful state ownership.

**Behavior that must survive.** Preserve authored bank-id iteration order, the single linked jitter draw and its already-recorded cooldown, snapshot restoration of fractional debt, independent simultaneous banks, and existing event timing. Keep snapshot restore distinct from starting fire: `beam.rs:226` explicitly documents why restore cannot reset fractional damage. Preserve the exceptional relight cleanup at parent `beam.rs:541`: it removes an old beam and emits an ending event without the ordinary cooldown transition, and is currently guarded out. It must not accidentally acquire ordinary completion semantics through blanket consolidation. Preserve the current order of damage accumulation, line-of-sight handling and lifetime advancement; even mathematically similar rearrangements can change tick results.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** Parent `console/weapons/server_tests.rs:1853`, `:1881`, `:2172` and `:2217` cover arc/range severance, vanished targets and independent cooldown behavior. The test at `:5387` explicitly checks that an expiring cycle serves its drawn cooldown, and `:5424` checks that an unjittered bank consumes no jitter-stream draw. The inspected test comment at `:5375` also points to snapshot-resume coverage; that referenced test was not independently inspected. Gameplay `weapons/phaser_tests.rs` has bank geometry/range coverage, but no owner-local tests for this `ActiveBeam` transition were found in the gameplay test search. Add owner-local transition tests if this change is pursued, while retaining parent integration checks for events and damage ordering. This is not a claim that the behavior is currently untested.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limits.** Moderate implementation scope: snapshot and digest consumers require state access, so full field privatization is not the objective. A transition shared across two state holders needs care to preserve ECS access and phase order. Confidence is high in the observed repeated caller knowledge and moderate in the size of the useful extraction; not every beam caller or restoration path was inspected. No replacement interface is prescribed here.

#### 2. Concentrate Helm operator query knowledge in gameplay while retaining the live-world adapter seam

**Files and evidence.**

- `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/ship/helm_ai/engines.rs:107` exposes an operator that accepts the world view plus Bevy resources and an eleven-element entity query. Its parent wrapper at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/ship/helm_ai/engines.rs:16` restates that query and forwards it at line 44.
- The same pattern is visible in gameplay `ship/helm_ai/lateral.rs:111` and parent `ship/helm_ai/lateral.rs:12`: the parent repeats the exact entity fields, optionality, mutable admitted-command access and high-fidelity filter before forwarding.
- Gameplay `ai/host.rs:146` defines `AiWorldView`; the parent implementation is at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/ai/host.rs:165`. This seam supplies live borrowed flags/names without gameplay importing World or Session.
- Gameplay already consolidates real operator behavior in `ship/helm_ai/mod.rs:391` (`HelmAxisHost`) and `:523` (`run_helm_axis`), with different concrete axis implementations. It also uses owner-defined query grouping for related policy state at `:79` (`HelmPolicyRuntime`).

**Problem.** Gameplay owns the operator and its interpretation of entity state, but the composing caller must spell out its detailed query shape. Changing an operator's required state therefore touches both its owner and a parent wrapper that does not interpret that state. The interface is deeper in policy execution than in input acquisition. Optionality is especially significant: the Lateral wrapper comments explain that making `BehaviourSection` required would silently exclude ships, so these are semantic declarations, not harmless repeated type names.

**Solution.** Concentrate the stable, owner-specific query declaration or system-input grouping beside each gameplay operator, allowing the parent to name that grouping while continuing to supply the concrete live-world adapter and register the system in the existing schedule. Keep this narrowly scoped to repeated operator input knowledge; do not make every axis query identical, copy mission facts into a refreshed resource, or introduce a generic event bus.

**Benefits.** Improved locality: an operator's entity selection and borrow requirements change in the same module as its implementation. Increased leverage: the parent composes an operator without restating all its internal inputs. It also makes an owner-local test adapter more practical because tests can use the same declared query surface rather than maintaining another hand-written projection.

**Deletion test and justified structure.** Deleting the parent wrapper would not remove its necessary work: something must obtain the concrete World/Session context and expose a schedulable Bevy system while gameplay remains independent. Deleting `AiWorldView` would push forbidden imports or copied context into gameplay. Both structures therefore earn their keep under PASM `architecture/reusable-layers.yaml:13`. Likewise, `HelmAxisHost` has multiple real adapters with different behavior; the inspection does not support replacing it with six independent decision implementations. The candidate targets leaked query knowledge, not these seams.

**Behavior that must survive.** Preserve all query filters, optional fields and access sets; retain high-fidelity scoping and per-entity command emission through Admission. Preserve the parent schedule registration, cadence, state-machine-before-output order and same-tick visibility of world flags. Engines' missing-plan early exit (`engines.rs:145`) and Lateral's missing-frame early exit (`lateral.rs:141`) are different and must remain different. Do not widen one query to satisfy another axis, since that can change entity participation or Bevy scheduling.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** Parent `ship/helm_ai/mod_tests.rs:53` exercises the common decision seam and `:85` checks every axis's AI gate/accepted verb. The existing suite additionally names tests for shipped hull execution (`:352`), independent gates (`:600`) and high-fidelity scoping (`:1032`); only the common decision test body was inspected closely. No owner-local Helm axis test sibling was found in the gameplay file inventory. If this change proceeds, place narrow tests of gameplay operator selection/emission at its interface with a small live-world test adapter, and retain parent tests for actual scheduling and world-context wiring. Tests must not import the simulation parent into gameplay.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limits.** This is a lower-priority maintainability opportunity, not a correctness defect. A grouping adds a name and may be less transparent than a short query; it is worthwhile only where it removes repeated semantic declarations. Do not use it to hide broad mutable access or treat reduced signature length as depth by itself. Confidence is high in the Engines/Lateral duplication and moderate that extending the approach to other axes is worthwhile; their full wrapper implementations were not audited here.

#### Coverage limits and retained architecture

This large module was sampled around its exposed mechanics and operator seams. The audit did not deeply inspect every entity parser/validation branch, the complete policy evaluator, all Helm fact builders, docking/tractor/transporter coupling, security/demolition/science/civilian mechanics, region effects, repair, objective mechanics, or all weapon families. File inventory and symbol searches are not exhaustive review of those implementations. No claims of complete absence of other opportunities or runtime regressions are made.

Compatibility facades in `lib.rs`, independent branch manifests, deterministic math/RNG contracts and parent-owned live composition were treated as requirements, not candidate deletions. Pure damage and physics modules should remain Bevy-free; the proposed phaser and Helm work does not justify moving ECS dependencies into them. No source edits, builds, tests, formatting, installations or network research were performed.

### Reducing maintained lines

Module: Simulation Gameplay (`crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/`). Source inspected in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, against the supplied source baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e`; the supplied current HEAD changes navigation documentation only. This was a read-only audit; no builds, tests, source edits or network research were performed.

Scope and orientation: read the repository operating constraints, wiki schema, vocabulary, index and reusable-layer ownership guidance; inspected the reusable-layers architecture slice and the shield/weapon architecture material relevant to the candidates. No nested `AGENTS.md` was found beneath `crates/`, and `docs/adr` is absent. Inventoried the Gameplay Rust files and their sizes, then concentrated on crate composition, entity schema/defaults and policy-validation dispatch, weapon console state and policy helpers, and their actual Simulation callers and tests. The findings below are bounded opportunities, not a claim that the whole gameplay implementation has been exhaustively audited.

#### 1. Use the existing struct defaults as the deserialization defaults for three fully optional configuration blocks

**Files and evidence.** `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/entities/config/shields.rs:53` defines `ShieldsConsoleConfig`, with a field-level Serde default declaration for each field and a separate `Default` implementation at line 135. Seven private three-line helper functions at lines 113–133 exist solely to supply scalar defaults to those two paths. `ShieldsBaseConfig` repeats the pattern at lines 165–204: four field default annotations, four private scalar helpers, and a struct `Default`. `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/entities/config/repair.rs:18` does the same for `RepairConfig`, including two scalar helpers at lines 53–58 and the `Default` implementation at line 60. Searches of Gameplay source found no additional callers of those thirteen private helper functions.

These defaults are exercised through the complete entity parser, not just test constructors. The schemas are exported by `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/entities/config.rs:29` and `:33`. Composed templates invoke `EntityConfig::from_toml` through `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/entities/include_resolve.rs:10`. Simulation consumes the parsed repair timing conversion at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/entities/ship_spawn.rs:506` and shield base conversion at `:758`.

**Observed maintenance cost.** Each scalar's default is already centralized numerically, but its plumbing is maintained three times: a field attribute names a helper, the helper declares its value, and `Default` calls the helper. For these three structs every field already admits omission. Serde's container default mechanism can express that existing contract without retaining the field-by-field plumbing.

**Concrete change opportunity.** On these three structs only, use a container-level `#[serde(default, deny_unknown_fields)]`, retain the existing manual `Default` implementations, and put the scalar values directly in those implementations. Remove the thirteen now-unused private scalar helpers and redundant field-level default attributes. Keep unrelated Serde behavior, particularly `skip_serializing_if` on `RepairConfig::external_dispatch`. Keep `default_shield_frequency`: it is also referenced by `ShieldArcConfig` at `shields.rs:266`, so this change does not make it dead. Leave the structs' public names and their `to_runtime` conversion methods intact.

This is true deletion of repeated default machinery, not relocation into another crate or a macro. The thirteen helpers account for 39 physical lines before adjacent blank lines. Removing the redundant attributes contributes approximately another 19 lines while the existing container-attribute lines and `Default` bodies can be updated in place. Thus roughly **55–65 production lines** are removable in the three inspected blocks. This is a gross production reduction; the final repository net reduction depends on how much additional missing-field coverage is required. No test or explanatory documentation deletion is included in that estimate.

**Behavior that must survive.** Missing fields must resolve to exactly the same values, explicit values must continue overriding defaults, and unknown fields must remain errors. An absent `[repair]` or shield base block must remain absent; defaults inside a present block must not manufacture equipment. In particular, omitted repair-team count still means zero and `RepairConfig::declares_teams` at `repair.rs:72` must continue gating that capability. Shield focus values, 0.5 frequency, `None` policy/base values, serialization field names, and omission of absent external dispatch must remain unchanged. No schema-wide blanket default is proposed: `ShieldArcConfig` at `shields.rs:227` has required identity/geometry fields, and defaulting those would weaken content validation.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** Retain `repair_block_full_round_trips`, `repair_block_absent_yields_none`, `repair_block_partial_keeps_defaults_for_missing_fields`, conversion and runtime-default parity tests at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/entities/config_tests.rs:4380`, `:4393`, `:4399`, `:4411`, and `:4423`. Shield tests cover full fields, absence, partial defaults, conversion, and runtime parity at the same file's lines 4461, 4483, 4503, 4521, and 4536. These are existing parent integration tests; using them for verification does not justify importing the parent into Gameplay. Add or extend narrow Gameplay-owned parsing cases for an empty and partially authored `ShieldsConsoleConfig`, explicit scalar overrides, and unknown-field rejection if those paths lack direct coverage. Preserve the pure shield mechanics tests, including recovery behavior at `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/weapons/shield_tests.rs:66`; none becomes redundant because parsing plumbing shrinks.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limitations.** Confidence is high that the listed helper/attribute duplication is removable; behavior equivalence has not been executed in this audit. Container defaults mean a future new field with a `Default` value also permits omission automatically. That matches the current three blocks but must remain a conscious schema policy. Keep field comments describing defaults so readers do not need to navigate the implementation for basic authoring information. Do not extend this mechanically to structs with required fields or deserialize-specific defaults.

#### 2. Remove the duplicate phaser cooldown setter after switching its two test callers

**Files and evidence.** `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/console/weapons/beam.rs:263` implements `PhaserCooldown::start_bank(&mut self, bank: &str, cooldown_secs: f32)` by inserting the supplied value into the per-bank map. At line 267, `start_bank_with_cooldown(&mut self, bank: &str, secs: f32)` performs the identical insertion. Neither clamps, adds a default, accumulates a value, or uses a different key.

Repository searches across `crates`, `src`, and `tests` found only two callers of the longer name: the `start_phaser_cooldown` fixtures in `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/console/weapons/server_tests.rs:562` and `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/server_app/mod_tests.rs:1034`. Production uses the shorter method, including beam termination at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/console/weapons/beam.rs:1168`, `:1228`, `:2057`, and `:2086`, and target despawn at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_despawn.rs:263`.

**Concrete change opportunity and benefit.** Change the two fixture calls to `start_bank` and delete `start_bank_with_cooldown`. This removes **three production lines plus a separator blank line** and one redundant API name. It is a small but certain deletion, not a proposed generic cooldown abstraction. The existing tests then exercise the same public setter used by the runtime instead of its identical alias.

**Behavior that must survive.** Preserve insert/replace semantics and the supplied cooldown value exactly, including zero or negative fixture values. Do not clamp in the setter as part of this cleanup. Preserve per-bank isolation, tick decay, sorted snapshot capture, and wholesale restore. These live in other methods of the same implementation (`beam.rs:254`–298) and are not candidates for removal. The cooldown value already bought by a beam cycle must continue being passed by each production termination path; deleting an alias does not authorize recomputing that value.

**Existing tests and verification.** Keep all callers and assertions, changing only the method name in the two fixtures. Existing integration coverage includes `fire_phaser_rejected_during_cooldown` at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/console/weapons/server_tests.rs:1701`, `retarget_after_cooldown_cancels_prior_beam_and_starts_new` at `:1953`, and `independent_bank_cooldown_does_not_disturb_live_captured_beam` at `:2217`. No new behavior test is needed solely to prove two identical insertions are identical; the affected fixtures must still compile and the existing cooldown cases should run during implementation validation.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limitations.** High confidence for in-repository callers. The method is public, so deleting it could break an uninspected external Rust consumer. If preserving that precise public symbol is a contractual requirement, retain a forwarding alias and do not claim the three-line deletion. There is no evidence in the inspected repository that the longer method is a required historical compatibility path. This modest cleanup is best bundled with nearby maintenance rather than treated as a substantial refactor.

#### Inspected similarities that do not currently justify line-reduction work

- **Authored config versus runtime config:** `ShieldsBaseConfig::to_runtime` at `entities/config/shields.rs:208` copies four matching fields into `weapons/shield.rs:62`'s `ShieldConfig`. They look collapsible, but the authored type also owns Serde traits, strict unknown-field behavior and partial-field defaults; the runtime type is a pure mechanics input. The default-plumbing reduction above keeps this boundary. `RepairConfig::to_runtime` at `entities/config/repair.rs:97` projects only part of a larger authored block and renames the travel field; that is meaningful conversion, not a disposable copy. No savings are claimed for merging either pair.
- **Weapon fact seeders:** `console/weapons/beam.rs:368` and `console/weapons/blaster.rs:39` repeat readiness fact insertion, but phasers additionally seed frequency, and both small functions have concrete typed signatures. A generic parameter object/helper would introduce lines of its own and could accidentally seed unsupported facts for the wrong host. No net reduction has been established, so none is claimed. Their policy-fire functions are live production callers, not dead post-spine remnants.
- **Policy-validation dispatch:** `entities/config.rs:963`–1024 repeats optional Helm policy validation, but the host identity, channel and allowed verbs differ, and Steering additionally validates its parameter sets at line 982. Power's allowed channels are derived from authored groups at lines 1146–1164. A blanket registry or macro would require new metadata, retain these exceptions, and interact with host-naming coverage (`crates/phoenix-simulation/src/entities/ai_flag_hosts_tests.rs:985`). This audit has not demonstrated a worthwhile net deletion; preserving the explicit validated mapping is justified.
- **Compatibility modules and independent tests:** `src/lib.rs:25`, `:34`, and `:61` expose compatibility-shaped paths over contracts or owner types. They are not duplicate implementations. Moving pure mechanics tests or implementations back into Simulation to reduce this crate's count would merely relocate lines and undermine the independent sibling requirement in `pasm/spec/architecture/reusable-layers.yaml:13`. No such move is recommended.

#### Coverage limits

The crate is large: inventory identified substantial files such as `ai/core.rs`, `entities/ai_flag_hosts.rs`, `entities/ai_policy_schema.rs`, `ship/helm_ai/facts.rs`, and numerous independent test siblings. These were inventoried and selected portions examined, not fully reviewed line by line. This audit did not deeply inspect the complete AI evaluator, every policy declaration, steering geometry, physics integrators, damage distribution, modifier caching, repair-team state machine, docking/tractor/transporter/umbilical mechanics, demolition, security, civilian traffic, debris, region geometry/effects, radar, or all large test fixture bodies. It did not search external consumers of public Rust APIs. No savings are extrapolated from those unexplored areas, and no tests, documentation or required branch independence are proposed for deletion to meet a line metric.

### Overengineering

Module: Simulation World (`crates/phoenix-sim-world/`). Audit source: `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, source baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e` with the documented navigation-only successor commit.

Scope and inspection: examined the package manifest and compatibility modules; script engine construction, registration descriptors, generic and typed registration validation, loading, invocation and selected tests; trigger registry, delayed-action partitioning and registry tests; action-command conversion; task lifecycle structure; and selected deadline, commitment, workforce, recipient and Objective-instance interfaces. Traced relevant callers into Simulation, the browser bridge and Workshop. Consulted `AGENTS.md`, wiki schema/index, `CONTEXT.md`, reusable-layers guidance, World Plugin guidance and relevant portions of PASM `reusable-layers.yaml`, `world-files.yaml` and `scenario-scripting.yaml`. No nested `AGENTS.md` was found under the inspected crate tree; `docs/adr` was absent. This is a read-only, targeted audit, not exhaustive coverage of every world subsystem. No builds or tests were run.

#### 1. The generic `on(event, handler)` surface retains milestone machinery without delivering an event

**Observed implementation and callers.** `crates/phoenix-sim-world/src/world/script/engine.rs:84` defines `Registration` separately from `ScriptTrigger` at line 109. `BuilderState` keeps both collections at lines 128–131. `register_loading_vocabulary` registers `on` at line 153, records the opaque event string and function name, and advertises it through `host_fn!` as a callable authoring verb. The loader carries that collection into `CompiledScripts` (`world/script/load.rs:74`, `:289`, `:300`) and runs `validate_registrations` at `:388`. That validator (`world/script/validate.rs:72`) checks whether the handler exists; it does not connect the event string to a runtime event.

This is not merely a missing caller inferred from a name search: the composition adapter explicitly states that the generic collection has no runtime consumer (`crates/phoenix-simulation/src/world/server.rs:1806`). Typed script triggers and deadline handlers take the live activation paths. The in-repository assets search found no `on(...)` invocation in shipped `.rhai` or `.toml` content. The generic examples were found in tests, including `world/script/load_tests.rs:196` and `:218` and `world/script/engine_tests.rs:601`.

Nevertheless, the generic verb is offered to authors. The derived registry reaches the browser through `src/server/bridge.rs:4323` and native Workshop through `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/workshop/provider.rs:603`. A successful compile and handler-reference check therefore grants apparent legitimacy to a declaration that will not dispatch anything.

**Why this is overengineering.** This retains a second registration data model, collection path, validation pass and authoring vocabulary from the M1 stage, alongside the typed registration implementation that actually supplies runtime behavior. Its cost is maintenance across those sites and a misleading author-facing choice, rather than proven material CPU cost. Adding a generic runtime event dispatcher solely to justify the existing API would increase machinery without an observed shipped-content requirement.

**Concrete change opportunity / deletion test.** Retire the generic registration collection and validator, and remove `on` from autocomplete. Prefer a small, explicit load-time refusal naming the typed alternatives if compatibility requires helpful diagnostics; such a refusal does not need a second retained registration model. Rework the generic loader/validation tests to exercise typed registrations. This removes machinery without removing currently observed scenario behavior. Do not remove `ScriptTrigger`, typed `on_*` builders, `gm_event`, deadline pairing, or the shared unresolved-handler finding helper.

**Invariants and verification.** Typed handlers must retain authored order, source attribution, missing-handler rejection, atomic activation, per-layer ownership and deterministic continuation identity. Existing generic tests currently prove only collection and referential cleanliness: `full_load_of_a_resolved_registration_is_clean` proves neither dispatch nor a gameplay effect. Retain the corresponding protections using the typed paths (`world/script/validate_tests.rs:60`, `:66`). Add a regression that a legacy generic declaration receives a clear diagnostic rather than passing silently, and assert autocomplete no longer recommends it. Existing trigger and layer integration tests should remain unchanged in behavior.

**Tradeoff, confidence and limitations.** High confidence that this is inert in the inspected production graph; medium confidence on immediate removal because external/custom world content was not inventoried. Such content cannot obtain runtime events from this API today, but could intentionally use it as a function-existence assertion. The PASM scripting slice contains milestone-era descriptions (`scenario-scripting.yaml:132`, `:160`) as well as later delivered behavior; deprecation must reconcile that documented surface rather than silently treating all historical M1 declarations as current requirements. No scenario content redesign is proposed.

#### 2. `call_immediate` exposes a deliberately lossy test shortcut as a normal runtime API

**Observed implementation and callers.** `crates/phoenix-sim-world/src/world/script/engine.rs:699` exposes `RuntimeHost::call_immediate`. It creates a fresh tick budget and zero clock, supplies empty deadline, commitment and evidence state, invokes the complete host, then retains only `.commands`. `resolved_cmds` at line 59 further discards name-resolving `BufferedEffect::Action` values while logging a warning. Deferred callbacks, delayed work, Comms opens and deadline/commitment mutations disappear through the projection.

The repository-wide Rust caller search found only five calls in `world/script/engine_tests.rs:19`, `:40`, `:298`, `:597` and `world/script/triggers_tests.rs:449` (the list includes four calls in the first file and one in the second). The production invocation adapter instead uses the scoped full-result call in `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/world/script_invocation.rs:55`. There is no observed production need for the lossy method.

**Why this is overengineering.** It adds a second behavioral contract to an already substantial invocation API merely to shorten a few tests. Callers must understand which effects survive, that reads see empty auxiliary state, and that budgets reset per call. This maintenance burden is visible in contradictory documentation: `engine.rs:695` says a name-resolving effect panics, while the implementation at `:64` logs and drops it. The extra convenience surface can also let a future test pass while exercising less than the real invocation contract.

**Concrete change opportunity / deletion test.** Remove the public shortcut and `resolved_cmds` from normal runtime code. Migrate the five callers to a shared test helper around the full call, with explicit extraction/assertions for the expected command-only result. Alternatively, keep a private test-only helper if migration readability is poor. Tests should reject unexpected additional effect categories rather than silently erase them. No replacement runtime abstraction is needed.

**Invariants and verification.** Preserve the authored interleaving of flag writes and commands checked by `effects_and_flag_writes_emit_in_authored_order` (`engine_tests.rs:24`), scheduled closure execution (`:260`), dev error policy (`:589`) and typed-trigger parity (`triggers_tests.rs:449`). Keep full-result coverage for dialogue name resolution (`engine_tests.rs:70`), scheduled dialogue callbacks (`:116`), shared budget charging (`:154`) and all-buffer discard on script failure (`:506`). These existing tests demonstrate why the full result matters. The missing verification is an assertion in the command-only helpers that no non-command effects were unexpectedly emitted.

**Tradeoff, confidence and limitations.** High confidence for the in-repository usage graph. A few test call sites become more explicit, and a test helper may still be justified; the saving is a narrower production contract, not necessarily fewer total test lines. External Rust consumers were not inspected. No WASM-size or performance reduction is claimed, because unused methods may already be eliminated by the compiler.

#### Suspected complexity that is justified by current requirements

- **Two Rhai engines and operation budgets:** loading executes registration statements once, runtime invokes retained functions without rerunning top-level code (`world/script/engine.rs:139`, `:191`). The fixed hashing seed, per-call limit, aggregate budget and call cap protect deterministic native/WASM execution. The PASM scripting slice records the measured basis for these choices. Collapsing them to a generic unrestricted engine would remove requirements, not excess complexity.
- **`WorldTriggerRegistry`:** paired state/handler entries and generation invalidation (`world/trigger_registry.rs:55`, `:69`, `:165`, `:329`) prevent stale observer baselines and mispaired handlers after layer replacement or restore. Tests explicitly cover equal-length replacement (`trigger_registry_tests.rs:67`), atomic refusal (`:134`) and paused/skipped state semantics (`:185`). Replacing the registry with independently mutable vectors fails the deletion test.
- **Independent package and action-command type parameter:** `commands.rs` permits a script command to exist without a Gameplay-owned resolved spawn configuration; Simulation supplies that concrete type and preserves ordered application. The repetitive conversion is visible, but importing Gameplay to eliminate it would violate `pasm/spec/architecture/reusable-layers.yaml`'s sibling independence requirement. This audit does not recommend that shortcut or count compatibility re-exports as unnecessary abstractions.
- **Derived editor descriptors:** the `HostRegistry` wrapper and `host_fn!` macro (`world/script/registry.rs:51`, `:120`) have real browser and native Workshop consumers and eliminate a separately maintained vocabulary mirror. Finding 1 concerns one inert advertised verb, not the descriptor mechanism itself.

#### Coverage limits

The module is large. Full parser branches, native render options, every effect-registration overload, Comms choice policy, GM decision modules, complete narrative/report/evidence implementations, all Objective-instance transitions and the recipient scanner's full grammar were not audited line by line. Deadline/commitment/workforce and task lifecycle interfaces were sampled to distinguish existing domain requirements from speculative frameworks. Host adapters were read only where needed to establish callers and activation behavior. No scenario timing, consequences or balance were analyzed, and no authored world files were changed. Findings above are bounded to the inspected code and repository callers; they are not a claim that the remaining areas have no overengineering.
