### Overengineering

Module: `phoenix-runtime`.

Scope: read-only inspection of `crates/phoenix-runtime/` at the specified reusable-game-layers worktree, source baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e` (documentation-only HEAD `2de7d5329fba55e06ad00fbd8ca13f0cb4e8c73c`). Inspected every production module: `commands`, `continuation`, `digest`, `identity`, `recovery_plan`, `session`, `transfer`, package exports and manifest. Inspected the companion command, transfer, session and recovery tests, and traced representative real callers in Phoenix admission, lockstep continuation/recovery/snapshot adapters, and the Grid example. Read the repository orientation material and the reusable-layers, deterministic-simulation and host-mesh-lockstep PASM material relevant to these decisions. No nested `AGENTS.md` was found under the inspected crates/examples search; `docs/adr` is absent in this checkout.

#### 1. The transfer receiver exposes a second completion operation whose success state cannot occur

**Files and evidence:** `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/transfer.rs:331`, `:390`, `:400`, `:405`, `:411`, `:425`; `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/transfer_tests.rs:198`; `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/snapshot_relay.rs:177`; `examples/grid/src/lib.rs:152`.

**Observed implementation and callers.** `accept` buffers validated chunks, then immediately takes the private active reassembly and returns `Accepted::Complete` when its chunk count reaches the declared total. `SnapshotReceiver` and `Reassembly` have private fields, so callers cannot install an already-complete active reassembly. Nevertheless, `finish` takes that same state, checks incompleteness, puts an incomplete transfer back, and carries another successful reassembly path at line 425. Through the public operations, its success path is unreachable: an active transfer is always incomplete, and a completed transfer has already been removed by `accept`.

Both current applications use `accept` as their completion operation. Phoenix's `MeshSnapshotReceiver::accept_chunk` stages the text from `Accepted::Complete`; Grid's `recover` likewise collects that result and refuses when it never arrives. A repository-wide `SnapshotReceiver` usage search and inspection of the raw receiver integration-test locations found only the owner-local dropped-chunk test invoking this receiver's `finish` (as distinct from unrelated Bevy `App::finish` calls). That test expects an `Incomplete` error, then proves that supplying the missing chunk still completes the transfer.

**Why this is excess machinery.** This is a small API and state-space cost, not a major runtime cost. The second method advertises an alternate way to obtain a completed string, requires mutable ownership choreography to produce what is currently only a diagnostic, and obliges readers to reason about a complete-but-still-active state that the implementation prevents. Its documentation says it can force completion, although it can only report incompleteness in reachable states. The deletion test succeeds for its alternate completion path: removing that path changes no reachable successful transfer.

**Concrete opportunity.** Simplify or retire the alternate completion API around the actual diagnostic need. The existing `progress` and `missing` queries already expose stalled-transfer information (`transfer.rs:308`, `:318`). If compatibility requires retaining `finish`, preserve its observable incomplete-error behavior and remove the impossible success machinery rather than introducing a new persistent completed state merely to make the method succeed. Retain a meaningful test of missing-chunk reporting and later completion.

**Invariants and verification.** Last-chunk acceptance must remain the one point that verifies the whole hash and emits the completed text. Reporting a gap must not discard the in-flight transfer. Keep the existing reordered-delivery, duplicate-conflict, per-chunk corruption, whole-hash corruption and dropped-chunk tests (`transfer_tests.rs:59`, `:346`, `:153`, `:175`, `:198`), plus Grid's restore-continuation tests (`examples/grid/src/grid_tests.rs:35`, `:70`). There is naturally no reachable-success test for `finish`; before changing its public surface, add or retain explicit checks for reporting before any transfer and after completion so those diagnostics remain intentional. No tests were executed in this audit.

**Tradeoff, confidence and limitation.** High confidence in the unreachable success state and the two traced application paths. Low-priority cleanup: retaining the existing signature as a compatibility shim may be preferable to breaking downstream users. Repository searches cannot establish the absence of consumers outside this repository, so unconditional public API removal is not justified solely by this audit.

#### 2. The UTF-8 splitter handles a hypothetical chunk budget forbidden by its own constant and test invariant

**Files and evidence:** `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/transfer.rs:71`, `:216`, `:223`, `:230`, `:233`, `:344`; `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/transfer_tests.rs:127`, `:373`.

**Observed implementation and callers.** `chunk` has a fixed 32 KiB chunk size, not a caller-supplied size parameter. After moving an end position backward to a character boundary, it carries a fallback for `end == start`: move forward to include a whole codepoint even if doing so exceeds the chunk budget. The accompanying comment expressly says a budget below four bytes is not shipped. The bounds test additionally contains a compile-time assertion that `SNAPSHOT_CHUNK_BYTES >= 4`.

Starting from a UTF-8 character boundary, a nonempty valid Rust string always contains a complete next codepoint within four bytes. Consequently, with the shipped budget, backing up from `hard_end` cannot land at `start`. Neither Phoenix nor Grid can configure a smaller budget; both call this same fixed-budget function. Moreover, the receiver rejects an over-budget chunk at line 344, so the fallback would not establish an interoperable small-budget mode even if the constant were reduced.

**Why this is excess machinery.** This is a very small speculative fallback, with no current application behavior or supported configuration to justify it. It makes the bound appear negotiable and requires reasoning about an oversize emission path alongside an otherwise hard receiver bound. There is no asserted measurable performance problem; the cost is unnecessary maintained branching and a misleading future-configuration promise.

**Concrete opportunity.** Remove the unreachable fallback and state the minimum-budget invariant next to the production constant, preferably as a compile-time assertion if future edits are a concern. Retain the ordinary backward boundary adjustment: multibyte text really is supported and that logic is necessary. Do not add a configurable budget or alternative encoding to justify this branch.

**Invariants and verification.** Preserve byte-for-byte reconstruction, per-chunk maximum size, sequence numbering, checksums and the one empty chunk for empty input. The existing multibyte test at `transfer_tests.rs:127`, size-bound test at `:102`, and empty-body test at `:75` cover the relevant behavior. A boundary case placing a four-byte codepoint across the hard split is useful if the existing multibyte fixture does not exercise that exact placement; this audit did not establish exhaustive boundary-position coverage. Native and WASM must receive identical chunk text and hashes.

**Tradeoff, confidence and limitation.** High confidence that the branch is unreachable with the declared constant; very low severity. The change removes only a handful of lines and should be bundled with nearby transfer maintenance, not turned into a separate framework or major refactor. Supporting a sub-four-byte budget later would require a deliberate wire-contract change anyway.

#### Machinery examined and justified by current requirements

- **The generic command queue is exercised by two different games.** Phoenix wraps `CommandQueue<PendingCommand>` in `crates/phoenix-sim-session/src/command_admission/log.rs:327`; Grid uses `CommandQueue<OrderedMove>` in `examples/grid/src/lib.rs:36`. The runtime owns only canonical `(tick, origin, sequence)` ordering and delayed draining (`commands.rs:6`, `:45`, `:61`); admission and application stay with the games. Removing the queue abstraction would duplicate an invariant that Grid's order-sensitive movement test actually exercises (`grid_tests.rs:4`) and Phoenix must share across peers. The generic payload is not speculative abstraction.
- **Lockstep membership and recovery operations represent distinct transitions.** `session.rs:103` seeds a nonzero activation tick, `:205` records departures to suppress stale observations, and `:229` explicitly re-admits a recovered slot. Their extra state cannot be replaced by a single ready-watermark map without allowing delayed frames to resurrect departed peers. The tests at `session_tests.rs:93` and `:138` cover loss and rejoin behavior. The similarly shaped `watermark_of` and `observed` methods (`session.rs:187`, `:251`) have identical implementations but real callers in owner-loss and recovery paths; collapsing their naming would offer negligible overengineering benefit and public compatibility cost.
- **Recovery planning is not a redundant generic policy layer.** `recovery_plan.rs:189`, `:245`, `:320` calculate the earliest complete fleet disagreement, strict-majority election and a shared future boundary. Phoenix calls the composed decision in `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/recovery.rs:411`. A lowest-slot shortcut would change the rule by allowing a divergent host to become authoritative. `recovery_plan_tests.rs:120` explicitly compares plans from every host's viewpoint, and `:64`/`:80` preserve refusal for split fleets. Grid currently demonstrates digest detection and direct checkpoint restoration, not this majority-election workflow; absence of a second election consumer is not grounds for deleting Phoenix's required policy.
- **Continuation's transaction and status are required by the current owner-loss protocol.** The topology and epoch checks in `continuation.rs:75`, replay gate at `:128`, and acknowledgement/frontier validation at `:142` are used by `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/continuation_systems.rs:149` and `:166`. PASM explicitly requires holding every survivor until authenticated history reconciliation is acknowledged (`pasm/spec/architecture/host-mesh-lockstep.yaml:14`). A generic state-machine rewrite or deletion of retry/epoch checks would need substantially more evidence than type or field similarity. The adapter tests exercise refusal and holding behavior (`continuation_systems_tests.rs:82`, `:94`, `:265`).
- **Chunk framing, integrity checks and compatibility facades survive the deletion test.** Snapshot payloads exceed transport frame limits; bounded reassembly, UTF-8 splitting, duplicate/conflict distinctions and integrity checks serve real transfer paths. The runtime remains independent of Phoenix state serializers and Bevy. One-line re-exports in Phoenix's `lockstep/{session,transfer,recovery_plan,continuation}.rs` preserve existing paths after ownership moved; they are not duplicate algorithms. No recommendation here changes command ordering, simulation schedule, admission authority, snapshot fold order or pure-JavaScript phone clients.

#### Coverage limits

This audit inspected the complete runtime production surface, but not every assertion in the large Phoenix integration suites or every browser transport/continuation implementation. Digest code was read, with its run-comparison semantics considered, but no performance profile or complete history-retention analysis was performed. The checks above are source-level evidence, not newly executed native/WASM or network acceptance results. External package consumers were not discoverable from this checkout. No builds, tests, formatting, installs, network research or source changes were performed; only this assigned report was written.


Audit 38/72 · Agent: `/root/audit38_runtime_reuse` · Lens: reuse

### Reuse

Module: `crates/phoenix-runtime/`.

Scope: read-only audit of the reusable runtime at source baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e`, through the documentation-only HEAD identified by the audit instructions. Inspected the crate manifest and all seven implementation modules (`identity`, `commands`, `continuation`, `digest`, `session`, `recovery_plan`, `transfer`), its entry point, command/session tests, recovery test cases and transfer-test coverage. Compared concrete callers in Phoenix command admission, continuation, recovery, slot recovery and GM health, the Grid consumer, simulation compatibility modules, the existing math history helper, and browser continuation framing. Read repository operating guidance, wiki schema/index/layer guidance, domain vocabulary and relevant reusable-layer and lockstep/determinism PASM material. No nested `AGENTS.md` was found under the inspected crate paths; `docs/adr/` does not exist in this checkout.

#### 1. Low-priority cleanup: two public watermark accessors independently implement exactly the same lookup

**Evidence and callers.** `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/session.rs:187` (`watermark_of`) and `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/session.rs:251` (`observed`) both return `self.ready_through.get(&slot).copied()`. Both therefore return the same value for an active remote peer and `None` for the local slot, an unknown slot or a departed slot. The documentation emphasizes different uses, but there is no difference in mechanism or result.

Both names have real callers. The continuation commit adapter reads `watermark_of(previous_owner)` at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/continuation_systems.rs:188`, then supplies it to `validate_commit`; host-loss processing uses it at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/mod.rs:1193`. Recovery's resumed-peer check uses `observed` at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/recovery.rs:660`; slot recovery uses it at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/slot_recovery.rs:443`, and GM health uses it at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_health.rs:744`.

**Why this matters for reuse.** The distinction between loss-frontier decisions and recovery liveness belongs to those callers. The runtime accessor does not enforce or calculate either distinction. Independently spelling the lookup twice gives a maintainer two apparent operations to choose between, and allows later filtering or representation changes to affect only one name. This is a very small maintenance opportunity, not evidence of a substantial duplicate subsystem or current behavioral defect.

**Concrete opportunity.** Make one method a documented forwarding alias of the other. `observed` can call `watermark_of(slot)` while retaining its recovery-oriented documentation and existing API. If a later API cleanup chooses one name, migrate callers together; deletion is not necessary to realize the single-implementation benefit. No new helper or dependency is warranted.

**Invariants and verification.** Preserve the exact optional lookup result, including local/unknown/departed behavior, and preserve rejoin's supplied watermark rather than imposing monotonicity on the accessor. Existing `session_tests.rs:93` and `session_tests.rs:138` cover departure, ignored stale observations, readmission and the resulting `watermark_of` values; `session_tests.rs:39` covers reordered watermarks. The inspected local tests do not directly assert equivalence of the two accessors. For an alias-only change, these existing behavioral cases plus compilation of callers provide the relevant verification; if accessor semantics change, extend those same cases to exercise both names rather than constructing a separate parallel suite. No checks were executed during this audit.

**Tradeoff, confidence and limitation.** High confidence that the implementation is identical; low expected benefit. Keeping both documented names may help readers distinguish caller intent, so forwarding is preferable to broad caller churn merely to remove a tiny duplication. Nothing here justifies changing the continuation or recovery decision rules.

#### 2. Investigated and justified: digest comparison and fleet divergence detection are different operations

`crates/phoenix-runtime/src/digest.rs:167` implements `DigestLedger::first_divergence` over two completed run ledgers. It pairs common sample ticks, skips samples absent from the other ledger, and falls back to comparing final digests. `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/recovery_plan.rs:189` implements `earliest_divergence` over a live fleet. It must gather every member's digest for a candidate checkpoint, stop when the shared view is incomplete, and return the complete slot-to-digest mapping for deterministic election. That operation already reuses `DigestLedger::digest_at` for each peer rather than inventing another ledger representation.

The similar checkpoint loops are not interchangeable. Reusing the completed-run comparator for recovery would permit a partial-fleet conclusion or treat a final digest as a recoverable sampled disagreement. Conversely, forcing complete fleet semantics onto replay diagnostics would stop comparing independent runs with different sample sets. `recovery_plan.rs:245` then uses the full map to elect a strict-majority group and its lowest slot; the ordinary two-ledger comparator has neither that input nor that obligation.

Existing coverage records the distinction: `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/recovery_plan_tests.rs:120` compares plans from every host's perspective, `:175` requires pending when one peer sample is missing, and `:216` rejects a one-sided sample as evidence of disagreement. `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/sim_digest_tests.rs:1066`, `:1089` and `:1116` exercise sampled divergence, final-only divergence and agreement for the reused ledger type. These tests are evidence from inspection, not a fresh passing test result.

No consolidation recommended. Confidence is high for this semantic distinction. A future performance change to sample indexing could benefit both callers through the existing ledger API, but this inspection does not establish a need for a new comparison abstraction.

#### 3. Investigated and justified: runtime transfer and browser continuation chunks do not share a drop-in implementation

`crates/phoenix-runtime/src/transfer.rs:216` chunks application-provided UTF-8 text; `:331` begins the receiver's `accept` implementation. Runtime chunks carry a transfer identity, per-piece CRC, whole-text hash, sequence and total. They can arrive out of order, an identical retransmission is inert, a conflicting duplicate is refused, and a different transfer cannot replace one underway. The byte bound and split points preserve UTF-8 characters.

`packages/session/src/fleet-continuation-wire.js:7` also divides large messages, but its receiver at `:18` is a different protocol: one assembly per reliable ordered authenticated connection, consecutive indices beginning at zero, interleaving refused, JSON envelope validation after assembly, and limits expressed in JavaScript string characters. It does not offer runtime's unordered delivery, independent transfer identity or checksums. It can frame retained continuation messages that themselves contain snapshot traffic; collapsing the two is not simply deleting a repeated string-slicing routine.

The runtime's actual consumers already reuse it. Grid's `examples/grid/src/lib.rs:111` creates recovery chunks and `:152` reassembles them through `SnapshotReceiver`; Phoenix's `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/transfer.rs:2` is a re-export, not another implementation. Application capture and restore remain with the game, so moving those into the chunk helper would introduce a game dependency instead of improving reuse.

`crates/phoenix-runtime/src/transfer_tests.rs:59` exercises reorder-independent reassembly, `:127` tests multibyte boundaries, `:153` and `:175` distinguish piece and whole-record corruption, `:198` covers a missing piece, and `:346` distinguishes conflicting and identical duplicates. Those requirements must survive any future common framing work. This audit inspected the browser implementation for compatibility differences, not its full test suite or production transport behavior. No consolidation recommended; confidence is high that the currently available implementations are not substitutes.

#### 4. Existing reuse is already in place for hashing, command ordering and compatibility paths

The crate has only `serde` and the workspace `vellum-digest` dependency (`crates/phoenix-runtime/Cargo.toml:8`). `digest.rs:3` re-exports the existing FNV helper as `hash_bytes`; `transfer.rs:217`, `:257`, `:350` and `:448` invoke the same digest dependency for whole-text hashes and CRCs. `crates/phoenix-content/src/archive.rs:107` also aliases the dependency CRC. Runtime is not maintaining its own alternative CRC or FNV byte loop. There is no reuse gain from making Runtime depend on the game Content package for its CRC alias; that would reverse the declared reusable-layer boundary. The external dependency's source implementation was not audited.

Likewise, `crates/phoenix-sim-session/src/command_admission/log.rs:326` stores `CommandQueue<PendingCommand>`; its origin, sequence, insert and reset methods delegate to runtime. Its private enqueue path at `:368` supplies game-admitted commands and preserves a remote order when one is already present. Grid directly consumes the same generic queue at `examples/grid/src/lib.rs:39`, `:49`, `:61` and `:73`. The extra game wrapper is necessary for authority ownership, ECS routes and logging when commands apply; replacing it with publicly writable generic queue state would weaken Admission. Runtime's BTreeMap already supplies canonical `(tick, CommandOrder)` ordering and replacement of a pending retransmission. Its tests at `commands_tests.rs:4` and `:24` demonstrate the shared order, replacement and run reset behavior.

`crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/session.rs:2`, `continuation.rs:2`, `recovery_plan.rs:2` and `transfer.rs:2`, plus `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/sim_digest.rs:2326`, expose runtime definitions through compatibility paths. These are not duplicate implementations to remove on a reuse audit. Keeping them does not fork the algorithm or types. Confidence is high; no additional extraction is recommended for these inspected seams.

#### 5. Investigated and justified: the existing bounded-history helper cannot replace digest history without changing the retention contract

The repository already has `BoundedRing<T>` at `crates/phoenix-math/src/bounded_history.rs:206`. Its bounded FIFO semantics deliberately evict old values as the capacity is exceeded. Runtime's `DigestLedger` at `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/digest.rs:83` instead retains sampled tick records for completed-run comparison and exposes an explicit recovery cutoff through `forget_through` at `:155`. Its duplicate-most-recent guard and by-tick lookup are not supplied by the ring.

Converting this ledger to a bounded ring for the sake of reusing a history container would silently discard evidence used by first-divergence diagnostics, alter its serialized shape and add a workspace dependency. Introducing a retention policy might be a separate justified performance project, but no equivalent existing helper was found that preserves the current contract. No reuse change recommended. Confidence is high for the inspected helpers; memory/performance measurements were outside scope.

#### Coverage limitations

All production files in the assigned runtime crate were inspected. Cross-crate exploration followed concrete reuse candidates and callers; it was not a complete audit of Simulation, Session, Grid, browser networking or dependency internals. Test bodies were inspected selectively outside the command/session suites, and no build, test, benchmark, formatter, network query or source modification was performed. In particular, continuation's Rust transaction checks were compared with the browser coordinator's requests and the game adapter, but the full browser journal and all failure journeys were not independently verified. Similar phase names on those sides do not by themselves show removable duplication: the browser coordinates authenticated tails while Rust checks the frozen fleet and safe simulation frontier (`continuation.rs:75`, `:142`; `packages/session/src/fleet-owner-continuation.js:31`, `:45`). No recommendation to merge them follows from this audit.


Audit 39/72 · Agent: `/root/audit39_runtime_architecture` · Lens: architecture

### Architecture

Module: Runtime (`crates/phoenix-runtime/`). Audit source: `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, HEAD `2de7d5329fba55e06ad00fbd8ca13f0cb4e8c73c`; audited code baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e` as specified by the audit instructions. Read-only inspection; no builds or tests were run.

Scope and inspected areas: the crate manifest, exports, and all seven implementation modules (`identity`, `commands`, `session`, `digest`, `recovery_plan`, `continuation`, `transfer`); the runtime test inventories and selected test bodies; Grid's complete game implementation and four game tests; Phoenix command queue wrapping, continuation adapter and its tests, snapshot receiver staging, recovery caller references, digest and session compatibility paths. Orientation included AGENTS.md, CONTEXT.md, wiki schema/index/reusable-layers, the architecture skill and LANGUAGE.md, and relevant reusable-layers and host-mesh-lockstep PASM declarations. No nested AGENTS.md appeared in the repository file search, and `docs/adr/` does not exist in this checkout. PASM therefore supplies the relevant recorded architectural constraints.

#### 1. Deepen the continuation Module so its Interface owns transition rules currently implemented by its Phoenix Adapter

**Files.** `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/continuation.rs:40`, `:49`, `:60`, `:128`, `:142`, `:172`; `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/continuation_systems.rs:26`, `:89`, `:140`, `:173`, `:189`; `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/continuation_systems_tests.rs:42`.

**Problem and observed callers.** The runtime Module contains meaningful validation: `begin` checks the survivor topology and epochs; `replayed` refuses a latched failure; `validate_commit` checks the transaction identity, complete acknowledgement set, and loss frontier. But its Interface also exposes every `Continuation` field publicly, exposes mutable `Transaction.replayed`, and provides an unconditional `commit(loss_tick)`. Thus completing a valid transaction is a convention spanning the runtime Implementation and its caller rather than a property enforced by the Module.

The production Phoenix Adapter demonstrably needs that knowledge, rather than merely reading diagnostics:

- `continuation_systems.rs:29` increments `status.generation` directly. Lines 63–66 implement the rule that queueing a request must preserve a latched Refused phase.
- Lines 89–98 inspect both phase and transaction epoch to decide whether the continuation lane is active.
- Lines 150–160 derive and write `status.loss_tick` during replay acknowledgement.
- Lines 173–180 recognize an already-committed transaction by comparing its retained epoch, previous owner, next owner, and separately retained loss tick, then write Committed back into the status. This retry policy is absent from the pure Module.
- Lines 189–212 call `validate_commit`, perform an additional live-roster check and the world-side ownership/departure effects, then call the unguarded `commit`.

A caller using the runtime Interface alone can call `commit` on a default or refused state, or modify `transaction` independently of status. That is an Interface misuse possibility established by inspection, **not a finding that the current production Adapter takes that path**. The existing Adapter carefully supplies the missing protocol.

**Why it matters.** Understanding one continuation transition currently requires following public state across two packages. A change to refusal latching, duplicate acknowledgement handling, or transaction completion can be made in one place while leaving the other inconsistent. The pure Module hides topology arithmetic but leaks enough state-machine knowledge that its Depth is reduced. This is a Locality problem even with one current Adapter; it does not require predicting additional games.

**Solution opportunity.** Consolidate the technical continuation transition rules and retained transaction invariants inside the existing runtime Module. Its caller should supply live observations and apply game-owned effects while the runtime owns whether a transition or retry is valid and what continuation status follows. Limit outside mutation of transaction internals. Keep the actual world ownership transfer, `PendingHostLoss` update, authenticated frame handling, and GM proposal handling in the Phoenix Adapter. This is a proposed deepening of an existing Module, not a proposal for a new generic orchestration framework or a replacement Interface design.

**Benefits and deletion test.** Deleting `Continuation` would push its substantial topology, epoch, acknowledgement, and frontier rules into Phoenix; it earns its place. Conversely, consolidating the pure phase/retry rules now living in the Adapter would remove caller knowledge and place those rules beside their invariants. Leverage comes from testing complete technical transitions through the same Interface a host uses, without constructing a Bevy World for every invalid transition. The benefit is stronger Locality and a smaller conceptual Interface, not a promise of reduced line count.

**Behavior and invariants that must survive.** The initial Begin establishes the hold synchronously; replay and commit must remain after normal mesh Admission (`continuation_systems.rs:23`). Refusal must preserve the hold and cannot be undone by a replay acknowledgement. Old-owner grants must be ingested before successor authority applies. The acknowledged survivor set must match exactly; loss occurs at the recovered owner watermark plus one; a successful transition preserves SimTick, the ship roster, and agreed command delay. Exact commit retries remain inert. Runtime must remain Bevy-free and independent of all Phoenix packages. An implementation must not release a hold before Phoenix confirms that the ownership/departure effects can be applied: mechanically combining validation and mutation without preserving that ordering would make the architecture worse.

These constraints match the bounded owner-continuation transaction in `pasm/spec/architecture/host-mesh-lockstep.yaml:13` and the game-independent runtime role in `pasm/spec/architecture/reusable-layers.yaml:10`. This candidate does not propose revisiting those decisions.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** The current test surface is predominantly the Phoenix Adapter. `continuation_systems_tests.rs:42` covers preserved clock/roster/delay/frontier and a repeated commit; `:76` covers incomplete acknowledgements retaining old authority and hold; `:91` covers invalid roster and premature commit; `:105` checks old grants ingest before commit and successor sequencing; `:243` checks the fixed-frame hold; `:261` checks refusal cannot be blessed by replay; `:278` checks epoch/source ingress. These are valuable integration checks and should remain.

`crates/phoenix-runtime/src/continuation.rs` contains no local test module. A deepened Module would permit direct transition-sequence tests for stale/conflicting Begin, replay before Begin, commit before replay, failed acknowledgement/frontier validation, refused-state persistence, exact retry, and conflicting retry, asserting status and hold behavior through its Interface. Keep the existing Adapter tests to verify that the pure decision and live world effects occur in the correct schedule order. These suggested cases were not executed during this audit.

**Tradeoffs, confidence, and limitations.** Confidence is high that ownership of technical transition rules is split, and moderate that this is worth prioritizing: the present Adapter is explicit and tested. The separate validation/apply/commit sequence has a legitimate purpose because the runtime cannot mutate a Phoenix roster. Encapsulation must preserve that effect sequencing rather than hide it behind callbacks or game dependencies. Grid currently does not use continuation, so there is only one observed continuation Adapter; this audit claims no cross-game savings for that Module and recommends no speculative Adapter abstraction.

#### Existing seams that pass the deletion test

These inspected areas do not warrant an architecture finding merely because they are small or have wrappers:

- **Command ordering.** `commands.rs:7` hides ordered storage, issuer sequence, retransmission replacement, due-tick partitioning, and run reset. Phoenix's `PendingCommands` (`crates/phoenix-sim-session/src/command_admission/log.rs:326`, `:368`, `:423`) adds admitted-command routing and records drained commands in its game log. Grid uses the same queue with `OrderedMove` (`examples/grid/src/lib.rs:49`, `:61`, `:73`). Removing the runtime Module would duplicate real behavior in these two callers; moving Phoenix's command-log semantics into it would introduce game knowledge. `commands_tests.rs:4` and `:24` test ordering and retransmission/reset through the runtime Interface; Grid's `grid_tests.rs:4` tests this together with readiness.
- **Readiness.** `session.rs:93` and `:103` construct zero/nonzero activation sessions; `:169` observes monotonic watermarks; `:256` exposes the readiness predicate. Grid uses this directly while Phoenix integrates it with its fixed-step clock and separate hold reasons. A Bevy Adapter is justified: the pure Module should not own `Time<Fixed>`, scheduling, or Station authority. Runtime tests exercise duplicate/reordered observations, departure and rejoin; Grid's first test demonstrates a withheld tick. No alternative clock or merged simulation branch is recommended.
- **Recovery planning.** `recovery_plan.rs:320` composes shared digest evidence, majority election and deterministic pause arithmetic into `decide`; Phoenix consumes the plan in `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/recovery.rs`. `recovery_plan_tests.rs:120` explicitly computes the same plan from each host's perspective; `:175` checks incomplete evidence defers. The separation from game capture/restore is useful Depth, not an extraction made solely to test a helper. Grid's simpler recovery does not call this majority planner, and is not evidence of a second planner Adapter.
- **Chunk transfer and game restoration.** `transfer.rs:216`, `:272`, and `:331` hide UTF-8-safe chunking, bounded reassembly, checksums, duplicate handling and completion. Grid calls them at `examples/grid/src/lib.rs:111` and `:152`; Phoenix stages completed text in `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/snapshot_relay.rs:120`. Phoenix's additional sender/restore-arm checks and world readiness are game/transport Adapter responsibilities; they must not be erased as duplicate transfer machinery. Runtime transfer tests cover reordering, corruption, missing chunks, limits, foreign transfers and conflicting duplicates (`transfer_tests.rs:38`, `:59`, `:153`, `:198`, `:230`, `:328`, `:346`). Grid tests at `grid_tests.rs:35`, `:53`, and `:70` cover pending-command recovery, damaged recovery preserving the running game, and restored digest agreement.
- **Compatibility and shared vocabulary.** `identity.rs` provides ordered technical host/command identities. The tiny re-exports at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/{session,continuation,recovery_plan,transfer}.rs` deliberately preserve compatibility paths after extraction. Their small Implementation does not establish removable complexity; callers retain those paths while Runtime supplies the actual behavior. `digest.rs` similarly owns a useful comparison Interface while each game owns canonical state bytes.

#### Coverage limitations

This was an architecture audit, not a protocol-correctness or security audit. All Runtime implementation files were inspected, but test bodies were sampled rather than exhaustively reviewed. Phoenix tracing concentrated on direct Rust consumers; browser continuation transport, native bridge plumbing, full recovery/snapshot ingestion, digest-authoritative-state traversal, and every scheduling registration were not exhaustively traced. Grid's core and native test callers were read; browser/native end-to-end execution was not performed. No native/WASM determinism, test pass status, or deployment result is claimed. The single candidate above is grounded in current caller knowledge; potential generic collection optimizations, hostile-input edge cases, and hypothetical future game requirements were not elevated into architecture findings.


Audit 40/72 · Agent: `/root/audit40_runtime_loc` · Lens: loc

### Reducing maintained lines

Module: Runtime (`crates/phoenix-runtime/`). Source inspected in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, HEAD `2de7d5329fba55e06ad00fbd8ca13f0cb4e8c73c`; audit instructions identify `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e` as the unchanged source baseline.

Scope inspected: all eight production Rust modules (`lib`, `identity`, `commands`, `session`, `continuation`, `digest`, `recovery_plan`, `transfer`), Cargo manifest, command/session/recovery-plan tests, and transfer tests (round trips, corruption, bounds, missing chunks and duplicates). Caller tracing covered the Grid checkpoint/recovery implementation, Phoenix command-queue ownership, runtime compatibility re-exports, watermark call sites, and snapshot/recovery adapters. Orientation included `AGENTS.md` (particularly the layering and deterministic input rules), `wiki/SCHEMA.md`, `CONTEXT.md`, `wiki/index.md`, `wiki/concepts/reusable-layers.md`, `pasm/spec/architecture/reusable-layers.yaml`, and the recovery/lockstep portions of `pasm/spec/architecture/host-mesh-lockstep.yaml`. No nested AGENTS file was found for the crate; `docs/adr` is absent. No source edits or execution of tests/builds were performed.

#### 1. Delete the UTF-8 splitter fallback for a chunk size the crate explicitly forbids

**Evidence and callers.** `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/transfer.rs:71` fixes `SNAPSHOT_CHUNK_BYTES` at 32 KiB. The splitter at `transfer.rs:219` starts on a character boundary, advances by that budget (or to the end of the valid string), and backs up at most three bytes to another character boundary. Consequently `end` cannot equal `start` while there is input remaining. Nevertheless `transfer.rs:230`–`238` carries a second forward-scanning branch for the case where one codepoint is larger than the entire budget. `transfer_tests.rs:379`–`383` already contains a compile-time assertion requiring a budget of at least four bytes. There is no caller-provided chunk size. Phoenix calls this same splitter through `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/snapshot_relay.rs:459`; Grid calls it at `examples/grid/src/lib.rs:112`.

**Opportunity.** Remove that unreachable fallback branch and retain the backwards boundary scan. Keep an explicit invariant that the budget fits any UTF-8 codepoint; placing the existing constant assertion alongside the production constant would enforce it in non-test builds as well. This is actual deletion of an unsupported execution path, not replacement by a clever string iterator or relocation of the splitting algorithm.

**Behavior to preserve.** Empty input still produces one empty chunk; no split may cut a character; every chunk remains within the byte budget; sequence numbers, total count, tick, sender and transfer identity remain unchanged; both integrity hashes must continue to cover the exact existing bytes. The receiver currently rejects oversized chunks at `transfer.rs:344`, so retaining a purported sub-four-byte fallback would not establish a valid alternative protocol anyway.

**Verification.** Existing coverage includes `multibyte_codepoints_are_never_split` (`transfer_tests.rs:127`), bounded frame facts (`:102`), empty input (`:75`), and round trips (`:38`, `:59`). Retain all of them. A focused four-byte character placed across the exact chunk boundary would make the maximum-backtracking case explicit; the current mixed Japanese/rocket fixture already exercises multibyte splitting but is not a separately named test of every offset.

**Savings, tradeoff and confidence.** Nine existing fallback/comment lines can be deleted. Allow approximately one short invariant comment and relocation of the existing assertion, so the production reduction is small, roughly 7–9 lines before any new test. High confidence: the branch is unreachable under both the actual constant and the stated minimum-size contract. If configurable tiny budgets become a real requirement later, that is a protocol change needing receiver agreement, not a reason to maintain this path now.

#### 2. Replace the incomplete-fleet bookkeeping with the function's existing `Option` exit

**Evidence and callers.** `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/recovery_plan.rs:189` returns `Option<DivergenceWindow>`. At `:199` it initializes a `complete` flag, then at `:206`–`:220` matches each optional fold, toggles the flag on a missing value, breaks the inner loop, checks the flag, and breaks the outer loop. The function then reaches `None` at `:232`. Nothing after either break needs the partial map or the flag. The production recovery adapter calls `recovery_plan::decide` at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/recovery.rs:411`, and `decide` maps this absence to `RecoveryDecision::Pending` at `recovery_plan.rs:329`.

**Opportunity.** Insert each fold with `digests.insert(slot, fold?);`, keeping the local-versus-peer fold selection as written, and remove the `complete` variable, match and subsequent conditional break. Retain a short comment explaining that the first missing fleet sample defers the whole decision. The key reduction is removal of a flag and two levels of exit plumbing; it does not change the decision model or require a helper abstraction.

**Behavior to preserve.** A missing sample must stop the entire search, not merely skip this checkpoint and examine a later one. That rule is central to every host deriving the same earliest divergence independently of frame arrival. Preserve iteration order, the fleet's exact participant set, last-agreement reporting, majority election, lowest-slot leader selection and saturating boundary arithmetic. Do not combine this with replacing the fleet comparison by `DigestLedger::first_divergence`: that function compares two ledgers' shared samples and final digests, whereas this function requires the entire fleet's sample before deciding.

**Verification.** `recovery_plan_tests.rs:175` asserts that a missing peer sample yields Pending, `:216` rejects treating a one-host-only sample as disagreement, `:158` checks earliest divergence, and `:120` compares the complete plan across all hosts. Retain those tests. A useful additional case would put a gap at an earlier checkpoint and a complete divergent sample later, explicitly pinning that the later sample cannot be selected; the current missing-sample fixture ends at the missing checkpoint. No gates were run during this audit.

**Savings, tradeoff and confidence.** The current flag/match/conditional sequence occupies about 18 lines; a single insertion plus a short explanatory comment removes roughly 14–16 production lines. A new behavioral regression test may offset the raw total, but it would add distinct coverage rather than mirror the replacement syntax. High confidence in equivalence: both exits discard all partial local state and return `None`. This is a small readability improvement and not grounds for redesigning recovery.

#### 3. Simplify the receiver's completion code around its already-enforced private invariant

**Evidence and callers.** `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/transfer.rs:273` stores reassembly privately inside `SnapshotReceiver`. Only `accept` constructs or populates it. `accept` rejects zero totals (`:332`), out-of-range sequences (`:338`), size violations and corrupt payloads before buffering. Its BTreeMap admits at most one value per valid sequence. At `:387`–`:400`, it returns More while fewer than `total` entries exist, otherwise takes the active transfer and reassembles immediately. Therefore a public method boundary never exposes an active *complete* transfer.

Despite that invariant, `finish` (`transfer.rs:411`–`:426`) takes active state out, tests its count, restores it on incompleteness, and has a success/reassembly arm. That success arm is unreachable through the public API: the final `accept` already consumed the transfer, even if the whole hash failed. Separately, private `Reassembly::reassemble` (`:435`–`:448`) walks every index and handles a missing key although its caller guarantees completeness and range validation plus map length proves every key is present.

Phoenix's mesh wrapper owns a `SnapshotReceiver` at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/snapshot_relay.rs:121` and performs capture framing at `:459`; Grid's recovery loop consumes `Accepted::Complete` directly at `examples/grid/src/lib.rs:152`–`:161`. The identified direct `finish` call in the runtime tests is the deliberately incomplete transfer at `transfer_tests.rs:213`. The public API should not be removed merely because the audited production callers use automatic completion.

**Opportunity.** Keep the public `finish(&mut self) -> Result<String, TransferError>` signature for compatibility, but derive and return its reachable `Incomplete` result from the current progress (zero/zero when inactive), without taking and restoring state or maintaining an unreachable successful path. In the private reassembler, concatenate the ordered map's values after an assertion of the private completeness invariant if desired. BTreeMap values are already in sequence order, so the explicit indexed lookup/missing-key branch duplicates what admission and the length check have established. Keep the whole-payload hash check untouched.

**Behavior to preserve.** A diagnostic `finish` must leave an incomplete transfer resumable. Calling it before any input or after automatic completion still reports `Incomplete { received: 0, total: 0 }`. A whole-hash failure currently consumes active state; retain that behavior rather than accidentally keeping a completed bad transfer alive. CRC failures, mismatching transfer identities and conflicting duplicates must keep their existing refusal ordering and state preservation. None of this justifies weakening validation of untrusted chunks; the removable checks are downstream of that validation in private state.

**Verification.** `a_dropped_chunk_is_reported_as_a_gap` (`transfer_tests.rs:198`) both checks `finish` and then supplies the missing chunk successfully. Jumbled arrival (`:59`), whole-hash refusal (`:175`), out-of-range/zero total (`:305`) and duplicate conflict (`:346`) cover the invariant's foundations. Before simplifying, add explicit public-API checks for idle finish, finish after successful automatic completion, and state after whole-hash failure. No test currently shown directly asserts all three boundary states. Preserve existing tests, including independent runtime protocol tests and Grid recovery tests.

**Savings, tradeoff and confidence.** The 16-line `finish` body can become approximately 4–6 lines; the roughly 14-line indexed concatenation/absence branch can become roughly 3–5 lines including an invariant assertion. Estimated production savings are about 19–23 lines, not counting new tests. Confidence is high in the private-state argument, moderate in prioritizing this as maintenance work: an always-error compatibility method can look surprising, so explain its diagnostic role clearly. If future code permits complete state to remain buffered, it must revisit this invariant; leaving a second completion pathway today does not provide coverage of that future behavior. This candidate is optional and lower priority than the simpler first two deletions.

#### Reductions considered and not recommended

- `session.rs:187` (`watermark_of`) and `session.rs:251` (`observed`) perform the same one-line lookup. Both names have real users: host-loss/continuation uses `watermark_of`, while recovery liveness uses `observed` (`crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/recovery.rs:660`, `slot_recovery.rs:443`, and `gm_health.rs:744`). Delegating one method to the other removes no lines; removing a public name forces caller/API churn for a three-line implementation. Preserve the distinction or treat naming cleanup as an explicit API change, not a material LOC opportunity.
- `CommandQueue` (`commands.rs:6`) is an actual shared mechanism used by the Phoenix command log (`crates/phoenix-sim-session/src/command_admission/log.rs:132`) and Grid (`examples/grid/src/lib.rs:5`). Its order key, replacement-on-retransmission behavior, sequence restore guard and run reset are not boilerplate to eliminate. Replacing it with game-owned queues would increase maintained mechanisms and weaken independent tests.
- Runtime compatibility modules under Simulation re-export the actual implementation (`lockstep/session.rs:2`, `transfer.rs:2`, `recovery_plan.rs:2`, `continuation.rs:2`). These are not copied algorithms. No deletion savings are attributed to moving ownership again or removing stable import paths.
- `DigestLedger::first_divergence` and fleet recovery inspect related data but answer different questions, as detailed in finding 2. The former's end-of-run distinction and diagnostic variants must not disappear to save branches. Likewise, readiness's monotone observe, depart, admit and rejoin operations deliberately differ; their similar small guards are not evidence of unnecessary state-machine paths.
- `continuation.rs` retains transaction identity, replay acknowledgement, refusal and committed epoch separately. The simulation adapter reads that state directly (`crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/continuation_systems.rs:149`, `:166`, `:180`); collapsing fields or comparing whole transactions would risk treating the mutable replay flag as part of retry identity. No behavior-preserving deletion was established there.
- Comments and tests account for substantial space in this crate because they explain ordering and recovery safety. They are not included as an independent deletion target. No new dependency, macro framework or generalized state-machine implementation is justified by these small reductions.

#### Coverage limitations

Production runtime bodies and the owner-local test files were inspected; surrounding Phoenix systems were traced at the relevant symbols rather than audited exhaustively. Full snapshot capture/application, mesh authentication and admission, browser continuation journals, deterministic native/WASM execution, and the full Grid and root integration suites remain outside this module audit. Relevant PASM lockstep/recovery sections were read, not every architecture/design slice mentioning recovery. Findings establish source-level opportunities, not tested patches; savings are approximate production-code reductions and deliberately exclude deleting coverage or documentation for a metric.


## 11. Rust Transport

Scope: `crates/phoenix-transport/`


Audit 41/72 · Agent: `/root/audit41_transport_overengineering` · Lens: overengineering

### Overengineering

Module: **Rust Transport** (`crates/phoenix-transport/`). Source inspected in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, against the supplied source baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e` and documentation-only HEAD `2de7d5329fba55e06ad00fbd8ca13f0cb4e8c73c`.

Scope inspected: the manifest and every production file in the crate (`lib`, `transport`, `connections`, `shared_connections`, `socket`, `codec`, `rendezvous`, `relay`, `relay_socket`), its socket tests, Phoenix and Grid protocol adapters, Phoenix native transport composition, relay admission/replacement tests, and protocol parity tests. Orientation included `AGENTS.md`, `CONTEXT.md`, `wiki/SCHEMA.md`, `wiki/index.md`, reusable-layer documentation, networking/codec documentation, and the reusable-layers and rendezvous-transport PASM slices. The repository file search found only the root `AGENTS.md`; `docs/adr` does not exist in this checkout. No tests, builds, network research, or source edits were performed.

#### 1. The handshake boundary decodes an already-decoded payload again

**Evidence and callers.** `crates/phoenix-transport/src/relay.rs:263` decodes an incoming, not-yet-admitted peer's payload into `HandshakeFrame` and checks `kind == JOIN_HANDSHAKE`. It then discards that parsed value and passes the original text to `answer_handshake` at line 267. That private function, whose only call is this branch, decodes the same text again at lines 219–223, extracts `data.stamp`, and asks `P::check_stamp` for the verdict. Both decodes use the concrete codec at `crates/phoenix-transport/src/codec.rs:27`. Phoenix supplies its real build check at `src/native_host/relay_transport.rs:15`; Grid supplies a different stamp policy at `examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:39`.

**Why it matters.** This is a small, demonstrated unnecessary processing boundary: every validly shaped initial handshake gets two JSON parses and two sets of owned decoded strings. More significantly, the answering function appears to accept arbitrary raw payloads and treats decoding failure as an absent stamp, although its only caller has already proved successful parsing. That fallback is unreachable through the current production call. It makes the distinction between malformed input and a valid handshake without a stamp less local than it needs to be. This is not evidence of a material throughput problem: admission happens far less frequently than ordinary game dispatch.

**Change opportunity and deletion test.** Retain the decoded handshake in the ingress branch and carry its stamp into the private answering step. Delete the second decode and its unreachable parse-failure fallback. Keep the codec module and the application's compatibility-policy hook: neither is made unnecessary by this observation. No generic parsing framework or new public interface is needed.

**Invariants to preserve.** Malformed JSON and non-handshake messages from an unadmitted peer remain dropped; a valid `JoinHandshake` with no stamp reaches `check_stamp(None)` and receives the application's refusal; a refused peer cannot later become admitted by sending a new handshake; acceptance is sent on the reliable class before Identify becomes useful. Do not merge transport admission with Session or Station authority.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** `src/native_host/relay_transport_tests.rs:251`, `:277`, and `:305` cover matching, mismatched, and absent stamps; `:323` covers pre-handshake crew traffic, and `:340` covers traffic after refusal. `tests/native_relay_protocol.rs:151` pins handshake spellings across languages. For this cleanup, verify malformed JSON and a valid non-`JoinHandshake` envelope carrying a valid stamp both still produce no admission. Assert observable output/admission, not decoder call counts. Tests were inspected, not executed.

**Tradeoffs, confidence, limitations.** High confidence in the redundant parsing; low priority and small benefit. Carrying the parsed data slightly changes a private function's contract but removes a responsibility it does not need. No line-savings or runtime-speed claim is made.

#### 2. Relay admission uses two independently writable flags for one three-state decision

**Evidence and callers.** `RelayPeer` declares `admitted` and `refused` at `crates/phoenix-transport/src/relay.rs:53–59`. New peers initialise both false at `:393`; a compatibility verdict writes both at `:233–234`; identity refusal writes both again at `:325–326`. The live meanings are awaiting handshake, admitted, and refused. The fourth representable combination, admitted-and-refused, has no legitimate meaning. Consumers reconstruct the decision differently: ingress first checks refusal and then admission at `:255–258`, while `relayed_peers` at `:187` and `audience` at `:511` examine admission alone.

**Why it matters.** The current writes are consistent; this is not a reported admission bypass. The unnecessary machinery is the maintenance obligation to update two fields together and remember their relationship at multiple readers. A missed write in a future refusal path could produce contradictory diagnostics/egress and ingress judgments. The present cost is small but concrete: repeated paired assignments and an implicit invariant that readers cannot obtain from the field types.

**Change opportunity and deletion test.** Represent the private peer status with one explicit three-way value and make each existing reader test the state it needs. Delete the redundant flag and the paired-write invariant. Keep it local to `RelayPeer`; do not introduce a general state-machine framework, configurable transitions, or a new shared lifecycle abstraction. This is an optional cleanup when admission is next touched, not a reason to rewrite the transport.

**Invariants to preserve.** Awaiting-handshake and refused are different: the former may be accepted and the latter is terminal for that physical peer. Admitted is still not identified: registry binding remains a separate prerequisite for sender identity and audience membership. Preserve incarnation-tagged pending drops (`relay.rs:531`) so a delayed close cannot evict a replacement; removing those tags is not part of simplifying admission state.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** The handshake tests identified above exercise the three states. `src/native_host/relay_transport_tests.rs:365` and `:508` pin the additional Identify gate. `:710` and `:982` cover reserved, invalid, and changed identities, and `:913` covers cross-leg replacement. Those observable tests should survive unchanged. A useful added transcript would retry a valid handshake after a refusal and then attempt Identify and dispatch, proving refusal stays terminal on both ingress and egress. The inspected refusal test follows refusal with Identify, rather than a valid handshake retry.

**Tradeoffs, confidence, limitations.** Medium confidence that this improves maintenance enough to be worth doing; high confidence in the three-state analysis. A small enum may occupy as many source lines as the two booleans, so this is reduction of independently maintained state rather than a LOC claim. Keeping the current private flags is defensible while their limited writers remain unchanged. There is no demonstrated runtime defect here.

#### Required machinery that passed the deletion test

The following candidates were considered and are **not** recommended for removal:

- **`Profile`, `RelayProtocol`, and `IdentityPolicy`.** These are backed by two actual game consumers, not a hypothetical extension. Phoenix binds `ClientMessage`, `ServerMessage`, delivery stamps, and reserved identities (`src/native_host/transport.rs:40`, `src/native_host/relay_transport.rs:12`, `crates/phoenix-sim-session/src/session_connections.rs:4`). Grid binds its own Input/Output, `grid/1` compatibility, and `OpaqueIdentity` (`examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:31–62`). Removing the policy boundary would either put Phoenix rules back into a reusable crate or copy the relay into Grid. The reusable-layer PASM slice explicitly forbids that dependency reversal.
- **Paired transport and shared connection ownership.** `crates/phoenix-transport/src/transport.rs:60–98` has one small ordered composition, not a transport plugin framework. The native host actually folds multiple legs into one link at `src/bin/phoenix_host.rs:977–990`. The shared registry prevents the same logical token from receiving twice or being disconnected by an old physical connection. `src/native_host/relay_transport_tests.rs:913`, `:1042`, and `:1112` exercise cross-leg ownership, superseded panes, and the shared browser transcript; `src/native_host/transport_tests.rs:101` exercises three-leg composition. Separate binding and owner indexes in `connections.rs:64–65` retain stale physical identities while selecting only the current owner. Collapsing them into a simple token map would lose behavior.
- **`RelaySocket` and the socket supervisor.** The narrow trait in `crates/phoenix-transport/src/socket.rs:7` has a real remote implementation (`relay_socket.rs`) and a real LAN implementation (`src/native_host/direct_join.rs:1000`), as well as test doubles. The LAN service deliberately reuses the same compatibility/identity/dispatch logic. The supervisor at `relay_socket.rs:238` owns blocking I/O away from frame polling and redials after routine service loss. Replacing this with direct synchronous reads in `Transport::poll`, or deleting reconnect because it looks like infrastructure, would violate actual host behavior. An async runtime would add machinery rather than remove a demonstrated need. Socket URL and backoff tests are present in `relay_socket_tests.rs:4`, `:33`, and `:48`.
- **Notice handles and the separate Bevy resource.** `RelayNotices` at `relay.rs:99` supports transport erasure and shared operator reporting; `src/bin/phoenix_host.rs:949` actually merges notices from multiple relay legs. `src/native_host/relay_transport.rs:35` supplies the Bevy resource wrapper outside the game-independent crate. Moving Bevy into the queue or downcasting boxed transports would undo useful separation. Direct `drain_notices` is used by the concrete Grid host at `examples/grid/src/main.rs:63`, so it is not a redundant API merely because Phoenix uses the shared handle.
- **Wire vocabulary, codec wrappers, and JavaScript counterparts.** The four codec functions are concrete boundary functions, not a speculative codec hierarchy. Flat rendezvous frames, the typed/issued `CodeField`, and separate compatibility envelopes preserve the existing JSON protocol. `tests/native_relay_protocol.rs:38–184` pins revision, verbs, classes, handshake names, advertised limits, and endpoints against the JavaScript service. The pure-JS phone requirement precludes replacing browser modules with the Rust crate. The flat-frame documentation's claim that an enum necessarily breaks on every additive field is too broad, but does not by itself establish that replacing the current working representation pays for a new codec migration.
- **Delivery classes, service-provided limits, and deferred drop records.** `relay.rs:577–650` distinguishes lossy snapshots from reliable messages that must either be delivered or terminate their link. `RelayLimits` accepts authored service values with fallback defaults (`rendezvous.rs:80`). These are current protocol constraints, not optional tuning introduced for future users. `src/native_host/relay_transport_tests.rs:447`, `:476`, `:523`, and `:829` cover the distinction. Removing either class or treating reliable overflow as ordinary shedding would invalidate simulation assumptions.

#### Coverage limits

All crate production files were inspected, but this was not an exhaustive concurrency or security audit. Native LAN/socket consumers and browser protocol peers were traced for ownership and wire requirements; the entire direct-join service, every browser reconnect path, native fleet adapters, and every host integration test were not reviewed line by line. Socket tests were read; real TLS, service eviction, thread shutdown races, and backlog behavior were not exercised. The report does not claim those paths are defect-free. It identifies only two local, low-priority simplifications and finds no source-grounded case for deleting the crate's principal reusable abstractions.

#### 1. Small local opportunity: share handshake transmission and retain the already decoded handshake

**Evidence and callers.** `crates/phoenix-transport/src/relay.rs:263` decodes a handshake to check `JOIN_HANDSHAKE`, discards the decoded value, and calls `answer_handshake` with the original text at line 267. That private method decodes the same payload again at lines 219–223 to obtain the stamp. The codec already returns the complete `HandshakeFrame` (`crates/phoenix-transport/src/codec.rs:25`); there is no compatibility conversion between the two reads.

The same area has two implementations of transmitting a handshake result. `answer_handshake` encodes a verdict, sends it using `CLASS_RELIABLE`, and records an encoding fault at `relay.rs:242`. `refuse_peer` does the same at `relay.rs:318`, after constructing a `HandshakeFrame::refused`. The fault prose differs (join verdict versus refusal), but the encoding, envelope and delivery policy are identical. Both already reuse `send_payload` at `relay.rs:208`, which in turn uses `RendezvousFrame::relay`; the remaining repetition is immediately above that shared layer.

`on_game_payload` is reached from the `relay` frame branch at `relay.rs:399`. Its compatibility path is exercised through the same generic `RelayTransport` by Phoenix (`src/native_host/relay_transport.rs:10`, `:12`) and Grid (`examples/grid/src/main.rs:29`, `examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:36`). This is therefore local repetition inside the existing shared implementation, not a reason to merge the applications' distinct protocol policies.

**Concrete opportunity.** Retain the decoded frame after the kind check and pass its stamp or decoded data to the private answering method. For result transmission, a small private helper can use the existing `encode_handshake_frame` and `send_payload` once, with context for the current fault wording if preserving that wording matters. Keep compatibility and identity refusal state transitions at their existing callers. This is a small cleanup, not a new public transport abstraction or a new dependency.

**Behavior that must survive.** The caller must still verify the kind before consulting application compatibility policy; a syntactically valid `Identify` or `JoinAccepted` must not be treated as a client handshake. Missing stamps must continue to reach `P::check_stamp` as `None`, malformed input must remain discarded, and pre-admission game payloads must never be buffered for later execution. Results must remain reliable transport-plane payloads. In particular, do not implement compatibility refusal by simply calling the whole existing `refuse_peer`: `answer_handshake` marks a peer refused, whereas `refuse_peer` additionally schedules a connection-specific pending drop at `relay.rs:324`. The encoding operation is common; their lifecycle behavior is not identical.

**Existing verification and gap.** `src/native_host/relay_transport_tests.rs:251`, `:277`, `:305`, `:323` and `:340` cover acceptance, compatibility mismatch, missing stamp, early game traffic and traffic after refusal. Identity refusal has separate coverage at `:710` and `:982`. Those assertions should remain behaviorally unchanged. A focused malformed-handshake/non-handshake-frame case should accompany any change that moves the kind check; the inspected early-traffic test sends `Identify`, rather than systematically covering the other handshake kinds. No test counting parser calls is warranted: parsing once is an implementation simplification, not a public promise.

**Tradeoff, confidence and limitations.** High confidence that the parse is repeated and the transmission blocks duplicate the same operation. Low impact: compatibility handshakes are infrequent, the encoding wrapper is short, and an extra helper is worthwhile only if it makes the code easier to follow. There is no evidence of a material performance problem or externally observable defect here. If editing this area for another reason, this is a reasonable local cleanup; it does not justify a broad refactor.

#### Existing reuse and suspected duplication that should remain

- **Physical ownership is already centralized.** `crates/phoenix-transport/src/connections.rs:108` owns bind/replacement, `:137` supplies only the current authenticated sender, `:153` closes only the current owner, and `:164` selects recipients. The Phoenix Session facade at `crates/phoenix-sim-session/src/session_connections.rs:3` re-exports the shared types and supplies `PhoenixIdentity`; it does not reimplement the registry. The browser Rust adapter also stores this same registry (`crates/phoenix-simulation/src/session_connections/browser.rs:14`). This is intentional application policy injection, not a duplicate implementation to delete. Cross-leg replacement, stale input/close and browser ownership transcript tests exist at `src/native_host/relay_transport_tests.rs:913`, `:1042` and `:1112`.

- **Phoenix and Grid reuse the relay implementation rather than copy it.** The Phoenix adapter at `src/native_host/relay_transport.rs:12` supplies build policy, application encoding and identity extraction, while its transport is a type alias to the crate. Grid supplies its own `RelayProtocol` at `examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:36`. Likewise, `src/native_host/transport.rs:37` binds the generic transport profile to Phoenix messages. Moving Phoenix admission or Station policy into the reusable crate would reverse the declared dependency boundary. The application-side `RelayNotices` wrapper at `src/native_host/relay_transport.rs:35` adds the Bevy resource trait; the crate's queue at `crates/phoenix-transport/src/relay.rs:96` is independent of Bevy. That wrapper is purposeful adaptation.

- **Host and fleet socket paths already share the implementation.** `crates/phoenix-transport/src/relay_socket.rs:107` and `:112` select endpoint paths through one `socket_url`; `connect` and `connect_join` at `:145` and `:151` both use `connect_url`. Native fleet calls that implementation at `src/native_host/host_lobby/fleet.rs:713`. There is no second socket supervisor here to consolidate. Its concrete socket module is selected only with the `native` feature on non-WASM targets (`crates/phoenix-transport/src/lib.rs:10`, `Cargo.toml:9`, `:15`).

- **The native pump is not a replacement WebSocket implementation.** It calls tungstenite for request construction and handshake (`relay_socket.rs:197`), message send/read (`:329`, `:352`) and close. Its own code handles the std-thread-to-frame-loop seam, reconnect lifecycle and queued-byte signal. The direct-join queue inspected at `src/native_host/direct_join.rs:736` is not an interchangeable existing queue: it separates reliable and snapshot frames, sheds older snapshots, measures sustained reliable pressure and drains reliable first (`:768`, `:827`). `WsRelaySocket` instead moves already encoded service frames through a supervisor. Similar byte counters do not establish equivalent semantics, and moving the application-host queue into transport solely to deduplicate those counters is not supported by this review.

- **JSON and frame structures already use existing dependencies.** `crates/phoenix-transport/src/codec.rs:3` through `:28` are typed `serde_json` calls, not a handwritten JSON codec; `rendezvous.rs:136` uses serde's flat, optional-field frame shape and `:112` uses its untagged enum for the two real `code` shapes. Application codec re-exports at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/core/codec.rs:393` are compatibility paths. Deleting them merely because their names also appear in the transport crate would break callers rather than remove duplicate behavior.

- **Rust/JavaScript counterparts are necessary under the current targets.** `crates/phoenix-transport/src/rendezvous.rs:37` and the browser/service protocol modules speak the same wire vocabulary in different runtimes. Pure-JavaScript phones cannot import this Rust implementation. `tests/native_relay_protocol.rs:35` pins constants and subsequent tests pin verbs, error reasons, classes and handshake names. These are deliberate cross-language checks, not evidence that one implementation should be deleted. The tests explicitly distinguish textual compatibility from live semantics at `tests/native_relay_protocol.rs:11`.

- **Do not assume similarly documented URL/backoff code is identical.** Native URL construction uses bounded string parsing at `relay_socket.rs:116`; browser `socketUrl` uses the platform `URL` constructor at `packages/transport/src/rendezvous-transport.js:307`. Rust's backoff caps at 60 seconds (`relay_socket.rs:222`), while browser `nextBackoffDelay` defaults to 30 seconds (`packages/transport/src/connection-manager.js:1`) and host service retry supplies only the 1-second initial value (`packages/transport/src/rendezvous-transport.js:1051`). These actual differences invalidate a mechanical replacement based on the native comment's claim of the same ladder. Existing native tests pin the minute ceiling (`relay_socket_tests.rs:34`). This review does not establish that the different ceilings are a defect; neither JavaScript function is directly reusable from the native crate. No speculative URL or retry dependency is recommended.

#### Inspection limits

All production files in the assigned crate were read. The surrounding caller/test inspection was targeted, not an exhaustive audit of `src/native_host/direct_join.rs`, panes, fleet state, browser transport or service registry. The large rendezvous PASM slice was used for transport boundaries and admission requirements, not exhaustively reviewed as a fleet design audit. Dependency source internals were not inspected; no claim is made that tungstenite exposes an equivalent of the full supervisor, byte-accounting or application relay policy. No builds, network connections, test runs or runtime timing measurements were performed. Beyond the small handshake cleanup above, this inspection found no substantiated substantial reinvention of an existing Rust helper within the assigned crate.
