   **Problem and evidence:** `RelaySocket` exposes text frames through `poll`, and separately exposes present connectivity through `is_open` (`socket.rs:10,27`). Its documentation describes false as occurring once the link is gone; the real adapter reconnects and makes the value true again. The supervisor writes true on entering a newly connected pump, then false when the pump ends (`relay_socket.rs:255-265`), drops queued outbound frames and retries on a backoff (`273-291`). The relay module detects socket loss only if a particular invocation sees false (`relay.rs:540-552`). That observation triggers the necessary implementation work: closing current connection incarnations, clearing the issued code and clearing `registered` (`478-484`). A subsequent `ready` is ignored if `registered` remains true (`354-363`).

   The interface therefore carries an undocumented temporal obligation: the application must poll at least once during every disconnected interval. Grid normally polls after a 100 ms sleep (`examples/grid/src/main.rs:76`), and Phoenix normally polls per frame, but neither is a guarantee against a suspended process, debugger pause or long frame. **Hypothesis, not an executed reproduction:** a socket can die and reconnect while the caller is not polling; on resumption the caller sees true, retains the old registration/peer state, and ignores the new connection's `ready`. This follows from the inspected branches, but the exact visible failure depends on which additional frames the remote endpoint emits. The inbound channel also survives reconnection and is not purged or labelled by connection generation (`relay_socket.rs:162,243,349-350,369-382`), so deciding which old frames are still meaningful is another lifecycle fact missing from the seam.

   **Solution:** concentrate connection-incarnation transitions and frame ownership in the socket module's observable lifecycle contract. A caller must be able to learn that the physical connection changed even after missing the entire disconnected interval, and old-connection frames must have an explicit disposal/ordering rule. Keep relay registration and compatibility policy in the relay module, but make their triggering independent of polling frequency. This is a deepening direction, not a proposed replacement interface.

   **Benefits:** more depth at the socket seam removes timing knowledge from every relay consumer. Locality improves because socket loss, frame provenance and reconnection are verified together rather than inferred across a thread and a frame-loop boolean. Leverage accrues to both Phoenix and Grid without adding game policy to transport.

   **Deletion test:** deleting the socket seam would move threading, queueing and redial complexity into both consumers; it earns its keep. The opportunity is to strengthen the existing seam, not remove it or introduce an async runtime.

   **Invariants to preserve:** nonblocking application polling; synchronous first-dial failure for invalid startup configuration; bounded retry cadence; no replay of old-peer outbound messages onto a new record; one Session departure only for a current connection owner; a new registration after actual socket replacement; ordinary duplicate `ready` on the same connection must remain idempotent. Neither game commands nor authoritative simulation state should acquire transport generations.

   **Tests and missing verification:** `src/native_host/relay_transport_tests.rs:198` checks duplicate-ready suppression and `:877` checks a permanently dead fake socket produces one departure. `crates/phoenix-transport/src/relay_socket_tests.rs:34` checks backoff arithmetic. Those are useful, but do not exercise down-and-up between application polls or retained inbound data across a connection generation. `tests/native_relay_live.rs:279-281` is an explicitly ignored real-service test; Grid smoke at `tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs:57-65` reloads a client, which is different from replacing the host's service socket. Add deterministic lifecycle tests through the socket/relay interface for missed downtime and stale queued frames, plus a focused adapter test for supervisor event ordering; do not rely only on a sleep-driven remote test.

   **Tradeoffs, confidence and limitations:** an explicit lifecycle contract adds state and may require adapting the direct in-process socket and test doubles. This is justified by existing automatic redial, not hypothetical transport expansion. High confidence in the hidden timing obligation; medium confidence in the precise end-user consequence until a deterministic reproduction is written. Live scheduling and remote deployment behavior were not tested.

2. **Make the reusable transport interface its own test surface instead of locating most mechanism verification in Phoenix callers.**

   **Files:** `crates/phoenix-transport/src/lib.rs:3`, `crates/phoenix-transport/src/relay.rs:21`, `crates/phoenix-transport/src/connections.rs:78`, `src/native_host/relay_transport.rs:12,46`, `src/native_host/relay_transport_tests.rs:181`, `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/session_connections_tests.rs:4`, `examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:31`.

   **Problem and callers:** production ownership now sits in the reusable crate, but the main relay behavior suite remains attached to the Phoenix adapter (`src/native_host/relay_transport.rs:46-48`). That suite tests substantial reusable behavior: host registration (`relay_transport_tests.rs:182`), duplicate readiness (`198`), routing (`392,411,429`), delivery classes/backpressure (`447,476`), per-request versus terminal failures (`640,685`), stale owner departure (`765`), cross-leg replacement (`913`), and immutable identification (`982`). Generic connection ownership is independently exercised through a simulation compatibility path in `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/session_connections_tests.rs:4-47`. The reusable crate itself has only the socket helper test module attached (`relay_socket.rs:406-408`); its relay and connection implementations have no corresponding package-local suites.

   These tests are not absent and should not be deleted. The architectural friction is locality: a maintainer following the required `cargo test -p <owner>` workflow for transport does not run the bulk of its own behavioral contract, and understanding generic behavior requires traversing Phoenix messages, DeliveryStamp and compatibility facades. Grid already implements the same real seam with different inbound/outbound types and a unit stamp (`examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:31-61`), so the generic policy is demonstrably not Phoenix-only. The missing owner-local verification makes the extracted implementation less independently maintainable than its dependency graph suggests.

   **Solution:** place game-independent mechanism contract tests beside the transport implementation, using a small non-Phoenix test profile and observable socket input/output. Retain Phoenix integration tests where they prove actual DeliveryStamp decisions, reserved-token policy, message decoding, Bevy event delivery, Pane composition, and browser transcript parity. Preserve the independent Grid smoke as consumer-level proof. Move or factor tests according to which behavior they establish, not simply their current filename; this is not a request to copy the entire Phoenix suite or make Grid a dependency of transport.

   **Benefits:** locality aligns ownership, implementation and verification. The existing `RelayProtocol` interface gains leverage as the common test surface for registration, routing, replacement and delivery semantics. A maintainer can validate generic transport changes without first compiling game simulation/presentation dependencies, while Phoenix-specific failures still remain visible at the adapter seam.

   **Deletion test:** deleting the generic profile/relay module would duplicate handshake sequencing, connection ownership and delivery handling in Phoenix and Grid. It is a deep module already, and the test organization should reflect that. Deleting compatibility facades would not produce the benefit; they preserve callers' established paths.

   **Invariants to preserve:** transport stays independent of Phoenix packages and Bevy; identity policy is application-owned; compatibility precedes Identify, which precedes routed commands; replacement owns inbound and outbound routing before old-close processing; reliable messages never become silently lossy; snapshots remain shed-able; no phone-side WASM is introduced. Tests must observe the interface, not assert internal map layouts or private call counts.

   **Existing verification and missing verification:** preserve the comprehensive Phoenix suite cited above, the browser/native ownership transcript at `relay_transport_tests.rs:1112`, Pane supersession at `:1042`, protocol spelling checks in `tests/native_relay_protocol.rs:38-184`, and Grid native/browser smoke at `tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs:40-65`. The missing package-local coverage is especially visible for an alternate identity policy: Phoenix reserves host/AI tokens, while Grid uses `OpaqueIdentity`. Generic tests should show application policy is consulted without importing Phoenix's reserved shapes. Add missed-reconnect coverage described in finding 1 separately; relocating existing tests alone does not prove that case.

   **Tradeoffs, confidence and limitations:** some fixture scaffolding will remain in both generic and application suites because they prove different contracts. Keep that deliberate separation rather than turning tests into another complex shared framework. High confidence in the locality issue from inspected module declarations and test placement. No claim is made that current full-workspace CI omits these tests; the concern is the owner-local test surface, not total absence of coverage. No tests were run.

**Suspected issues that are justified by current requirements**

- `Profile`, `RelayProtocol` and application-owned `IdentityPolicy` are real seams, not speculative extensibility. Phoenix binds `ClientMessage`, `ServerMessage` and DeliveryStamp in `src/native_host/transport.rs:40-44` and `src/native_host/relay_transport.rs:12-33`; Grid binds its own messages and stamp in `examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:31-61`. Removing them fails the deletion test.
- `PairedTransport` is small (`crates/phoenix-transport/src/transport.rs:60-98`) but hides shared connection ownership plus ordered poll/dispatch composition. The cross-leg relay and Pane tests cited above demonstrate why simple independent dispatch to multiple transports is insufficient. Its size is not evidence of unnecessary abstraction. Construction must occur before accepting crew (`relay.rs:518-523`); I found no inspected caller requiring live recomposition, so I do not recommend a new builder/state hierarchy solely to encode that rule.
- `SharedConnections` exposes a mutex-backed registry and `ConnectionLeg` carries a leg identifier (`shared_connections.rs:5-35`). Narrower operations could hide locking, but the inspected evidence does not establish that replacing these with a large forwarding interface would improve depth. Physical adapters legitimately need atomic registry operations; any change should be driven by a demonstrated ordering defect rather than surface style.
- The Rust rendezvous vocabulary and JavaScript vocabulary deliberately describe the same external wire; native Rust and pure-JavaScript phones require both. The transport codec's tiny named functions (`codec.rs:3-32`) enforce the declared serialization seam. No deletion candidate is proposed simply because a function delegates to serde or a facade re-exports an owned type.
- Native socket feature/target gating (`lib.rs:10-11`, `Cargo.toml:8-17`) is necessary for native/WASM reuse. Game command meaning remains in the consumer's codec and identity extractor; the relay implementation should not absorb Station authority or authoritative Admission.

**Coverage limits:** inspected the complete Rust transport production surface, but sampled bodies of the much larger Phoenix relay suite after enumerating its tests. Did not audit the complete direct-join implementation, Ultralight Pane transport, browser transport package, worker registry, fleet continuation/recovery, TLS implementation, operating-system socket behavior or every PASM networking design slice. Those areas are cited only as consumers or existing verification; no claim of exhaustive cross-language/network correctness is made. This read-only audit proposes no changes to simulation ordering, native/WASM determinism, compatibility wire spelling, or independent simulation siblings.

### Reducing maintained lines

Module: Rust Transport (`crates/phoenix-transport/`). Source inspected in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, against source baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e` (the recorded HEAD adds documentation only).

Scope inspected: all ten Rust files in the crate and `Cargo.toml`: connection ownership, shared registry handles, transport composition, envelope codec and types, relay admission/routing/backpressure, native WebSocket supervisor and its sibling tests. The Rust files contain 1,793 physical lines including comments, blanks and tests. Caller and test inspection covered the Phoenix relay adapter, Grid protocol adapter, native transport composition tests, selected native relay tests, the protocol parity test inventory, and the browser ownership documentation. Orientation included `AGENTS.md`, `wiki/SCHEMA.md`, `CONTEXT.md`, `wiki/index.md`, `wiki/concepts/reusable-layers.md`, the networking wiki, and the rendezvous/reusable-layers PASM architecture slices. No nested `AGENTS.md` was found; `docs/adr/` does not exist in this worktree. No code was changed and no validation commands were run.

#### 1. Pass the already-decoded handshake into the admission decision

**Evidence and callers.** `crates/phoenix-transport/src/relay.rs:263` decodes an incoming payload into a `HandshakeFrame`, checks `kind == JOIN_HANDSHAKE`, and then discards the decoded value. Its sole private callee, `answer_handshake` at `relay.rs:219`, immediately decodes the same payload again at lines 220–223 just to obtain `data.stamp`. The call is at line 267; both parses use the same codec. The verdict still belongs to the injected application: Phoenix implements `check_stamp` in `src/native_host/relay_transport.rs:15`, while Grid independently implements it in `examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:39`.

**Opportunity.** Keep the successful decoded frame in `on_game_payload`, check its kind, and give the private answer routine the decoded stamp or handshake. Remove that routine's second decoding and `.ok().and_then(...)` fallback. This deletes redundant work and the second representation-conversion path rather than relocating the codec. Expected production reduction is roughly 3–5 physical lines, depending on the formatting of the retained kind check; it is a small local cleanup, not a substantial size reduction.

**Invariants.** Malformed frames and valid frames of another kind must still be silently ignored before admission. A correctly shaped `JoinHandshake` with an absent stamp must still reach `P::check_stamp` as `None`; it must not become an ignored message. Refused peers must continue to be excluded before this path runs. Keep application-specific compatibility codes, accepted/refused state changes, reliable verdict delivery and notices unchanged. No change to wire shapes, identity ownership, or command admission is needed.

**Verification.** `src/native_host/relay_transport_tests.rs:251`, `:277`, `:305`, `:323` and `:340` already exercise successful admission, wrong builds, missing stamps, pre-handshake traffic and refused-peer traffic. A focused case should also pin malformed handshake JSON and a different handshake kind carrying a valid stamp, because retaining a decoded frame must not accidentally weaken the kind guard. Retain the independent Grid adapter coverage rather than replacing it with Phoenix-only tests.

**Tradeoff/confidence.** High confidence that the second parse is redundant: the caller already established successful decoding of exactly the same immutable string. Low absolute LOC benefit; do this with nearby admission maintenance, not as a standalone abstraction project. Adding a generic decoded-frame wrapper would cost more than it saves.

#### 2. Centralize only the repeated local snapshot-shedding accounting

**Evidence and callers.** `RelayTransport::dispatch` has identical five-line accounting/notice blocks at `crates/phoenix-transport/src/relay.rs:596` and `:639`: add the audience size to `shed_snapshots`, then emit `RelayNotice::Shedding` with the first target and cumulative total. One runs for an oversized snapshot, the other for send-buffer backpressure. These are the same local measurement and are maintained twice. They are reached through ordinary `Transport::dispatch`, including the paired fan-out at `crates/phoenix-transport/src/transport.rs:87`.

**Opportunity.** A small private helper taking the existing recipient slice can own this accounting and notice emission, with one call in each branch. Ten repeated body lines become one approximately seven-line helper and two calls: around 1–3 production lines saved after normal formatting. The stronger benefit is a single definition of the cumulative count and diagnostic peer selection. This is worth doing only if the helper makes the two failure branches easier to read; the raw savings alone do not justify churn.

**Invariants.** Keep the failure branches distinct. Oversize snapshots additionally emit the size `Fault`; congestion-only snapshots do not. Oversized reliable messages must still send `relay-close` to each recipient and record generation-bearing pending departures (`relay.rs:603`–`:622`), whereas reliable backlog traffic must still be sent. Count dropped recipient deliveries, not one broadcast. Preserve the existing threshold comparisons and the early return for an empty audience. Do not fold service-originated `relay-degraded` totals (`relay.rs:443`–`:452`) into the local counter: those measure a different queue and are deliberately independent.

**Verification.** Existing tests include class preservation (`src/native_host/relay_transport_tests.rs:447`), congestion shedding while commands survive (`:476`), service-authored limits (`:523`), service-originated shedding (`:807`), and oversized reliable-link failure (`:829`). The congestion test checks that a shedding notice exists, not its exact count. Before changing the accounting, add or extend coverage for multi-recipient local cumulative totals, oversized snapshots, and the difference in accompanying `Fault` notices. Preserve the service-counter case independently.

**Tradeoff/confidence.** High confidence about semantic duplication; low expected net maintained-line benefit once stronger tests are included. A combined oversize/backlog decision tree is not recommended: it would hide the reliable failure policy to achieve cosmetic compression. This is a conditional cleanup, not an argument for refactoring the whole dispatch method.

#### 3. A struct-level Serde default can replace five identical field annotations on `JoinCode`

**Evidence and callers.** `crates/phoenix-transport/src/rendezvous.rs:58` derives `Default` for `JoinCode`, then applies `#[serde(default)]` separately to each of its five `String` fields at lines 60–69. All five default to the same values the struct's derived `Default` supplies. Issued codes enter through `CodeField::Issued` (`rendezvous.rs:119`) and the relay's `hosted` branch (`relay.rs:370`); they are also consumed by the native live-test client (`tests/native_relay_live.rs:295`).

**Opportunity.** Replace those five identical annotations with one `#[serde(default)]` on `JoinCode`. That is exactly four production lines removed with no new helper, dependency, generator or public interface. Keep the fields, names and derives intact.

**Invariants and verification.** Missing fields must continue to become empty strings, provided fields must retain their exact values, wrong field types must still fail decoding, and serialization must continue to emit the same keys. A small codec regression should compare partial and complete issued-code objects and malformed field types. The inspected operator-code test (`src/native_host/relay_transport_tests.rs:210`) and protocol parity checks do not by themselves establish every partial-object default. Do not expand this mechanically to `RendezvousFrame`: `v` and `type` are currently required when decoding (`rendezvous.rs:140`–`:142`), despite the Rust type deriving `Default`. A blanket default there would change protocol acceptance.

**Tradeoff/confidence.** High confidence, very small opportunity. A container default also means future fields default unless explicitly made required, so retain field-level defaults if the team prefers every wire field's missing-value policy to be locally explicit. Any new test may exceed the four lines saved; coverage should not be sacrificed to claim a net reduction.

#### Inspected apparent duplication that should remain

- **Two registry indexes are not redundant stored state to delete.** `crates/phoenix-transport/src/connections.rs:63`–`:64` retain physical bindings and current logical owners separately. `bind` must reject identity changes and stale re-identification, `sender` must reject stale traffic, and `close` must not disconnect the replacement owner (`:109`, `:133`, `:147`). Simplifying this to one token-to-connection map would lose the old connection's immutable identity history. `src/native_host/relay_transport_tests.rs:765`, `:913`, `:982` and `:1112` pin stale ownership, cross-leg replacement and the shared browser transcript. The checked incarnation increment at `connections.rs:98` must not become wrapping arithmetic for brevity.
- **Pending drops need their generation.** The `(peer, ConnectionId)` entries at `relay.rs:83` and the identity comparison at `:531` protect the current incarnation when dispatch schedules a departure before the next poll. Replacing the tuples with just peer strings would save syntax but weaken a lifecycle invariant.
- **Typed codec functions are an intentional boundary.** All four wrappers in `crates/phoenix-transport/src/codec.rs:3`–`:29` are small, but removing them would spread JSON operations into transport/caller modules, contrary to `AGENTS.md:897`. Rendezvous and handshake functions also distinguish an outer envelope from an inner payload. Their re-exports and Phoenix adapter aliases are compatibility and package-independence scaffolding, not demonstrated dead code.
- **Paired composition has live callers.** The forwarding implementation for `Box<T>` and `PairedTransport` (`transport.rs:44`, `:65`, `:77`) supports dynamic three-leg folding exercised at `src/native_host/transport_tests.rs:100`. Sharing one registry across nested pairs, deterministic first-then-second polling, and preserving delivery class on both dispatches are necessary. Replacing forwarding with macro-generated code would not eliminate the underlying contract and has no demonstrated maintenance benefit here.
- **Native and browser socket code are required counterparts.** `relay_socket.rs:116` performs native URL conversion and `:250` supervises reconnects; the JavaScript side cannot be imported into native Rust. `relay_socket.rs:368` explicitly distinguishes an empty channel from a disconnected channel, updating `open` for the latter. Replacing its receive loop with a terse iterator would lose that distinction unless equivalent code were added elsewhere. Likewise, plain and Rustls timeout access at `:312`–`:317` is target-specific adapter work, not two implementations of transport policy.
- **Independent tests and rationale are not deletion targets.** The socket's 63-line sibling test file preserves URL and backoff contracts. Existing protocol parity tests in `tests/native_relay_protocol.rs:38`–`:184` guard native/browser wire agreement. The longer comments around per-request errors, class-specific failure and stale ownership explain prior failure modes; deleting them to produce a larger numeric saving would not reduce the maintained behavioral surface.

#### Coverage and limitations

All production files inside the assigned crate were read. External callers and tests were followed selectively, not exhaustively: native direct-join service internals, the full pane transport, all browser transport implementations, fleet adapters, complete protocol codec test suites, and live socket failure behavior were not audited in depth. The native socket threading and queue implementation was read but not stress-tested. No tests, builds, network research or formatting were run under the audit instructions. The small estimates above describe production edits only and are not a claim of equivalent net repository savings after regression coverage. #### 1. Remove the unused second epoch representation on GPU uploads and its orphaned equality helper

**Files and observed callers.** `crates/phoenix-platform/src/upload.rs:186` defines `PaneUpload`; its `epoch` field at line 197 is described as preparation for the pane thread. `frame_is_current` at line 339 is simply `epoch == current`. Repository-wide Rust searches find no production caller of that function. Its only executable references are the three assertions in `crates/phoenix-platform/src/upload_tests.rs:325`.

The pane thread now exists, but uses a different, stronger gate. `src/native_host/panes/mirror.rs:187` checks both that the pane still exists and that its current epoch matches. The native host invokes that gate at `src/native_host/panes/ultralight.rs:2314`, discards refused frames with closed/stale attribution, and only afterward constructs `PaneUpload` at line 2328. It nevertheless copies `frame.epoch` into that object at line 2330. Neither extraction nor rendering reads that field: `crates/phoenix-platform/src/upload.rs:383` transfers queued uploads; `upload_pane_frames` at line 400 selects the GPU image by `image`, validates dimensions and buffer layout, and writes pixels. `defer` at line 287 groups uploads by image ID, not epoch.

The one direct read of an upload's epoch is in `crates/phoenix-platform/src/upload_tests.rs:258`, where different epoch values are used as a convenient marker proving that newest-wins deferral retained the second object. This does not establish an epoch-dependent rendering requirement. Frame attribution already retains its own original `SurfaceIdentity.epoch` in the buffer's `FrameTrace` (`crates/phoenix-platform/src/surface_stats.rs:12`, `:379`), independently of `PaneUpload.epoch`.

**Why it matters.** This is small residual machinery, not a major performance problem. The current cost is an extra field every upload constructor must provide, a public helper presenting an apparent generation gate that no live host uses, and documentation/tests suggesting the render seam owns a check actually performed before that seam. The duplicated representation makes future lifecycle changes harder to reason about because a maintainer must determine which epoch is authoritative. No measurable speed improvement is claimed.

**Concrete change opportunity and deletion test.** Remove `PaneUpload.epoch`, `frame_is_current`, their obsolete slice-5 explanation, and the corresponding constructor arguments. Rewrite the newest-wins test to distinguish its frames by bytes or rectangle instead of by an otherwise unused field. Retain the behavioral test; the equality-only helper test can disappear with the unused helper. Deleting this state does not change a production branch or remove any current rejection check. This is actual deletion, without a replacement abstraction.

**Invariants that must survive.** Keep `PaneFrame.epoch`, mirror epochs and `accepts_frame` intact. Closed-pane and previous-generation frames must be refused before binding them to the current image. Keep newly minted image IDs on resize, layout refusal, recycling on every disposal path, and trace identity unchanged. The proposal does not remove generations generally or substitute dimensions for identity.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** `src/native_host/panes/mirror_tests.rs:17` tests previous-generation refusal and `:36` tests closed-pane refusal. `src/native_host/panes/pane_thread_thread_tests.rs:337` covers resize generation changes and publication stopping on close. Platform upload tests at `upload_tests.rs:184`, `:240`, `:268`, `:308`, and `:332` cover layout refusal, newest-wins recycling, full-frame promotion, bounded deferral, and renderer-less draining. Those behaviors remain meaningful after removing the redundant field. An implementation should check the live handoff still passes through the mirror and retain these tests; the equality-only test is not a substitute for that check. No test or hardware run was performed for this audit.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limitations.** High confidence for the checked-in consumers: production never reads the upload epoch or calls the helper. Removing public API can affect uninspected downstream consumers outside this repository, so any external API stability commitment needs checking. Expected benefit is modest maintenance and ownership clarity; this is not justification for redesigning the upload pipeline.

#### Machinery that passes the deletion test under current requirements

These are examined suspicions, not additional removal findings.

- **The bounded frame pool and recycle-on-drop ownership are required.** `crates/phoenix-platform/src/frames.rs:185` returns owned allocations when their final consumer drops them; `:291` replaces the return channel when reconfiguring a pane; `:319` lends existing buffers rather than allocating while starved. The actual worker constructs the pool at `src/native_host/panes/pane_thread.rs:571`. The render queue can retain frames beyond a worker iteration, and a resized surface can have the same byte length as its predecessor. Collapsing this into a reusable single buffer would violate concurrent ownership; reusing the old return channel would let old-generation allocations re-enter the new pool. `src/native_host/panes/pane_thread_thread_tests.rs:393` explicitly covers bounded starvation, recycling, equal-length reconfiguration and late returns after close. Three staging buffers and a cap of four are explicit resource limits, not a generalized cache framework. The configurable count is a small bounded parameter; no material simplification was demonstrated by replacing it with another hardcoded value.

- **The persistent upload path and renderer-less drain solve real lifecycle problems.** `crates/phoenix-platform/src/upload.rs:1` documents why mutating an Image asset each frame recreates GPU resources. The implementation writes dirty rectangles into persistent textures, defers only a bounded number of frames, retains at most one deferred frame per image, and recycles discarded buffers (`:287`, `:400`). `PaneUploadPlugin` at `:349` installs `discard_pane_uploads` when no render sub-app exists. Removing that branch would strand borrowed pool buffers on Contract hosts. The full/partial distinction is read by both deferral promotion and telemetry, unlike the unused epoch identified above. Platform tests exercise these non-GPU guarantees; this audit does not validate GPU timing or pixel correctness on hardware.

- **Surface attribution is an explicit delivered diagnostic requirement.** `pasm/spec/architecture/performance-measurement.yaml:208` requires bounded attribution, original surface identity through frame lifetimes, distinct worker/main denominators, and disclosed unknown dirty regions. `crates/phoenix-platform/src/surface_stats.rs:235` bounds stored raw detail while retaining totals; `:288` closes and transfers a recording; `:379` supplies a non-cloneable trace with one terminal outcome. `src/native_host/panes/surface_stats.rs:55` installs it only when capture is requested, and `:105` closes it without waiting on a stalled worker. Tests at `src/native_host/panes/surface_stats_tests.rs:69`, `:93` and `:105` cover terminal uniqueness, truncation totals and late-event refusal. Removing the observer to shorten the frame types would remove the measurement contract, not merely decoration. The lock and event taxonomy have a present consumer; no evidence here establishes excessive capture overhead.

- **Monitor rematching and separate input state are justified by actual multi-display hosts.** `crates/phoenix-platform/src/monitors.rs:175` orders matching strengths and `:254` preserves identities across roster changes. Real display refresh uses it at `src/native_host/bridge_display.rs:484` and `:2033`. A naive fresh enumeration would change an unchanged monitor's key when its identical twin appears or disappears. The regression cases beginning at `src/native_host/bridge_profile_tests.rs:1244` exercise those transitions. Likewise `FocusRing`, `PointerMotion`, `MouseCapture` and `ContactCaptureMap` in `crates/phoenix-platform/src/input_routing.rs` separate keyboard focus, actual motion and ongoing gestures. The native adapter calls `pointer_follow_focus` at `src/native_host/panes/ultralight.rs:1853`; treating cursor presence as motion would undo keyboard-selected focus. The regression at `src/native_host/input_routing_tests.rs:588` and contact-release coverage at `:831` address real boundaries, not hypothetical reuse.

- **The desktop-global input path is dormant, but deletion is not a demonstrated priority.** `crates/phoenix-platform/src/input_routing.rs:163` has test consumers rather than a current native event caller. The native adapter explicitly documents this at `src/native_host/panes/ultralight.rs:1979`: winit currently supplies window-associated touches, and desktop routing is reserved for a future device-global input backend. This is a limited speculative seam, not a second active routing framework. Its cost is a small transform and placement-origin data shared with the declared profile geometry, with tests for negative desktop origins. Removing it would be reasonable only alongside an explicit decision to narrow that deferred input contract; this audit does not infer that decision from the absence of a current backend.

- **File policies, capture callbacks, feature gates and adapter boundaries have present callers.** `crates/phoenix-platform/src/native_file.rs:8` distinguishes preference temporary ownership from uniquely created Workshop/Grid files. Preference writers include `src/native_host/layout_store.rs:332`; Workshop uses Unique at `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/workshop/provider.rs:1017`; Grid uses it at `examples/grid/src/main.rs:69`. Tests in `native_file_tests.rs:4` and `:44` preserve replacement failure and debris ownership. Capture callbacks similarly preserve caller-specific admission and check timing (`src/native_host/workshop/mod.rs:162`, `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/workshop/provider.rs:825`, and `provider/test_snapshot.rs:81`). The decoder is used for native playback (`src/native_host/audio/decoder.rs:21`) and offline asset admission (`crates/phoenix-content/src/pack_asset_validation.rs:274`). Optional surface/render/SDK/audio features in `crates/phoenix-platform/Cargo.toml:7` avoid forcing those dependencies on native-file or geometry consumers. Host facades and `UltralightHost` preserve game policy outside the reusable crate; collapsing them would violate the declared layer boundary or merely move code. The recording surface enables SDK-free bridge tests, and the concrete driver's lifecycle remains on its owning thread; `src/native_host/panes/pane_thread_thread_tests.rs:163` tests owner-thread use and views-before-runtime destruction.

#### Coverage limits

This was a source-level deletion audit, not an exhaustive behavior review of the native host. External vellum-ultralight internals, OS monitor identity quality, real GPU upload behavior, audio devices, touch hardware and measured performance were not inspected or exercised. Host code was followed where it consumes Platform; the full host lifecycle, every bridge/profile rule, all audio formats and every test body were not audited. The half-raster experiment remains explicitly opt-in with pending human acceptance in PASM (`performance-measurement.yaml:220`); its presence alone is not evidence that it is obsolete. No recommendation here alters simulation ordering, deterministic state, station admission or the pure-JavaScript phone boundary.

### Reuse

Module: `crates/phoenix-platform/`.

Scope: source audit at `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, code baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e` (the supplied later HEAD changes navigation documentation only).
