### Architecture

Module: Maths (`crates/phoenix-math/`). Audit 51/72.

Source inspected: `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, source baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e`; audit instructions identify HEAD `2de7d5329fba55e06ad00fbd8ca13f0cb4e8c73c` as a documentation-only continuation. This is a read-only Explore audit using improve-codebase-architecture and its LANGUAGE.md vocabulary. No builds or tests were run.

Scope inspected: the manifest, public module declarations, all five implementation modules (`simmath`, `composite_rng`, `bounded_history`, `audio_config`, `lod_tune`), their test surfaces and selected test bodies; the actual Helm AI, AI history, GM/trigger history, audio and LOD-tuner consumers; the Clippy policy and native/browser numerical-vector proof. Read the project orientation documents and relevant portions of PASM's reusable-layers and deterministic-simulation slices, and inspected the authored-live-audio declarations. No nested AGENTS.md under the inspected crate or docs/adr files were found. Consumer tracing is selective, not an audit of every numerical expression in the repository.

#### 1. Make the bounded-history module own its invariant across deserialization as well as mutation

**Files.** `crates/phoenix-math/src/bounded_history.rs:42`, `:63`, `:73`, `:85`, `:178`, `:205`, `:239`, `:257`; `crates/phoenix-math/src/bounded_history_tests.rs:16`, `:87`, `:109`, `:135`, `:164`, `:232`; `crates/phoenix-sim-contracts/src/flags.rs:611`, `:703`, `:730`; `crates/phoenix-sim-contracts/src/flags_tests.rs:1152`.

**Problem and observed callers.** The module deliberately hides storage behind private fields, exposing the useful contract that a history contains at most `capacity` values and that zero capacity retains nothing. Normal construction, push and resize uphold that contract. Both `BoundedHistory` and `BoundedRing<T>`, however, derive `Deserialize` directly over `capacity` and `samples`. Deserialization can therefore construct an over-capacity history or a zero-capacity history with retained samples without passing through `trim`.

For example, the serialized field combination `capacity: 2, samples: [10, 20, 30]` is representable by the derived deserializer. The resulting numeric history reports length three, reports full through `len >= capacity`, and reports a net change of 20 rather than the most-recent-two-sample change of 10. Reapplying `set_capacity(2)` does not repair it because equal capacity returns immediately. This is a source-derived example, not a test executed in this audit.

The serialization seam is real: `AiHistory` owns `BTreeMap<HistorySpec, BoundedHistory>` (`flags.rs:703`), its reducer checks fullness before reading extrema/trend (`:611`), and the RON round-trip test restores a policy memory containing a populated history (`flags_tests.rs:1152`). Its ordinary fold (`flags.rs:730`) reauthors capacity and then pushes or clears, which repairs excess length during that fold. That limits the likely runtime impact; it does not make a just-deserialized history satisfy its advertised interface. Other real uses are Helm travel/separation histories (`crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/ship/helm_ai/surfaces.rs:366`, `:387`) and bounded records in GM activity and trigger inspection (`crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_activity.rs:324`, `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/debug/scenario.rs:152`).

**Solution.** Deepen the existing module by making deserialization enforce its capacity invariant. Choose and document whether inconsistent serialized histories are rejected or normalized; for authoritative continuation, explicit rejection avoids silently changing saved state. Keep this decision inside the history module rather than requiring each snapshot/history caller to learn the private storage rules. This is an invariant repair, not a proposal to add a general validation framework or to merge domain-specific history lifecycles.

**Benefits and deletion test.** The current module earns its keep: deleting it would scatter eviction, fullness, resize and trend rules across AI and diagnostic callers. Closing its serialization seam improves depth because callers can trust the same invariant regardless of how a value was constructed. Locality improves by giving one owner responsibility for the bound and one interface test surface for both construction paths.

**Behavior and verification to preserve.** Preserve oldest-first eviction, zero-capacity disabled behavior, unchanged-capacity no-op for valid state, partial-window refusal for `all_at_least` and `net_change`, exact sample order and the current serialized field shape. Tests already cover normal eviction/resize/clear/fullness extensively, and the Contracts RON test establishes valid continuation values. Add tests through the public deserialization seam for excess samples and zero-capacity retained samples, alongside valid empty/partial/full round trips. The numeric and generic versions need equivalent capacity protection, but neither should acquire domain rules about ticks or identity resets.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limitations.** High confidence in the module-level invariant gap; only medium confidence that it warrants near-term work. Existing legitimate serialization produces valid data, and the next fold repairs an overfull AI window. This audit did not establish an externally reachable malformed-save exploit or fully trace all snapshot admission and restore guards. Rejecting malformed historical saves changes acceptance behavior and should be deliberate. This is not a claim that ordinary runs currently exceed their authored window.

#### 2. Give Phoenix audio declarations and payload shaping a game-level owner

**Files.** `crates/phoenix-math/src/lib.rs:17`; `crates/phoenix-math/src/audio_config.rs:28`, `:109`, `:144`, `:178`, `:210`, `:228`, `:262`, `:302`; `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/entities/config.rs:99`, `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/entities/spawner.rs:110`; `crates/phoenix-sim-world/src/world/config.rs:1326`; `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/core/codec.rs:116`; `crates/phoenix-presentation/src/server/audio.rs:179`, `:209`, `:235`; `src/native_host/audio/player.rs:6`, `src/native_host/audio/mod.rs:670`.

**Problem and observed callers.** The Maths module is described as a deterministic numerical foundation with only libm and serde dependencies. Its `audio_config` module owns considerably more than numerical calculations: ship and World TOML sections, forcefield damage-source vocabulary, computer-message severity lookup, red-alert sound declarations, a Host Channel payload, string-discriminated audio cues, and the rule that only the forcefield file crosses to JavaScript. A change to a Phoenix computer-message severity or Host Channel payload thus changes the mathematical foundation.

The callers reveal the ownership mismatch. Gameplay's complete entity parser consumes `ShipAudioConfig`, World's complete parser consumes `WorldAudioConfig`, Simulation's codec consumes `AudioConfigPayload`, and Presentation and the native host consume the same declarations and calculations. These are legitimate shared game dependencies, not accidental callers to remove. The present placement works because the module is Bevy-free, but being Bevy-free does not make ship/World audio vocabulary domain-neutral. The reusable-layers design already provides Model for shared game vocabulary and keeps game behavior above reusable mechanisms (`pasm/spec/architecture/reusable-layers.yaml:8`).

**Solution.** Rehome the shared Phoenix audio declarations and wire vocabulary under the existing game-level ownership structure, retaining compatibility exports where required. Keep payload shaping and audio calculations cohesive with their audio meaning; do not split each tiny function into another module merely to make the package names pure. Determine the precise home during implementation from the established dependency direction, rather than introducing an audio abstraction hierarchy. In particular, do not make Maths import Model, and do not make Gameplay and World import one another or Presentation.

**Benefits and deletion test.** This is an ownership/locality improvement, not a deletion of useful behavior. Deleting `audio_config` outright would redistribute serde spelling, missing-section behavior and payload rules among entity loading, World loading, browser output and native output. The existing module has depth and should survive as a cohesive module; its placement is the issue. Moving its ownership makes the Maths interface more accurately describe the invariants it supplies and puts shared Phoenix audio vocabulary near other shared game declarations. Tests can continue to cross the same pure module interface without a Bevy app or device.

**Behavior and verification to preserve.** Preserve every authored TOML and JSON spelling, `deny_unknown_fields`, required fields within present sections, optional-section silence, severity-specific silence, the forcefield file-only wire projection, the nonpositional computer-message cue, and the ship-relative heading convention. Preserve forcefield threshold inclusivity, the degenerate full-spike rule, linear decay and clamped final volume. Relevant existing tests include `audio_config_tests.rs:39`, `:60`, `:90`, `:115`, `:146`, `:166`, `:199`, `:245`, `:253`, `:275`, `:313`, `:340`, `:348`, `:371`, `:390`, `:436`, `:447`; Presentation also has behavior tests in `crates/phoenix-presentation/src/server/audio_tests.rs`, and the native host has separate asset, combat, computer-message and spatial tests. A move should retain those tests and later verify both independent simulation branches still build with the intended dependency graph. No numerical golden should change.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limitations.** Medium confidence, medium-to-low urgency. There is no observed cyclic dependency or runtime failure: the manifest's dependency restriction is currently satisfied, and the wiki explicitly lists audio configuration among Maths responsibilities. This candidate revisits that placement because real Phoenix schema evolution now reaches the foundation; it does not claim the extraction was invalid. A move has compatibility/import churn and is not worthwhile as file tidying alone. The entire audio lifecycle and all browser/native parity rules were outside this audit, so this report does not recommend moving those runtime systems or redesigning their interfaces.

#### Structures inspected that should remain

- **The thin simmath wrappers are a real policy seam, not unnecessary pass-throughs.** `crates/phoenix-math/src/simmath.rs:1` explains the native/WASM transcendental contract; `:52` onward routes operations through libm. Actual authoritative movement uses the seam (`crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/ship/physics.rs:261`). The deletion test moves platform-library selection back to every caller and weakens the single sanctioned path enforced by `clippy.toml:78`. Small implementation bodies do not imply low leverage here. Exact output tests at `simmath_tests.rs:14`, sin/cos equivalence at `:44`, scaled hypot at `:55`, and the cross-target battery in `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/simmath_vectors.rs:1` / `tests/smoke/simmath-vectors.spec.js:1` already test the actual numerical seam. The battery's dependency probes justify living outside the dependency-restricted Maths crate. No recommendation to pull nalgebra or Bevy into Maths.

- **Composite derivation and mutable simulation RNG answer different questions.** `composite_rng.rs:7` states the distinction; the live orbit transition builds its world/ship/System/transition/occurrence key and takes `signed_choice` at `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/ship/helm_ai/mod.rs:809`. The implementation hides ordered little-endian folding, FNV constants and avalanche details, while the caller retains the identity meaning it owns. Fixtures at `composite_rng_tests.rs:23` pin actual outputs, and `:72` tests order sensitivity. Replacing this with a mutable RNG stream would change the decision contract. The five named fields are intentional interface knowledge, not grounds to introduce a generic hashing strategy or pluggable adapter.

- **Two bounded-storage types are explicitly justified by snapshot compatibility.** `bounded_history.rs:186` explains why `BoundedHistory` preserves its own serialized storage while `BoundedRing<T>` supports nonnumeric records. Shared implementation might be possible with careful serialization work, but duplicate method shapes alone do not establish an architectural defect. Keep numeric reducers off the generic ring; they encode a different interface. Finding 1 does not require merging the types.

- **LOD maths already separates pure decisions from rendering.** `lod_tune.rs:1` exposes image comparison and knee decisions without GPU dependencies; actual callers are `src/bin/tune_lods.rs:729`, `:898`, `:976`, `:1156`, `:1243`. The decreasing range curve and increasing decimation-cost curve intentionally use different bend rules. Tests cover transparency, coverage, straight curves and both elbow shapes (`lod_tune_tests.rs:10`, `:18`, `:31`, `:51`, `:98`, `:108`). Deleting these helpers would put numerical decisions back inside the render driver and reduce test leverage. Returning zero for invalid image lengths is an explicitly documented and tested policy (`lod_tune.rs:43`, `lod_tune_tests.rs:40`); it is not silently treated here as an accidental omission. If future tuning requirements demand distinguishing unmeasurable images, that is a policy change requiring driver-level evidence, not a reason to generalize the current interface now.

Limitations: all Maths implementation modules were inspected, but tests were reviewed selectively and not executed. This report does not claim exhaustive coverage of snapshot admission, every simmath caller, GPU readback validity, generated WASM behavior, audio device handling, or browser playback. Source-level preservation requirements are stated above; none are new test-pass claims.


Audit 52/72 · Agent: `/root/audit52_math_loc` · Lens: loc

### Reducing maintained lines

Module: Maths (`crates/phoenix-math/`). Source inspected in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, against the specified source baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e` and documented documentation-only successor. This is a read-only audit; no builds, tests, or source changes were performed.

Scope inspected: `Cargo.toml`; `src/lib.rs`; all five implementation modules (`audio_config`, `bounded_history`, `composite_rng`, `lod_tune`, `simmath`); and all five corresponding sibling test files. Caller tracing included history reduction in Contracts, trigger-fire and GM activity recording in Simulation, Helm composite-key derivation in Gameplay, numerical calls in Content and Simulation, the LOD tuning binary, the cross-target vector battery, and compatibility exports. Orientation included root `AGENTS.md`, `wiki/SCHEMA.md`, `CONTEXT.md`, `wiki/index.md`, `wiki/concepts/reusable-layers.md`, and the reusable-layers and deterministic-simulation PASM slices. No nested `AGENTS.md` was found under `crates/`; `docs/adr/` was absent.

The opportunities below are deliberately small. This package is already a shared implementation with no Bevy dependency, and removing its boundary or numerical tests would undermine its purpose. Line estimates refer to production source only; none assume deleting documentation or test cases.

#### 1. Replace exact numerical forwarding bodies with explicitly named dependency re-exports, while retaining the sanctioned math facade

**Evidence and callers.** `crates/phoenix-math/src/simmath.rs:53` through `:129` define thirteen functions, each consisting solely of a call to one public `libm` function with identical arguments and return type. For example, `sin` calls `libm::sinf` at `:55`, `atan2` preserves the `(y, x)` order at `:97`, and `hypot` calls `libm::hypotf` at `:103`. There is no validation, conversion, state, or instrumentation inside these wrappers. The public module is exported by `crates/phoenix-math/src/lib.rs:29` and re-exported by `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lib.rs:240`. Real callers include `crates/phoenix-content/src/sound_cues.rs:43`, `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_contact.rs:91`, and `src/native_host/audio/hrtf.rs:29`.

**Change opportunity.** Keep the module and each sanctioned name, but express its exact mapping as a documented re-export, such as `pub use libm::sinf as sin`. Apply the same explicit mapping to the other twelve functions. This removes locally maintained forwarding function bodies without relocating them to another project file or inventing a wrapper-generating macro. It does not remove the facade, broaden the exported API to all of `libm`, or make callers import the dependency directly.

**Countable benefit.** Each current wrapper has four implementation lines (`#[inline]`, signature, delegation, closing brace). A one-line re-export replaces those four while preserving its documentation. Thirteen replacements remove approximately **39 production lines**. This is deletion of mechanical forwarding implementations, not deletion of numerical algorithms. It is correspondingly a modest maintenance improvement rather than a large architectural saving.

**Behavior and invariants.** The same resolved `libm` version and the exact same f32 functions must remain selected. Preserve `sin_cos` as the existing `sincosf` mapping, `ln` as `logf`, and binary argument order. Keep `clippy.toml:78` and its companion prohibited std methods, the facade's domain-specific documentation, all existing import paths, and the package's `libm`/`serde` dependency limit. Never substitute standard-library transcendentals or shorten `hypot` into `sqrt(x*x + y*y)`: that would change overflow and underflow behavior.

**Verification and limitations.** Existing owner-local evidence is `crates/phoenix-math/src/simmath_tests.rs:14` (exact result bits), `:44` (paired versus individual trig), and `:55` (scaled hypot). Broader evidence resides in `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/simmath_vectors.rs:1` and `tests/smoke/simmath-vectors.spec.js`, with mission-level comparison in `tests/cross_target_probe.rs` and `tests/smoke/cross-target-determinism.spec.js`. A future implementation should confirm native and WASM results without re-blessing fixtures, and confirm the public names still accept the same typed function-pointer uses. No test execution is claimed here.

**Tradeoff and confidence.** Confidence is high that ordinary call semantics remain identical. Re-exports expose the underlying function item's identity and rustdoc origin instead of providing a locally defined function item; debugger/profiler names may therefore differ. The inspected callers use numerical calls, not wrapper identity. If maintaining a locally owned function item or an independently adjustable inline boundary is an intentional API requirement, retain the wrappers: their cost is only 39 lines. This recommendation is conditional on that compatibility judgment and does not treat compatibility scaffolding itself as waste.

#### 2. Use the standard iterator reduction to remove manual empty-accumulator handling from numerical history reducers

**Evidence and callers.** `crates/phoenix-math/src/bounded_history.rs:118` and `:128` implement `min` and `max` using an `Option` accumulator. Each fold manually distinguishes its first sample from later samples and wraps every result in `Some`. `crates/phoenix-sim-contracts/src/flags.rs:611` calls these reducers after its own full-window gate; `:704` stores the histories in `AiHistory`. The distinction between empty, partly filled, and full histories is an actual policy input, not expendable helper behavior.

**Change opportunity.** Replace the two manual `fold(None, ...)` bodies with `reduce` over the same copied iterator, keeping the current operand order explicit: `reduce(|acc, v| v.min(acc))` and the corresponding `max` closure. This delegates only the first-item/empty-iterator bookkeeping to the standard iterator implementation. It keeps both named methods and does not introduce a generic reducer abstraction or change storage.

**Countable benefit and classification.** Each six-line fold body becomes one line, for approximately **10 production lines** removed. This is a local simplification that deletes duplicate `Option` bookkeeping; it does not delete the min/max algorithm, consolidate the two reducers, or reduce the number of supported behaviors. It should not be advertised as a major reduction in maintained logic.

**Behavior and invariants.** Empty remains `None`; a one-element history returns that element untouched; samples remain visited oldest first. Preserve `v.min(acc)` and `v.max(acc)` rather than casually switching to an opposite-operand function pointer. Signed zeros and NaN handling make operand and evaluation order worth preserving even where ordinary finite values appear commutative. Leave `all_at_least` and `net_change` fullness semantics unchanged, and do not replace an absent result with a numeric sentinel.

**Existing and missing verification.** `crates/phoenix-math/src/bounded_history_tests.rs:4` asserts empty reducer behavior, `:31` checks eviction affects min/max, and the subsequent maintained-window and trend tests exercise lifecycle semantics. These owner-local tests do not cover NaNs, signed-zero permutations, or infinities. Before changing the reducers, add a small comparison matrix for those cases, checking result presence and bits where meaningful, without weakening existing lifecycle tests. Retaining the existing argument order permits straightforward comparison to the original fold, including a sole NaN and multiple NaNs. This audit has not run that matrix.

**Tradeoff and confidence.** High confidence for the proposed exact-order substitution; low payoff in isolation. The shorter implementation is useful because `Iterator::reduce` directly states the operation being performed, not merely because it occupies fewer lines. An elaborate helper or macro would consume the entire saving and reduce readability.

#### 3. The two LOD knee functions share a removable normalization prelude, but the payoff is small and their selection rules must stay distinct

**Evidence and callers.** `crates/phoenix-math/src/lod_tune.rs:85` through `:99` and `:153` through `:167` repeat the same fifteen-line input-length check, endpoint selection, y-extrema scan, span calculation, and nonpositive-span rejection. Both have real callers: `src/bin/tune_lods.rs:976` uses the increasing-cost rule to select a decimation candidate; `:1243` uses the other rule for a distance boundary. These are distinct decisions made by the same offline tool.

**Change opportunity.** If this code is otherwise being modified, move only that common prelude into a small private helper returning the normalization values or `None`. Keep both public functions and their scoring loops explicit. The unsigned perpendicular-distance calculation at `lod_tune.rs:115` and the below-chord drop calculation at `:178` are not interchangeable. In particular, do not combine them with a boolean switch that encourages changing one formula to look like the other.

**Savings and classification.** The repeated prelude is thirty physical lines across two functions. A shared implementation still needs about the original fifteen lines, a signature/result, and two call sites. A simple tuple-based extraction would therefore save only roughly **5–10 net production lines**; a new struct with accessors could erase the saving. This is true consolidation of one repeated validation/normalization algorithm, but not enough to justify a framework. The estimate is intentionally rough because no replacement was implemented or formatted.

**Behavior and invariants.** Preserve the order of the y scan and use of `f64::min`/`max`, `n < 3`, unequal-length rejection, nonpositive x/y-span rejection, strict `>` comparison for the first winning interior point, and both current scoring expressions. Even though this module is explicitly offline and permits std transcendentals (`lod_tune.rs:24`), numerical edge behavior is still observable by its tool callers. Do not introduce an epsilon, total float ordering, an unconditional finite-input rejection, different multiplication order, or a new default knee as part of a line-count refactor.

**Existing and missing verification.** `crates/phoenix-math/src/lod_tune_tests.rs:45` onward covers too-short inputs, straight lines, ordinary elbows and nonuniform x spacing. Increasing-curve cases additionally cover concavity and a noisy candidate. Missing targeted cases for this extraction include unequal x/y lengths, constant y, repeated/reversed endpoint x values, equal-score interior ties, infinities and NaNs. A future patch should preserve the old outputs across those cases for each public function separately. A no-change comparison is more appropriate than declaring all unusual inputs invalid.

**Tradeoff and confidence.** High confidence that the prelude is duplicated; medium confidence that extracting it improves this small file. The current local repetition lets each algorithm be read completely in one place. Treat this as a low-priority opportunity, especially if the two functions begin accumulating identical fixes, rather than mandatory cleanup for a single-digit line saving.

#### 4. Investigated larger apparent savings that are not justified as behavior-preserving deletions

**Numeric and generic bounded histories.** `crates/phoenix-math/src/bounded_history.rs:43` and `:206` repeat capacity and queue management. This is the largest apparent duplication in the package. However, `:189` explicitly records the snapshot-shape reason for keeping the numeric type's storage independent. The APIs also differ: numeric `last`/`iter` return copied f64 values (`:138`, `:143`), whereas generic variants return references (`:289`, `:294`). The generic `Default` is deliberately hand-written to avoid requiring `T: Default` (`:211`). Real users include Contracts' serialized AI histories, `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/debug/scenario.rs:152` for trigger fires, and `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/gm_activity.rs:324` for GM activity. Deleting one type or aliasing the numeric type directly to the generic ring breaks more than internal duplication.

A private shared storage implementation with delegating numeric methods is a hypothesis, not a proved line-saving recommendation here. It would need to preserve serialized names, field order, snapshot continuation, Debug expectations where relevant, zero-capacity clearing, same-capacity no-op behavior, and the two iterator interfaces. Delegation itself costs lines; custom serialization can cost more than it removes. A transparent wrapper is not sufficient evidence for compatibility with every existing format or type-sensitive consumer. Existing sibling tests independently exercise both rings and must survive. No savings are estimated without a concrete compatibility-preserving design. The current duplication is justified by the stated compatibility requirement.

**Audio configuration and wire models.** `crates/phoenix-math/src/audio_config.rs:178` and `:228` have similar fields, but they are intentionally different boundary models. The forcefield projection at `:311` sends only a filename; envelope parameters remain in Rust. The wire model includes world red-alert configuration, and configuration models use strict unknown-field rejection. `audio_config_tests.rs:275` explicitly guards against envelope leakage; parsing and omission tests begin at `:39`. Replacing the wire payload with serialization of the authored configuration would save lines by changing behavior. Likewise, `AmbientAudio`, `PhaserLoopAudio`, and `ComputerMessageCue` have matching file/volume fields (`audio_config.rs:28`, `:51`, `:130`) but remain separately named public semantic types. Aliasing them would remove type distinctions and change debug/type identity for a very small saving. No such deletion is recommended.

**Composite-key randomness.** `crates/phoenix-math/src/composite_rng.rs:86` and `:113` contain similar FNV byte passes, but one folds fixed-width little-endian fields into an incoming accumulator and finalizes after every field; the other starts from the fixed offset and finalizes one whole name. Helm uses the name and signed-choice mapping at `crates/phoenix-sim-gameplay/src/ship/helm_ai/mod.rs:809`. The pinned fixtures in `composite_rng_tests.rs:23` protect recorded choices. A small byte-fold helper might consolidate the loop but adds its own signature and calls, producing negligible net deletion. Replacing the process with a platform/default hasher, a stateful RNG, or one finalizer over concatenated fields is not behavior preserving. Keep the current implementation and fixtures.

**Tests, documentation, and compatibility exports.** The five sibling test modules cover different contracts and follow the explicit repository layout rule. Combining files changes location, not maintained behavior. Removing rationale comments would not remove logic and would obscure the numerical or serialization constraints that make refactoring hazardous. The exports in `src/lib.rs` and downstream facades allow independent package ownership without forcing callers to move; they are required integration scaffolding, not duplicate numerical implementations.

#### Coverage limits

All implementation and sibling test files inside `crates/phoenix-math/` were read. Outside that crate, caller tracing was selective and does not constitute an exhaustive review of every simulation, audio backend, tuning pipeline, or serialization consumer. The external `libm` implementation was not re-audited. Cross-target vector and mission tests were identified as existing verification surfaces, not executed. No assumption is made that ordinary native tests alone establish WASM equivalence. There are no proposed deletions of tests, fixture goldens, package boundaries, wire projections, or platform-specific implementations.

It also implements its own recursive subtree walk and path-prefix matching. Whole-repository searches for the filename, exported functions and badge class found only this file. The explicit offline editor-module list at `scripts/build-workshop.mjs:15` does not include it. Current Workshop builds a findings surface at `gui/workshop-authoring.js:159`, registers it in the dock at `:294`, reveals it at `:365` and renders attributed status/refusal output at `:431`. The former editor entry is a redirect, not a hidden consumer of the badge layer (`editor.html:1`).

**Observed machinery and current cost.** This is a self-contained feedback subsystem with no caller: record-to-badge translation, severity normalization, duplicate suppression, validation-path matching and two subtree collectors. It also accommodates an old fake DOM in production implementation. `editor/validation-badge.js:89` explains that recursive walking exists because the test shim cannot query validation paths; `:76` manipulates `parentElement.children` as an array using `indexOf` and `splice`. In a real DOM that collection is normally an `HTMLCollection`, so this is not a trustworthy ready-made replacement for the current findings renderer. That compatibility concern is dormant, not an observed user-facing failure, because no current consumer was found.

**Deletion test and concrete opportunity.** Remove this module. It does not need to be generalized, migrated into a shared web component or wrapped in another adapter. There is no observed rendering path whose behavior must be replaced. If a future form needs field badges, that feature should use the current DOM and actual findings contract rather than inherit an unexercised old editor convention.

**Behavior and invariants to preserve.** Keep the active Workshop findings, inspector metadata, correlated action feedback and panel-specific refusal presentation. This finding does not recommend deleting `editor/validation.js` or claiming every older editor helper is dead; that module has separate consumers and the current build explicitly includes it. It also does not recommend removing bookmarks or the supported redirect pages.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** The symbol and filename searches found no remaining checked-in badge-specific test or integration consumer. Before removal, repeat the import/symbol search against the implementation revision and verify that the Workshop distribution still builds and that validation refusals remain visible through current authoring tests. A new test that imports this otherwise unused helper would not prove that users need it. No runtime verification was performed here.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limits.** High confidence for deletion within the checked-in application. The benefit is removal of a misleading alternative feedback API and obsolete shim accommodation; no download-size saving is claimed because the explicit Workshop distribution does not currently copy this file. As with the picker, unknown downstream users importing arbitrary repository files are outside the inspected scope.

#### Suspected complexity that is justified by current consumers or declared behavior

- **Shared edit sessions are substantive coordination, not gratuitous indirection.** `gui/workshop-edit-session.js:5` owns busy lifetime, stale-result rejection, edit landing and focus restoration. Consumers include Entity (`gui/workshop-entity-panel.js:438`), Definitions (`gui/workshop-definitions-panel.js:402`), Composition (`gui/workshop-composition-panel.js:447`) and Presets (`gui/workshop-presets-panel.js:555`). Removing the owner would reintroduce separate versions of a correctness-sensitive async sequence. The explicit refusal and exact-source invariants in `pasm/spec/architecture/workshop-source-handoff.yaml:85` explain the machinery.
- **Asset-job adaptation has two concrete consumers and protects real lifetime boundaries.** `editor/workshop-asset-job.js:6` coordinates serial native transitions, freshness, reviewed candidates and adoption. `editor/workshop-billboard-capture.js:34` and `editor/workshop-lod-generation.js:25` instantiate it. The inspected implementation retains output-specific adapter behavior while the shared owner handles cancellation and stale drafts. This audit found no evidence supporting deletion of its queue or validation gates.
- **Dock-layout history serves real stored generations.** `gui/dock-layout-history.js:9` is instantiated by both `gui/live-layout-model.js:92` and `gui/workshop-layout-model.js:53`. Workshop's version 11 registry and version-one rehoming are not evidence by themselves of speculative generality. `tests/client/workshop-layout-model.test.js:53`, `:191`, `:338`, `:354` and `:365` pin migration, closed-panel preservation and historical-vocabulary rejection. Removing historical support would change existing saved layouts; no retention-policy decision authorizing that was found.
- **The shared console component base is actively used despite its stale introductory comment.** `gui/components/ph-element.js:10` still says nothing extends it, but actual consumers include `gui/components/ph-blasters-controls.js:16`, `ph-battery-bar.js:9`, `ph-civilian-traffic.js:57` and many others. Its live `sendAction` lookup at `ph-element.js:123` addresses initialization order, and state coalescing at `:197` supports different rendering needs. The stale comment is documentation debt, not a reason to delete a used base class.
- **The apparently unused ordinal repair command is not a safe protocol deletion.** `gui/action-map.js:514` explicitly records that current consoles emit the system-target alternative; repository searches confirmed that the ordinal action's visible JavaScript callers are tests. However, `pasm/spec/architecture/engineering-damage.yaml:1050` deliberately retains two distinct payloads: a standing ordinal and a one-shot system pin. `gui/repair-dispatch.js:194` documents that distinction, and `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/console/repair/dispatch.rs:358` still consumes the ordinal command. This audit therefore does not infer that the host payload or admission behavior is redundant, nor recommend deleting it merely to shrink the UI action map.
- **Client reducer/effect routing and pure-JS projections are requirement-driven boundaries.** `gui/client-router.js:79` consumes merged reducer results, not raw host messages; its render guard protects a mid-game lobby interaction and its effect ordering preserves repeated feedback. `gui/sim-state.js:19` imports the shared result vocabulary, while `client.html:106` and `:142` load console projection and simulation state. These are real presentation and lifetime differences, not sufficient evidence for collapsing everything into the HTML entry script. Native/WASM interoperability and phone-client independence must survive any future simplification.

#### Coverage limits

The audit did not exhaustively inspect the large `console-state.js`, `settings-panel.js`, `server-settings.js`, fleet-session/host-mesh adapters, GM workspace and all individual Station components. It did not audit CSS, accessibility behavior across devices, every root HTML page, every localization path, all source parsers, the complete provider request vocabulary, native Rust Workshop internals or runtime resource usage. Provider/edit/job modules were examined for justification of their abstractions, not for a complete security or concurrency review. Repository searches establish checked-in consumers, not external integrations or dynamically generated downstream code. No claim of an exhaustive module-wide absence of overengineering follows from these two narrowly supported findings.


Audit 54/72 · Agent: `/root/audit54_browser_reuse` · Lens: reuse

### Reuse

Module: Browser UI and Workshop (audit 54/72).

Scope: read-only source inspection in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, code baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e`; the supplied current HEAD adds documentation only. Inspected the shared console DOM library, Objective/Deadline/Comms custom elements, Helm input components, Workshop editing/acceptance helpers and representative Ship/Slot forms, browser recovery and source handoff, export/download paths, dock model composition, root page entry wiring and associated tests. Orientation included AGENTS.md, wiki schema/index, domain vocabulary, reusable-layers, Console UI Library, Workshop Authoring, and the editor-authoring and reusable-layers PASM architecture slices. No nested AGENTS.md was returned by the repository file search; `docs/adr` is absent. No builds, tests or browsers were run.

#### 1. Three console list components repeat keyed DOM reconciliation already present in the console library

**Evidence and callers.** `gui/console-ui.js:27` implements `reconcileRows`: remove missing keys, create missing rows, reorder existing nodes and update their content without replacing their identity. `gui/components/ph-objective-list.js:75`, `gui/components/ph-deadline-list.js:74` and `gui/components/ph-comms-contact-list.js:42` independently construct a live-key Set, remove missing Map entries, look up each current row, create/append a missing node and repaint it. Their actual row bodies remain different: Objective buttons emit Captain semantic actions (`ph-objective-list.js:104`), Deadline rows resolve labels and countdown states (`ph-deadline-list.js:101`), and Comms pills emit hail actions and disable out-of-range contacts (`ph-comms-contact-list.js:62`). These are deployed controls, for example `gui/cruiser/captain.html:59`, `gui/courier/captain.html:66` and `gui/dynasty-cruiser/command.html:1` compose them. The searched `gui/` tree contained no production `reconcileRows` caller; its direct callers are currently its tests.

**Why this matters.** The row lifetime algorithm has a tested shared implementation but is reimplemented in several controls. The implementations have already diverged: the shared helper explicitly orders retained nodes to match incoming IDs, whereas these component loops append only when creating a row. A later permutation of the same IDs leaves their DOM in its original order. Whether that permutation is a user-visible defect depends on the payload ordering contract; this audit did not trace every authoritative producer and does not claim a confirmed gameplay defect. It is nevertheless a concrete compatibility difference that a reuse change must resolve deliberately.

**Opportunity.** Reuse the existing keyed reconciliation owner for these components, retaining component-local row construction, update functions, empty placeholders and semantic actions. Adapt its cache representation to safely support the components' Map keys rather than blindly replacing Maps with `{}`: the helper indexes an object directly, and authored IDs such as prototype-property names must not become object entries accidentally. A Map-capable version, or a carefully checked null-prototype cache, can preserve identity without creating a separate list framework. Choose and test ordering explicitly; incoming order should not be introduced accidentally under the guise of a mechanical refactor.

**Invariants and verification.** Preserve the `uuid`-first Comms identity with fixture-compatible `id` fallback; Objective identity, `unassigned` handling, captured click target and roving tabindex; Deadline label fallback and fired/cancelled formatting; stable DOM nodes and listeners; empty-state transitions. `tests/client/console-ui.test.js:56`, `:65`, `:75` and `:84` already cover creation, identity reuse, deletion and reordering. `tests/client/ph-deadline-list.test.js:78` covers in-place updates/removal; the corresponding Objective and Comms test files cover their component behavior. Add component-level same-ID permutation and focus/listener preservation cases, including unusual IDs and empty-to-nonempty transitions. Those tests must exercise the real component adapter, not only the shared helper.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limits.** High confidence in duplicated reconciliation; medium confidence in the value of consolidation because each loop is small and adapting the shared cache/order policy has a cost. Keep domain rendering in each component and do not infer that identical list mechanics justify a schema-generated console. This finding is confined to the three inspected components, not every UI list.

#### 2. Workshop ZIP export repeats the save/profile browser-download mechanism

**Evidence and callers.** `gui/snapshot-transfer.js:104` exports `downloadArtifact`, creating a Blob URL, appending a download anchor, clicking/removing it, and deferring URL revocation with a zero-delay timeout. Settings uses it at `gui/settings-panel.js:63` and `:1060`, and GM confirmation settings imports it at `gui/gm-confirmation-settings.js:4`. `gui/workshop-authoring.js:1113` separately implements the same URL/anchor/timer lifecycle in `downloadZip`, injected as the default download function at `:56` and invoked by the accepted export path at `:595`.

**Why this matters.** This is the same browser mechanism in two active user flows, including the important delayed revocation rule documented by `snapshot-transfer.js` (Chrome consumes the URL asynchronously). Maintenance is already asymmetric: ZIP export uses `try/finally` around the click, while `downloadArtifact` removes the anchor and schedules revocation only after a successful click. Conversely, the snapshot helper guards absent document, empty text and unavailable URL APIs; the ZIP helper expects browser APIs to exist and lets failures reach its caller. Consolidation can carry the deliberate cleanup behavior into both flows without changing their validation owners.

**Opportunity.** Factor the existing URL/anchor cleanup into a shared browser download primitive accepting a prepared Blob (or content plus MIME), and retain the two thin policy adapters. Keep ZIP as exact bytes with `application/zip`; keep saves/profile exports as text with the existing text MIME. Do not send ZIP bytes through the string-only helper unchanged. Keep Workshop's throwing/injected download contract and snapshot's boolean/empty-text behavior at their adapters, rather than silently making callers interpret a different failure result.

**Invariants and verification.** Export must still occur only after Workshop acceptance, and cleanup must be deferred until after the click. File names, Blob MIME, byte fidelity, originating document/window and failure propagation must survive. `tests/client/snapshot-transfer.test.js:100` checks offered content/name and eventual cleanup, while `:125` checks empty-text refusal. `tests/client/workshop-authoring.test.js` injects a download spy, so its export cases verify authoring behavior but do not by themselves prove the default browser helper's cleanup. Add shared-helper cases for binary content and click exceptions, retain the public adapter tests, and use the existing Workshop authoring smoke flow for integration when implementing.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limits.** High confidence that the lifecycle is duplicated, modest expected benefit because this is a small extraction. This is not a recommendation to merge snapshot and Workshop persistence or admission. Image object URLs in `gui/workshop-native-test-document.js:60` have a different replacement/disposal lifetime and are not download duplicates.

#### 3. Workshop recovery and source handoff can share IndexedDB connection opening, but not their transactions

**Evidence and callers.** `editor/workshop-recovery.js:7` and `editor/workshop-handoff.js:45` each define an `open()` closure which checks IndexedDB availability, caches the opening Promise, creates one store in `onupgradeneeded`, closes/resets the connection on version change, resolves the successful database, and clears the cached Promise after failure. Recovery opens `phoenix-workshop-draft` with store `draft`; handoff opens `phoenix-workshop-handoff` with store `source`. Recovery is mounted by `gui/workshop-authoring.js:57`; the handoff store is created by `gui/workshop-source-link.js:53` and consumed on arrival by `gui/workshop-boot.js:24`.

**Why this matters.** The connection lifetime, retry and version-change handling are essentially the same code under different names and error messages. Storage denial, blocked upgrades and connection-reset fixes therefore require synchronized edits. The shared portion is approximately the opening closure, not the entirety of either persistence service.

**Opportunity.** Extract a small connection-opening owner with explicit database/store identity and caller-owned error vocabulary. Reuse that mechanism in these two existing stores. Keep the databases, data shapes, migration ownership and operations separate. In particular, do not replace handoff with recovery's single-request transaction wrapper merely because both expose `save` and `clear`.

**Required compatibility differences.** Recovery's `editor/workshop-recovery.js:33` queue deliberately serializes operations so older snapshots cannot overwrite newer snapshots; its transactions support readonly load and return only after commit. Handoff's `editor/workshop-handoff.js:58` uses a readwrite transaction to read the pending record, check it and write/delete atomically. Its TTL, token comparison, occupied-slot refusal and single-use consumption (`:73` onward) are domain rules that a shared connection helper must not move or weaken. Recovery distinguishes blocked versus failed open errors; handoff groups them into an unavailable-storage error. Preserve those outward results if callers/tests depend on them.

**Verification.** `tests/client/workshop-handoff.test.js:12` tests source-copy isolation and the Live-to-Authoring sequence, mostly through injected store behavior; it is not proof of IndexedDB transaction atomicity. `tests/smoke/workshop-handoff.spec.js:11` and `:49` exercise the real store and consumed-token behavior. `tests/smoke/workshop-authoring.spec.js:110` covers real browser recovery. Add focused storage tests for failed-open retry, version-change close/reopen and blocked opens in both adapters, plus recovery write order and concurrent handoff attempts if changing the transaction boundary at all. Existing smoke coverage should remain independent of shared implementation details.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limits.** High confidence in the repeated open lifecycle; medium confidence in extraction payoff because there are only two small consumers. A general persistence framework would cost more than this duplication. No cross-database merger or shared record schema is justified.

#### Existing reuse and deliberately separate implementations

- `gui/components/ph-helm-joystick.js:13` and `gui/components/ph-lateral-thrust-joystick.js:12` already delegate continuous input to `gui/components/continuous-helm-input.js:2`. Remaining pointer geometry differs (a normalized two-axis circle versus a clamped horizontal track), as does immediate pointer dispatch. Similar connected/render methods are not evidence for collapsing those two controls.
- `gui/workshop-layout-model.js:53` and `gui/live-layout-model.js:92` use `createDockLayoutHistory`; Test uses the shared model at `gui/workshop-test-layout-model.js:26`. Distinct panel inventories and version histories are configuration, not another dock algorithm.
- `editor/workshop-acceptance.js:6` owns captured-candidate freshness and validation, with adoption at `:44`. Ship, Slot, Spatial, model structure, scripts and entity composition already use this owner. The sampled Ship and Slot UI mutation paths also use `runWorkshopMutation` from `gui/workshop-edit-session.js:72`. Their form/domain branches are not redundant acceptance engines.
- `editor.html:1` and `viewer.html:1` are migration pages pointing at the shared redirect script and Workshop. They are not independent old editors to delete. `workshop.html:14` mounts the authoring boot module; `client.html` remains a pure-JavaScript phone entry point. A cross-entry-page abstraction must not import Workshop WASM requirements into phone consoles.

#### Coverage limits

This is a focused reuse audit of a large combined module, not exhaustive coverage. Root `server.html` and `client.html` were inspected for entry-point context only, not their complete inline networking/lobby logic. I did not fully audit GM panels, every hull's HTML/CSS, radar projection/rendering, audio, remapping, private profiles, all localisation flows, archive codecs, every exact-source editor operation, native Workshop Rust adapters, model preview internals, or transport/session package implementations. No duplicate-parser, native/WASM codec or simulation-policy consolidation is proposed from superficial similarity. Test source was inspected; no passing test result or browser behavior is asserted by this report.


Audit 55/72 · Agent: `/root/audit55_browser_architecture` · Lens: architecture

### Architecture

Module: Browser UI and Workshop (combined agreed module), audit 55/72.

Source inspected: `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, code baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e`; the supplied audit instructions identify current HEAD `2de7d5329fba55e06ad00fbd8ca13f0cb4e8c73c` as documentation-only navigation changes. This was a read-only exploration; no builds, tests, browser sessions, or source edits were performed.

Scope and orientation: read the audit instructions, architecture skill and LANGUAGE.md, repository guidance, wiki schema/index, domain vocabulary, reusable-layer navigation, and the Client Architecture and Workshop Authoring pages. Checked for nested AGENTS.md (only the root file was found) and `docs/adr` (directory absent). Consulted PASM `architecture/client-shell.yaml`, `architecture/editor-authoring.yaml`, `architecture/workshop-source-handoff.yaml`, and reusable-layer declarations. The source samples covered root client/server startup, the Workshop and retired editor/viewer entry pages, Console mount and projection modules, Host Channel/audio/chrome composition, Workshop shell/edit-session/acceptance/runtime/provider modules, Composition and Ship panel callers, and relevant client/editor tests. The findings concern these inspected paths, not every file in this large module.

#### 1. Deepen page startup so readiness ordering belongs to one module rather than each extracted feature

**Files:** `server.html:3115`, `server.html:3256`, `server.html:3284`, `server.html:3327`, `server.html:3374`, `server.html:3388`, `server.html:3404`, `server.html:3436`, `server.html:6531`, `server.html:8344`; `client.html:2325`, `client.html:2365`, `client.html:2381`, `client.html:4248`; `gui/page-chrome.js:78`, `gui/host-channel.js:111`; `tests/client/host-channel-localisation.test.js:16`, `tests/client/host-audio.test.js:21`.

**Problem and observed callers.** Feature extraction has produced useful modules, but their composition still exposes a second, page-owned readiness protocol. In the server page, the classic script installs a queue-backed `window.__hostChannel`, later replaced and drained by `__hostChannelReady`. Page chrome separately holds `_pageChrome`, queues method-name/argument pairs, and drains them when its module island calls `__pageChromeReady`. Host audio repeats that lifecycle with `_hostAudio`, `__hostAudioQueue`, and `__hostAudioReady`. The audio island mounts the module, assigns several direct window functions, installs page visibility listeners, and finally releases the latch (`server.html:3256–3284`). A different ICE configuration promise is fulfilled by another module island (`server.html:6531`). Content preload explicitly awaits a dynamic import because Trunk initialization can beat independently loaded scripts (`server.html:8344`).

The phone page has the same chrome readiness handshake, and its connection flow waits for DOMContentLoaded before using module-dependent state (`client.html:4248`). These are actual callers and actual ordering requirements, not abandoned scaffolding. The interface a maintainer must understand therefore includes which functions are safe immediately, which are queue-backed, which globals appear only after deferred modules, and which lifecycle entry points await DOM readiness. That knowledge is distributed between HTML comments, module islands, classic script functions, and feature implementations.

This is a locality problem rather than a complaint that HTML is long. Adding another startup collaborator currently means deciding where to publish it and whether to add another latch or join an existing readiness condition. The internal feature tests cannot establish that those page-level decisions remain correct.

**Solution opportunity.** Make page startup and its readiness/teardown coordination a deeper module, with the existing audio, chrome, localisation and Host Channel modules remaining its collaborators. Keep globals that are required by Rust or other documents as explicit adapters, owned by that composition. Start with the chrome/audio/channel cluster whose repeated coordination is directly visible; do not attempt a wholesale rewrite of networking or the server page. This is a behavioral ownership proposal, not a proposed replacement interface.

**Deletion test, depth, locality and leverage.** Deleting `createHostChannel`, `createHostAudio`, or `mountPageChrome` would put substantial behavior back into callers; those modules earn their keep. Deleting only the latches today would lose legitimate early calls. The opportunity is to concentrate their ordering knowledge under one seam, then remove only the readiness machinery made redundant by that ownership. A deeper startup module would give callers installed capabilities and retain the ordering implementation privately. Its leverage is shared startup verification and fewer independent places to reason about early Host Channel delivery.

**Invariants and compatibility.** Preserve delivery order for queued Host Channel calls; preserve localisation exceptions for typed GM projections; preserve room-audio suppression for GM pages, storage-refusal behavior, page hide/show audio behavior, and wake-lock semantics. Keep the phone page pure JavaScript. Retain all externally consumed window entry points until their actual Rust/native/iframe callers are migrated. This finding does not propose moving host authority or transport policy into presentation, merging browser and native adapters, or delaying startup indiscriminately until every optional feature exists.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** `tests/client/host-channel-localisation.test.js:16` imports the actual dispatcher, with channel-routing and payload-localisation cases beginning at lines 63 and 131. `tests/client/host-audio.test.js:21` exercises output behavior, including reset and non-room behavior later in that file. `tests/client/page-chrome.test.js` covers the mounted chrome module. Those are good tests at each feature's interface. I did not find references to `__hostChannelReady`, `__hostAudioReady`, or `__pageChromeReady` in the searched test tree, so explicit tests of those exact page-owned readiness handshakes were not demonstrated by this inspection. That is not a claim that browser smoke tests never exercise them indirectly. A deepened seam should support delayed collaborator readiness, early queued delivery, repeated startup/cleanup, and failure cases without scraping HTML implementation text. A real browser check remains necessary for classic/module/Trunk scheduling.

**Tradeoffs and confidence.** High confidence that coordination knowledge is distributed; medium confidence in the payoff of extracting the entire startup sequence, because many page globals have other consumers beyond this sample. Converting a classic script to a module changes top-level scoping and is not a mechanical move. Incremental ownership is safer than a generic queue framework, which would merely add another shallow module. No observed race or lost event is claimed; the present queues explicitly defend against those risks. No ADR conflict was found in the inspected material.

#### 2. Deepen Workshop workspace-operation ownership above the existing edit helpers

**Files:** `gui/workshop-authoring.js:182`, `gui/workshop-authoring.js:250`, `gui/workshop-authoring.js:450`, `gui/workshop-authoring.js:513`, `gui/workshop-authoring.js:521`, `gui/workshop-authoring.js:577`, `gui/workshop-authoring.js:666`, `gui/workshop-authoring.js:889`, `gui/workshop-authoring.js:958`; `gui/workshop-edit-session.js:5`, `gui/workshop-edit-session.js:67`; `gui/workshop-composition-panel.js:19`, `gui/workshop-composition-panel.js:447`, `gui/workshop-composition-panel.js:472`; `gui/workshop-ship-authoring-panel.js:164`; `editor/workshop-acceptance.js:6`.

**Problem and observed callers.** Workshop already shares two useful forms of behavior: exact candidate acceptance in the editor module and busy/error/focus handling in the GUI edit-session module. Workspace exclusivity nevertheless remains a convention distributed through the shell and panels. The shell owns `pendingImport`, `pendingValidation`, `pendingRecovery` and the Test panel's `held()` state. At each of the Models, Definitions, Composition, Entity, Presets, Ship, Slot, Scripts and Spatial mounts, it supplies the same composite `busy` reader, a writable `setBusy` callback that changes `pendingValidation`, and a change callback that selects the file, refreshes, persists and announces (`gui/workshop-authoring.js:250–285`).

A Composition handler checks `busy()` before entering `guarded`, while `createWorkshopEditSession.guarded` itself sets busy and releases it after its operation. Ship authoring additionally captures a generation and passes currency/release callbacks to `runWorkshopMutation`. The shared helper explicitly owns busy presentation rather than freshness or cancellation policy (`gui/workshop-edit-session.js:67`). Meanwhile shell-owned inspect, validation/export, save, undo/redo and direct source editing each spell their own exclusion checks and release logic. The workspace rule therefore crosses a seam as mutable coordination callbacks rather than being owned by one workspace-operation implementation.

The observed cost is that a new panel must learn the shell's global exclusion convention and the appropriate helper's completion convention in addition to its own typed editing behavior. This is not evidence of a present overlap bug: the inspected handlers do check the gate, and tests cover important exclusivity cases.

**Solution opportunity.** Concentrate workspace-wide operation admission and completion ownership in the Workshop editing session, including its relationship to import, recovery and disposable Test. Keep panel-specific reads, form validation, focus destinations, and error vocabulary with the panels. Preserve `acceptWorkshopChanges` as the exact-source acceptance owner and let the workspace operation lifetime compose it where required. Do not impose full candidate validation on raw Source repair edits: PASM expressly keeps that workflow separate (`pasm/spec/architecture/editor-authoring.yaml:56`). The next design step would establish which lifecycle facts are truly shared; no replacement interface is proposed here.

**Deletion test, depth, locality and leverage.** Deleting the acceptance module would duplicate document capture, revision/dependency freshness, validation and grouped-history adoption across its real callers; retain it. Deleting the edit-session helper would duplicate the error/reload/focus ritual; retain that behavior too. By contrast, the repeated shell callbacks and per-handler gate discipline are coordination that can become implementation detail of one deeper module. The benefit is locality for deciding whether an operation may start and when controls become available again, with leverage across all mounted panels and shell commands. Simply wrapping the repeated callbacks in an object would save lines without adding meaningful depth.

**Invariants and compatibility.** Keep exact bytes and native asset references, grouped undo, immutable dependency inputs, runtime refusal semantics and stale-answer rejection. Preserve successful edits if the subsequent reread fails. Release controls before restoring focus. Do not let a completed stale operation re-enable controls held by a newer operation. Authoring and disposable Test must stay exclusive, and changes accepted by forms must still be the source consumed by export/save/Test. Native save capability and browser ZIP export remain separate real adapters, not optional flags to erase.

**Existing tests and missing verification.** `tests/client/workshop-edit-session.test.js:25` exercises one undoable edit and refresh/focus ordering; its cases from line 35 reject answers after edit, replacement or disposal; line 48 preserves an edit through a failed reread. `editor/tests/workshop-acceptance.test.js:32` runs Entity and Ship acceptance across browser, native-mod and native-project modes, including exact candidate adoption, refusal and revision/dependency changes. `tests/client/workshop-authoring.test.js:429` checks Authoring/Test exclusivity, including synthetic input at line 508; line 690 checks busy presentation; line 704 covers native save refusal. These are substantive protections, not tests to delete. Additional verification should cross the eventual workspace-operation seam with competing panel/shell attempts, replacement/disposal while awaiting an answer, and completion after a newer owner has acquired the workspace. The sampled tests establish pieces of that contract but do not establish a single owner today. No tests were executed.

**Tradeoffs and confidence.** High confidence in the repeated caller knowledge and medium confidence in the size of the refactor. A single-operation abstraction can become too broad if it absorbs panel freshness, all error wording, or every document action. Retain distinct editing and presentation policies internally; the useful target is shared workspace ownership, not a universal form framework. This candidate builds on current PASM acceptance/edit-session design rather than replacing it. No current concurrency defect is asserted.

#### Investigated structures that should be retained

- `gui/console-mount.js:123` already centralizes Console identity and load lifetime. Its live readers and presentation adapters are not automatically shallow: deleting it would move mounting, stale callback protection and publication ordering back into the shell. The distinct frame-identity lookup at `gui/console-mount.js:88` addresses shared HTML documents used by different Stations; collapsing identity to a console family would lose required behavior.
- `gui/console-state.js:2407`, `gui/console-state.js:2466` and `gui/console-state.js:2714` concentrate family projection and final Station enrichments. Its size alone is not evidence that splitting every builder produces a deeper interface. This sample did not establish a third architecture candidate there.
- `editor/workshop-runtime.js:13` and `editor/workshop-provider.js:61` adapt offline browser WASM and native requests. They are real adapters with different asset/persistence capabilities, not hypothetical seams. Native asset references must not be normalized into browser byte arrays for superficial uniformity.

### Overengineering

Module: Browser Transport. Scope: `packages/transport/`, with consumer tracing into Phoenix browser adapters, the independent Grid example, the rendezvous Worker and local rendezvous tooling.

Inspected baseline: source revision `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e` in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`; the audit instructions identify current HEAD `2de7d5329fba55e06ad00fbd8ca13f0cb4e8c73c` as documentation-only. This is a read-only source audit; no tests, builds, browser sessions or network requests were run.

Files/areas inspected: all eight package modules and its package manifest, with focused reads of host admission/channel adaptation, joiner construction/retry/relay attachment, registry lookup and relay integration, service mailbox behavior, join-code parsing and transport forcing. Consumer evidence includes `gui/rendezvous-transport.js`, `gui/fleet-session.js`, `examples/grid/app.js`, `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js`, compatibility re-exports and `scripts/rendezvous-dev-server.mjs`. Existing unit tests were inspected selectively for the findings and protocol constraints. Orientation included `AGENTS.md`, `wiki/SCHEMA.md`, `CONTEXT.md`, `wiki/index.md`, `wiki/concepts/reusable-layers.md`, networking documentation and relevant rendezvous/reusable-layer PASM declarations. No nested `AGENTS.md` was found in the inspected package/consumer directories; `docs/adr` is absent.

#### 1. Relay pairs expose a mutable limit-negotiation phase that no production path uses

**Files and evidence.** `packages/transport/src/rendezvous-relay.js:127` stores constructor limits in mutable `bounds`; `:197` exposes `applyLimits(next)`, permitting those bounds to be replaced for an existing pair. The sole located call is `tests/client/rendezvous-relay-channels.test.js:243`, in a test specifically demonstrating a later advertisement. Both actual creation paths already receive their complete advertisement at construction: host `relayPeerState` calls `createRelayChannelPair` with `relayLimitsFromFrame(limits)` at `packages/transport/src/rendezvous-transport.js:750`–`:756`; joiner `relayAttach` supplies `readyFrame.limits` at `:1482`–`:1489`, then opens the pair at `:1498`.

**Observed callers and deletion test.** Phoenix wrappers and Grid both call these same host/joiner factories (`gui/rendezvous-transport.js:30`, `:33`; `examples/grid/app.js:29`, `:38`). Neither obtains a relay pair to update. The Worker uses `service-relay.js`, not this browser channel-pair factory (`worker-rendezvous/src/relay.js:1`). Searching the repository found no runtime call to `applyLimits`. Removing this method and keeping the constructor-provided binding constant leaves both shipped attachment paths unchanged. This is more specific than suggesting removal of configurable limits: constructor injection and the service's advertised limits are actively used and must remain.

**Why it matters.** The unnecessary public mutator promises a second lifecycle that maintainers must reason about: whether limits can shrink during a live connection, whether partial updates replace or merge, and whether a changed bound affects both delivery classes immediately. Its implementation replaces the entire object while its fallback treats a falsy update as no change. None of that policy has a current protocol event or consumer. Its cost is small, but real: extra state mutability, an exposed API and a test maintaining a capability the application never invokes.

**Concrete opportunity.** Remove `applyLimits` and make the local bounds binding constant. Preserve `relayLimitsFromFrame`, constructor configuration, fallback values and all delivery-class enforcement. Replace the isolated post-construction-update test with construction-time advertised-limit coverage if the existing constructor tests do not already prove the same ceiling behavior. This does not call for removing useful boundary tests to reduce a line count.

**Invariants and verification.** Keep byte-counted frame ceilings; snapshots may shed while oversized reliable frames terminate the link; both channels close together; limits are installed before `open()`. Existing tests cover those behaviors at `tests/client/rendezvous-relay-channels.test.js:157`, `:176`, `:203`, `:220` and `:228`. Host-to-peer failure propagation is covered at `tests/client/rendezvous-transport.test.js:2063`. A focused future verification should assert both factory paths use service-advertised limits on their first outgoing frame. No runtime verification was performed in this audit.

**Tradeoff, confidence and limitation.** High confidence that the method has no in-repository runtime caller; low severity. Deletion gives up speculative live renegotiation, which could be restored if a future protocol actually advertises new limits. The package is private (`packages/transport/package.json:4`); external untracked imports cannot be excluded, but there is no evidence of a published compatibility requirement for this method.

#### 2. Registry namespace eligibility is configurable only for a hypothetical future namespace

**Files and evidence.** `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:209` explicitly explains that `joinableNamespaces` remains because a future namespace might exist only to make wrong-type refusals answerable. The option defaults to both existing namespaces at `:220`, becomes a `Set` at `:259`, and adds a lookup rejection at `:293`. Every located runtime construction uses that default: the Cloudflare adapter passes only `data` at `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:102`, and the local service passes only `data` at `scripts/rendezvous-dev-server.mjs:105`. A source search found no caller supplying `joinableNamespaces`, including tests.

**Observed behavior and deletion test.** Namespace identity is already a closed two-case concept: `namespaceOf` enumerates client and server in `packages/transport/src/join-code.js:200`, and the registry's `askedNamespace` accepts server or defaults to client at `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:327`. The required wrong-field check is the next line after the configurable-membership check, `:294`, which compares the record's namespace with the request's expected namespace. Therefore removing the unused constructor option, its set and the extra membership test does not remove typed crew-versus-fleet lookup under the current two-namespace contract. Grid does not construct a bespoke registry; its adapter uses the same service protocol and an application-owned format table (`examples/grid/app.js:3`, `:29`, `:38`). The option is not presently delivering a Grid-specific reuse requirement.

**Why it matters.** The configuration exposes policy combinations that the deployed adapters do not select and that existing tests do not appear to exercise. It suggests generalized namespace extensibility even though parsing and request interpretation still explicitly enumerate only two namespaces. A future third namespace would consequently require substantive changes elsewhere regardless of this option. The maintained cost is an extra constructor contract, lookup branch and explanatory future-facing documentation, rather than a significant runtime expense.

**Concrete opportunity.** Narrow the registry to its currently supported joinable client/server namespaces and remove the unused eligibility option. Preserve all namespace identifiers, expected-namespace comparisons and distinct unknown/wrong-type/version-mismatch results. If non-joinable namespaces are still an intended requirement, the alternative is to retain this seam deliberately and add a real consumer or explicit contract tests; do not describe it as serving an existing deployed variant.

**Design constraint.** `pasm/spec/architecture/rendezvous-transport.yaml` records the original staged rollout rationale: client-only joining first, then enabling server joining by adding an entry. That historical transition did happen and explains why this API exists. This report proposes reconsidering the now-unused variation point, not silently overriding PASM. Any implementation should reconcile that design statement with the current two-namespace contract and follow the repository's rules for changing ratified design.

**Invariants and verification.** Existing registry tests exercise typed lookup beginning at `tests/client/rendezvous-registry.test.js:150`, including wrong-type full codes at `:186`, and fleet joins beginning at `:311`, including both wrong-field directions at `:346` and `:362`. These are the regression surface for removing the redundant policy check. Keep admission closure, exact project/version lookup, namespace-specific record ownership and capability checks untouched. Missing verification is a test showing any supported deployment intentionally disables one otherwise valid namespace; none was located.

**Tradeoff, confidence and limitation.** High confidence about current call sites; medium confidence that maintainers want to retire the documented future seam. Low severity: this is a narrow deletion, not a reason to redesign the registry. An out-of-repository embedding that configures namespace eligibility would be a reason to retain it, but the private package and inspected consumers provide no such evidence.

#### Suspected complexity that the deletion test does not justify removing

- **The reusable package and Phoenix compatibility facades are serving different consumers.** `gui/rendezvous-transport.js:11` installs/localises Phoenix Welcome catalogues, `:21` selects Phoenix factories, and `:30`–`:34` injects application behavior. Grid imports the package directly at `examples/grid/app.js:1` and uses its own `grid/1` stamp at `:29` and `:38`. Removing the injection seam would pull Phoenix policy into the independent example or force another transport implementation. The Worker re-exports service mechanisms from this package. These are active boundaries, not abstract layering added for an imagined second client.

- **DataChannel-shaped relay channels and the peer stub unify a real admission boundary.** `packages/transport/src/rendezvous-transport.js:586` wires one reliable-channel compatibility gate. Both the real WebRTC path (`:655`) and relay path (`:739`) reach it, so a transport change does not create another route around `checkStamp`. `packages/transport/src/rendezvous-relay.js:247` supplies a minimal peer object for connection cleanup/diagnostics. Replacing this with null and duplicating guards would not delete the underlying lifecycle obligations. Eviction must sever both channels and drop in-flight data; the tests at `tests/client/rendezvous-transport.test.js:647`, `:677` and `:715` pin that boundary.

- **Service queues and sender-side shedding protect different resources.** `packages/transport/src/service-relay.js:129` owns bounded per-pair mailboxes; `:270` enqueues and `:333` drains them. The Worker explicitly marks unavailable destinations unwritable at `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:117`; it has no browser-style send-buffer visibility. Browser pairs instead inspect the sending socket's actual backlog in `packages/transport/src/rendezvous-relay.js:170`. Deleting either because both mention backpressure would discard a different bound. Reliable overflow must disconnect only the responsible pair; snapshot shedding must retain class semantics and counters. The service cannot prevent all WebSocket head-of-line blocking, and its comments correctly limit that claim. This audit does not infer that service-side queues measure live network congestion.

- **Transport forcing and injected factories make otherwise hard-to-reach paths observable.** `packages/transport/src/transport-levers.js:86` maps direct, TURN and WebSocket-only controls to concrete ICE/fallback restrictions. Host peer configuration applies them at `packages/transport/src/rendezvous-transport.js:512`; the joiner applies them again at `:1501`. They are not interchangeable settings: direct must withhold TURN servers, TURN must require relay candidates, and both must suppress WebSocket fallback so it cannot conceal the tested failure. Deleting them would weaken acceptance coverage; `tests/client/transport-levers.test.js` and transport relay cases exercise the branches. Factory injection also underpins deterministic socket/channel fixtures without needing live network services.

- **Fleet control and continuation are active, not dead generality.** `gui/fleet-session.js:1378` creates the joiner with non-localised host frames, an acceptance callback at `:1389` and fleet-control handling at `:1421`. Registry successor/takeover/grace entry points at `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:508`, `:571`, `:597` and `:778` implement bounded ownership continuity described in PASM. Removing that machinery because Grid does not use fleets would break a current Phoenix consumer. The corresponding service tests live in `tests/client/rendezvous-fleet-takeover.test.js`. Full capability-security and election correctness were not audited here.

#### Coverage limits

This is not an exhaustive correctness review of the approximately 89 KB browser state machine or 63 KB registry. The state-machine reads focused on real seams and proposed deletions; not every event interleaving, timeout, continuation proof or code-reclaim branch was traced. Native Rust transport counterparts were not audited line by line, and no claim is made that they can be merged with JavaScript implementations. Cloudflare deployment behavior, TURN provider behavior, browser-specific ICE behavior, actual Grid browser/native smoke execution and end-to-end fleet recovery were not exercised. Test references describe existing source coverage rather than freshly passing results. No removal of protocol revisions, compatibility facades, deterministic fixtures, independent examples, admission checks or delivery-class guarantees is recommended.
