#### 1. Small concrete opportunity: share delivery-class vocabulary instead of maintaining three equivalent JavaScript declarations

**Evidence and callers.** The package separately declares the same two strings at:

- `packages/transport/src/rendezvous-transport.js:152` and `:154`: `RELIABLE_CHANNEL` and `SNAPSHOT_CHANNEL`.
- `packages/transport/src/rendezvous-relay.js:71` and `:73`: `RELAY_RELIABLE_LABEL` and `RELAY_SNAPSHOT_LABEL`.
- `packages/transport/src/service-relay.js:77` and `:78`: `RELAY_RELIABLE` and `RELAY_SNAPSHOT`.

These are connected implementations, not merely unrelated values spelled alike. The direct offer creates DataChannels with the first pair (`rendezvous-transport.js:1542`); the answering host dispatches their labels at `:699`. The relay channel factory deliberately uses the second pair as both the DataChannel-compatible label and the outgoing relay frame's class (`rendezvous-relay.js:189`), while the service's `isRelayClass` validates against the third pair (`service-relay.js:81`, called by `service-registry.js:1106`). Both host and joiner instantiate the same relay pair through `rendezvous-transport.js:750` and `:1484`.

**Why it matters.** Keeping these three pairs aligned is a protocol requirement. One divergent edit can make a relay frame invalid or route it to the wrong local channel. The package already addresses exactly this kind of drift for the revision and UTF-8 payload measurement: `rendezvous-protocol.js:18` and `:37` own shared values/behavior consumed by the service and browser. Delivery classes could use that existing dependency-free owner too. This is a low-severity maintenance opportunity, not an observed runtime failure; the literals currently agree.

**Concrete change.** Declare the two stable wire strings once in `rendezvous-protocol.js`; import or re-export them under the existing public names from all three modules. Retain existing export names so GUI facades, Worker consumers and tests do not need an API migration. Do not make the service import the browser transport module or introduce a dependency on game/UI/session code.

**Invariants.** Preserve the exact strings, direct channel ordering/retransmission options, snapshot fallback behavior, invalid-class refusal, reliable-frame failure behavior, and the host's admission-before-delivery rule. Sharing names is not a reason to combine sender and mailbox policies.

**Verification.** `tests/client/rendezvous-relay-channels.test.js:35` is titled as a comparison with negotiated labels, but actually compares the produced labels only with the relay module's own exports. A narrow cross-export/literal contract assertion would make that intention explicit. Existing direct-channel behavior is covered at `tests/client/rendezvous-transport.test.js:927`; relay delivery-class behavior at `:1816`; shared mailbox bounds and refusal behavior live in `tests/client/rendezvous-relay.test.js`. Retain these behavioral tests even if names share one declaration. No test results are claimed from this audit.

**Tradeoff and confidence.** High confidence that the definitions are duplicate protocol vocabulary and safe to share while preserving exports. The code savings are tiny; the benefit is one edit point, not a sizeable reduction in implementation. This does not warrant a new abstraction or a general-purpose protocol framework.

#### 2. Existing reuse to preserve: the relay is an adapter onto the ordinary channel/admission path

`rendezvous-transport.js:586` owns the host's reliable-channel handshake, creates the ordinary connection adapter, and prevents refused or unadmitted traffic from reaching the page. Direct channels call it at `:706`; relayed channels call it at `:788`. The joiner similarly calls `wireChannels` (`:1412`) from the relay path (`:1495`) and direct offer path (`:1544`). `rendezvous-relay.js:118` supplies the DataChannel-compatible pair instead of copying handshake and application-delivery logic.

The remaining host and joiner channel handlers are not interchangeable duplicates: the host checks a supplied compatibility policy and hands an admitted adapter to the application, while the joiner sends a handshake, processes acceptance/refusal, identifies itself, and maintains retry state. Their signalling handlers also differ deliberately: the host answers SDP (`:797`), while the joiner receives an answer and captures the concrete attempt and candidate queue across promises (`:1707`). Extracting a single lifecycle merely because both queue ICE candidates would have to encode those role and generation differences. No demonstrated reuse gain justifies that change here.

Preserve this existing sharing and the distinctions around it. Tests exercise skipped handshakes (`tests/client/rendezvous-transport.test.js:647`), eviction of both channels (`:677`), stale SDP completions (`:1041`), and the real-order relay placeholder upgrade (`:1779`). Those are meaningful separate cases, not redundant test copies. Confidence is high for the inspected wiring; this is a retained-design observation, not a request to change code.

#### 3. Similar backpressure code represents different responsibilities, not a replaceable second implementation

The browser sender in `rendezvous-relay.js:156` measures the outgoing payload, sheds an oversized snapshot or fails an oversized reliable link, and at `:170` sheds a new snapshot if the WebSocket's send buffer already exceeds its ceiling. The service hub at `service-relay.js:278` validates incoming frames and at `:284` keeps bounded queues, discarding oldest snapshots while retaining newer ones. Reliable overflow marks the pair for termination; `:331` drains reliable traffic first when the adapter says the destination is writable.

The browser can observe `bufferedAmount`; the Durable Object adapter cannot. The service must also isolate mailboxes by sender/receiver pair (`service-relay.js:155`) so one joiner's burst does not terminate or evict another's traffic. The sender does not implement those mailboxes. Unifying these code paths would hide distinct queue ownership and loss semantics, not reuse an equivalent algorithm.

The genuinely identical calculation is already shared: both import `relayPayloadBytes` from `rendezvous-protocol.js:37`. Service bounds are parsed at `service-relay.js:91` and advertised from the single `service-registry.js:1079` helper; browser wire advice is translated at `rendezvous-relay.js:92`. Browser defaults intentionally use a conservative 65,536-byte ceiling before advice, while the service default is 262,144 bytes. Those defaults should not be mechanically consolidated.

Existing verification includes `tests/client/rendezvous-relay-channels.test.js`, `tests/client/rendezvous-relay.test.js`, and the oversized reliable frame integration case at `tests/client/rendezvous-transport.test.js:2063`. No additional reuse change is recommended. Confidence is high for the queue/sender distinction; actual network buffering behavior was not tested.

#### 4. Compatibility facades and native counterparts are justified; deleting them would remove necessary boundaries

**JavaScript facades already reuse the implementation.** `worker-rendezvous/src/registry.js:1` and `worker-rendezvous/src/relay.js:1` re-export package implementations. The deployed Worker constructs that registry at `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:102`; local tooling also consumes it through the facade (`scripts/rendezvous-dev-server.mjs:65` and `:105`). There is no second Worker registry to merge into this package. `gui/rendezvous-transport.js:6` imports the package and supplies Phoenix localization, join data and endpoint/factory defaults. Its wrappers at `:31` and `:34` are composition, not copied host/joiner state machines. `examples/grid/app.js:1`, `:29` and `:38` use the package directly with Grid's own policy. Removing the facade would shift Phoenix policy into callers or the reusable owner rather than remove duplicated behavior.

**Rust counterparts cannot simply import browser mechanisms.** `crates/phoenix-transport/src/rendezvous.rs:39` and `:53` declare matching native wire vocabulary, and `codec.rs:3` provides serde-based envelope encoding. `relay.rs:22` exposes application-supplied compatibility and payload/identity policy on a native socket transport. The browser equivalent has WebRTC callbacks and JavaScript channel objects. These must interoperate, but sharing source by introducing client WASM or an embedded JS interpreter would contradict the pure-JavaScript phone and reusable native boundaries.

Native join-code handling in `src/native_host/join_codes.rs:316`, `:329` and `:422` intentionally mirrors canonicalization, validation and structured-input parsing in `packages/transport/src/join-code.js:119`, `:175` and `:275`. In particular, the native `is_code_head` at `:482` and JavaScript `CODE_HEAD` at `:255` must agree about punctuation versus full identifiers. Keep parity coverage rather than deleting a target implementation. `src/native_host/join_codes_tests.rs:214` pins the head-shape rule; browser parsing has `tests/client/join-code.test.js` and authored-data checks in `tests/client/join-codes-data.test.js`. A full cross-language fixture audit was outside this review.

Even the retry formula has a meaningful current difference: browser `connection-manager.js:1` defaults to a 30-second ceiling, used with a 1-second initial host delay at `rendezvous-transport.js:1051`; native `relay_socket.rs:221` caps at 60 seconds. The native comment describes the schedules as matching, but their current behavior is not identical. That is a documentation/policy discrepancy to verify if retry tuning changes, not evidence that one target implementation can replace the other. Native delay tests exist in `relay_socket_tests.rs`; browser schedule tests start at `tests/client/connection-manager.test.js:206`.

**Tiny cryptographic formatting overlap does not justify a forbidden dependency.** Registry `mintSecret` (`service-registry.js:196`) and session `mintToken` (`packages/session/src/session-token.js:35`) both turn 16 random bytes into lowercase hex. The registry intentionally draws capabilities directly from crypto independently of its injectable suffix generator. Importing the session helper would couple two deliberately independent packages for a few lines and would introduce a browser-session module into the service dependency graph. Preserve the separate capability purpose and randomness source. Existing tests pin token formatting (`tests/client/session-token.test.js:12`) and distinct/private fleet capabilities (`tests/client/rendezvous-fleet-takeover.test.js:54`). No common utility package is justified by this overlap alone.

Confidence is high that the facades and target boundaries are intentional and currently exercised by callers. The native and cryptographic comparisons were targeted source samples, not complete audits of their owners.

#### Inspection limits

The report does not claim exhaustive analysis of the 1,000-plus-line service registry's fleet takeover, expiration and disconnect state machine, or every host/joiner reconnect branch. Those were traced only where relevant to concrete reuse candidates. Native codecs, host admission, join-code tests, Grid and GUI callers were sampled to establish boundaries and live usage; their complete implementations are separate audit scopes. The deployment adapters, production networking behavior, every PASM relationship, and every test body were not exhaustively reviewed. No speculative replacement dependency is proposed, and no runtime defect is asserted from mere code similarity.


Audit 59/72 · Agent: `/root/audit59_browser_transport_architecture` · Lens: architecture

### Architecture

Module: Browser Transport (`packages/transport/`). Source inspected in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, at the audit's declared source baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e` with the documented navigation-only HEAD change. This is a read-only architecture audit, not an implementation proposal or a claim that tests were run.

Scope and orientation: read the architecture skill and its LANGUAGE.md, repository AGENTS.md, wiki schema, domain vocabulary, wiki index and reusable-layer guide; consulted message-flow/client-architecture navigation and the reusable-layers and rendezvous-transport PASM slices. No `docs/adr` directory was present and no nested AGENTS.md was found under the inspected package, GUI, examples or Worker areas. Inspected all eight transport source modules at least at their public surfaces; concentrated detailed reading on host/joiner lifecycle, channel adaptation, relay channels and registry composition. Traced Phoenix's adapter, host recipient routing and page outbound send loop, the fleet joiner's acceptance hook, Grid's browser host and console, Worker registry composition, local rendezvous tooling and relevant test suites.

#### 1. Deepen the connection module so callers select a delivery class without managing its physical channels

**Files.** `packages/transport/src/rendezvous-transport.js:355` (`connectionAdapter`), `:427` (snapshot adoption), `:586` (shared host admission), `:1798` onward (joiner outbound delivery); `packages/transport/src/rendezvous-relay.js:118`; `gui/host-peer-routing.js:66`; `server.html:8297`; `examples/grid/app.js:52`.

**Problem and observed callers.** The transport module already knows both physical channels and their lifecycle, but its host-facing interface exports a raw `snapshotChannel` next to a reliable-only `send`. Its implementation updates that field and emits snapshot transitions (`rendezvous-transport.js:427`). Consequently Phoenix's recipient adapter must inspect `snapshotChannel`, test readiness and choose a physical sender for each delivery class (`host-peer-routing.js:66–75`). The host page then catches physical send exceptions per target (`server.html:8315–8323`). Grid independently implements that same readiness choice and exception handling (`examples/grid/app.js:54–56`). Neither application is simply requesting snapshot delivery; both have learned the mechanics of the channel pair.

This is an actual seam leak across two consumers, not a hypothetical abstraction justified by a long file. On the joiner side, the transport itself already chooses a snapshot channel and falls back to reliable sending. On the host side, equivalent knowledge is outside the module. There is also an existing difference worth making explicit: Grid catches a failing snapshot send and drops it, while the joiner falls through to reliable sending after a snapshot send throws. Phoenix's outer loop similarly catches and drops the selected sender's failing frame. Snapshot loss is permitted, so this difference is not by itself a correctness bug; it demonstrates that the policy currently lives in several places.

**Solution opportunity.** Deepen the host connection module to own delivery-class channel selection and send-failure handling. Keep the recipient decision with Rust's `BrowserConnections` and Phoenix's physical connection map. The transport should concentrate knowledge about which channel can carry a requested class, while application callers retain responsibility for *which logical connections* receive each message. This is a change in responsibility, not a request to merge identity routing into transport or to invent a replacement interface here.

**Deletion test, depth and benefits.** Deleting the connection adapter would push handshake, eviction, channel pairing and error handling into both applications, so the adapter earns its keep. The candidate is to increase its depth, not remove it. Conversely, the physical-channel selection branches in Phoenix and Grid can disappear if the connection module hides that decision. This provides leverage to both host consumers and locality for future relay/direct delivery changes. A caller should not need to know that a relay is represented by DataChannel-shaped objects to send a snapshot.

**Invariants to preserve.** Reliable commands must stay reliable; snapshots may be shed; a missing snapshot channel must retain the existing reliable fallback; one recipient's send failure must not stop the remaining outbound flush. Closing a refused connection must sever both channels and prevent in-flight messages from reaching Admission. Signalling loss must not close an established direct link, while relay socket loss remains a real link failure. Session identity, reserved-token refusal, replacement and recipient selection must remain authoritative in their existing owner. The transport cannot start interpreting game messages to decide their class.

**Tests and missing verification.** Existing tests already establish a substantial public behavior surface: `tests/client/rendezvous-transport.test.js:927` tests unordered, non-retransmitting negotiation; `:941` exposes the host snapshot channel; `:967` checks joiner preference/fallback; `:677` and `:715` cover eviction/refusal on both channels; `:1816` checks relay class separation. `tests/client/host-peer-routing.test.js:94` verifies per-recipient channel choice and live changes in readiness. `tests/client/rendezvous-relay-channels.test.js:109`, `:133`, `:157` and `:176` cover snapshot shedding, reliable preservation and oversized-frame behavior. These should survive as behavior checks, with channel-choice tests moving to the deepened connection's interface where appropriate, rather than deleting coverage. Missing at the proposed seam is one shared host-send contract exercised over direct and relay adapters, including snapshot closure between selection and sending and isolation of one failed recipient. Grid smoke (`tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs:50–81`) proves reuse and movement/recovery but is not a replacement for those narrow failure cases.

**Tradeoffs and confidence.** High confidence in the locality problem; medium confidence on refactor payoff relative to migration cost. Existing consumers and tests observe raw channels, so compatibility may need preserving during adoption, especially diagnostics. Do not silently make host snapshots retry reliably on every thrown send merely because the joiner currently does so: decide and test the intended semantics first. This finding does not establish that any shipped snapshot is being delivered incorrectly.

#### 2. Concentrate application message preparation in the application adapters instead of generic joiner mode switches

**Files.** `packages/transport/src/rendezvous-transport.js:1154–1189`, `:1445–1462`, `:1556–1563`, `:1798–1846`; `gui/rendezvous-transport.js:10–15`, `:34–35`; `gui/fleet-session.js:1378–1419`; `client.html:4800–4826`; `examples/grid/app.js:29–31`.

**Problem and observed callers.** The generic transport accepts `getIdent`, `onAccepted`, `localise`, `localiseDeliveredMessage` and `reasonStringId`. After acceptance it either calls `onAccepted` or manufactures an `Identify` message. Ordinary sends manufacture the crew-style `{type,data}` envelope, while `sendFrame` bypasses that envelope. Received messages are decoded and optionally passed through localisation. The Phoenix adapter correctly owns the actual String Table behavior, including Welcome catalogue installation before localisation (`gui/rendezvous-transport.js:10`), but the generic joiner still owns the decision of whether to apply it and whether this connection should identify as a crew member.

The fleet consumer demonstrates the extra interface knowledge: it supplies `localise: false`, replaces default Identify with `onAccepted`, and uses `sendFrame` for its own envelope (`fleet-session.js:1386–1409`). Grid also supplies `localise: false` and opts into the generic Identify shape. The implementations already vary in real callers; this is not an argument for adding an unused extensibility mechanism. The package has no forbidden Phoenix import, but its interface still reflects how the original crew client works.

**Solution opportunity.** Concentrate crew Identify generation, crew envelope conveniences and localisation policy in the Phoenix adapter; retain common connection establishment, compatibility handshake, reconnect generations and transport-plane fleet proofs in the reusable module. Grid can own its small application message preparation. The point is to make the reusable module's behavior neutral after compatibility acceptance, while allowing the existing Phoenix adapter to preserve the client page's convenient interface. Do not duplicate reconnect or move authenticated registry continuation into application game messages.

**Deletion test, depth and benefits.** Removing the transport would reproduce substantial real connection machinery across crew, fleet and Grid callers: it is deep and useful. The shallower part is the cluster of application-message conveniences inside that module, because callers either accept Phoenix-shaped behavior or must know the switches that suppress it. Moving those decisions to their existing adapters reduces interface knowledge and gives locality to changes such as Welcome catalogue ordering or crew identification. The reusable seam becomes a clearer test surface: connection acceptance/reconnect can be verified without loading Phoenix strings or manufacturing a crew identity solely to reach later transport behavior.

**Invariants to preserve.** Compatibility acceptance precedes application identity; the host must install its handler before sending acceptance (`rendezvous-transport.js:644–648`). Crew Identify must still be sent on every successful reconnect with the same Session token. Localisation must still install Welcome overlay catalogues before processing text, and retain raw delivered messages as the current adapter does. Fleet acceptance generation is meaningful: `fleet-session.js:1390–1404` distinguishes a real reconnect from duplicate acceptance and resets connection-bound continuation state and GM readiness accordingly. Native peers must keep the existing wire grammar; this opportunity requires no protocol revision. Pure-JS Consoles, separate fleet vocabulary and host Admission remain intact.

**Tests and missing verification.** Most transport behavior tests enter through `gui/rendezvous-transport.js`, as shown by `tests/client/rendezvous-transport.test.js:14–24`. They are valuable composition tests, not invalid tests. In particular `:405`, `:600` and `:1117` prove Identify ordering and reconnection; `:467` and `:492` prove localisation and correlation preservation. Keep these at the Phoenix adapter interface. Grid's direct package import and smoke test provide independent use evidence, but sampled smoke coverage is primarily successful movement/recovery. Add or relocate transport contract cases at the package interface with a deliberately different application vocabulary, asserting reconnect acceptance and opaque application delivery without implicit Identify/localisation. This would complement the Phoenix adapter tests rather than replacing them with tests of private helpers.

**Tradeoffs and confidence.** Medium confidence; lower priority than finding 1. The current hooks are compact, already support Grid, and avoid copying the entire transport. A migration is only worthwhile if it actually removes caller mode knowledge; moving the same switches to another generic wrapper buys little depth. Default machine reasons and the shared compatibility handshake are legitimate transport concerns. This is not a recommendation to remove `JoinHandshake`, to remove fleet control, or to replace the proven connection implementation with several protocol-specific copies.

#### Retained seams and limits of the audit

- The registry/Worker seam is justified. `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:216` takes authored data, time and randomness and presents connect/receive/disconnect behavior without sockets. `worker-rendezvous/src/registry.js:1` is an intentional compatibility re-export, and `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:109` onward composes the registry with Cloudflare sockets. `scripts/rendezvous-dev-server.mjs:65`, `:105` and `:182` use the same machinery in a different runtime. Deleting the registry would duplicate rules across adapters; deleting the compatibility facade would principally change import paths, not improve depth. No candidate is raised for either.
- Relay channels are a real adapter, not gratuitous imitation of WebRTC. `rendezvous-relay.js:118` provides the same observable shape that lets `rendezvous-transport.js:586` apply one compatibility gate to direct and relayed links. The relay hub (`service-relay.js:129`) owns bounded per-pair queues separately from registry lookup/lifecycle. PASM explicitly explains their different resource profiles and the Durable Object's lack of `bufferedAmount`. Collapsing these modules merely because their names overlap would lose useful locality.
- Join-code canonicalisation and the transport lever module also hide real shared rules. The latter derives consistent ICE policy, server-list use and WebSocket fallback from a chosen mode (`transport-levers.js:85–122`), preventing a caller from claiming a pinned path while silently falling back. No removal recommendation is warranted.
- The audit sampled, rather than exhaustively proved, registry takeover/grace logic and its tests (`tests/client/rendezvous-fleet-takeover.test.js`), the development server's impairment plumbing, and fleet-session integration beyond the transport call sites. It did not inspect every native Rust protocol counterpart, every smoke fixture, credential Worker implementation, all page UI behavior, or run network/browser/build/test commands. It therefore makes no claim about deployed interoperability, performance, resource exhaustion, security completeness, or every possible lifecycle race. No source, specification or documentation was edited.

Module: Browser Transport. Scope: `packages/transport/`, inspected in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, against source baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e`. This is a read-only source audit: no code edits, test runs, browser sessions or network checks were performed.

#### 1. Remove the relay hub's unused writability query and redundant boolean overflow query

**Files and observed behavior.** `packages/transport/src/service-relay.js:248` exposes `isWritable(peer)`, but repository searches of project JavaScript/TypeScript sources found no caller. The operational check is already inside `drain` at `packages/transport/src/service-relay.js:333`: it reads the participant and returns no frames unless that participant exists and is writable. The mutating `setWritable` at line 242 remains essential. The Worker reports socket failure through `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:122`; registry `setWritable` at `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:1185` updates the hub and flushes when writable again.

The same hub exposes `hasOverflowed(owner)` at `packages/transport/src/service-relay.js:310`, a boolean projection over the mailboxes. Production instead calls `overflowedSources(owner)` at line 318, because it needs to identify the offending sender. `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:1007` uses those identities to end only the affected joiner's relay. The only discovered callers of `hasOverflowed` are the two assertions at `tests/client/rendezvous-relay.test.js:116` and line 118; the adjacent assertion at line 119 already checks the richer source list.

**Concrete change opportunity.** Delete `isWritable` outright. Delete `hasOverflowed` after expressing the existing boundary assertions through `overflowedSources`: no overflowed sources at the bound, and exactly `['host']` after exceeding it. Keep the test scenario and all delivery assertions. This deletes implementation and obsolete API documentation rather than moving logic elsewhere or shrinking formatting. It removes two public methods with no production consumer while making the boundary test exercise the query the registry actually uses.

**Countable savings.** The `isWritable` block occupies five lines including its one-line doc comment (247–251); the `hasOverflowed` block occupies six (307–312). That is 11 physical implementation/documentation lines, seven non-comment method lines, plus up to two separator blank lines. Replacing assertions need not add test lines. No savings are claimed from deleting test cases.

**Invariants and verification.** Keep participant writability state, per-pair queues, reliable-before-snapshot draining, cumulative drop counters, and sender-specific overflow closure. Do not replace `overflowedSources` with the boolean query: that would discard the identity needed to avoid disconnecting unrelated phones. Existing tests at `tests/client/rendezvous-relay.test.js:112` exercise the precise reliable capacity boundary and retain queued reliable frames; line 136 tests sender isolation and line 429 tests registry closure of only the offending phone. These are meaningful coverage and must remain. Updating the boundary assertions is sufficient targeted verification for the query deletion; the relay contract suite should then be run during implementation, but has not been run here.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limitations.** High confidence for in-repository consumers. The package is private (`packages/transport/package.json:4`), although its wildcard exports and Worker facade expose the hub to any untracked local consumers; static search cannot prove absence of such callers or computed property access. Deleting a public method is an API change even when all known runtime consumers are unaffected. If external compatibility is required, retain it and do not claim the associated savings. This is a small cleanup, not grounds for reorganizing the hub.

#### 2. Delete the registry's unread creation timestamp

**Files and observed behavior.** Fresh registration writes `createdAt: now()` at `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:484`; rotation writes the same field at line 707. There are no discovered reads of that field in project transport, adapters, tooling or tests. The registry's records are closure-private (`records` at line 255), and its public diagnostic projection at line 1264 does not expose timestamps.

Expiry explicitly uses `lastSeen` at `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:365`, with the shorter `graceUntil` deadline checked first. The live host refresh is at line 1211. The comments at lines 357 and 485 explain why age since creation must not expire a talking host, but no remaining behavior needs to retain that historical timestamp. Worker and local service consumers use the public registry rather than inspecting its records (`worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:102` and `scripts/rendezvous-dev-server.mjs:105`).

**Concrete change opportunity.** Remove the two `createdAt` fields. Preserve the rationale that expiry measures inactivity, with concise wording that does not imply a stored creation timestamp. This is genuine deletion of unused state, two clock calls and two maintained assignments. It does not replace them with another cache or timestamp.

**Countable savings.** Two executable source lines. No documentation reduction is required or counted. The benefit is mainly removing a misleading second lifetime marker; it is not a material file-size reduction.

**Invariants and verification.** Keep `lastSeen`, its update before the expiry sweep, the reclaim deadline, and rotation's fresh secret/peer set/admission behavior unchanged. Existing registry tests at `tests/client/rendezvous-registry.test.js:447`, line 464 and line 488 cover expiry, survival across multiple TTL windows with activity, and notifying an expired idle host. Rotation coverage begins at line 899. These tests verify observable outcomes without inspecting `createdAt` and should remain unchanged. Check any injected test clock before implementation: deleting calls changes the call count, so a hypothetical increment-on-every-call clock could observe it; the inspected TTL tests use an explicitly advanced clock. No new test that merely asserts the absence of the private field is useful.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limitations.** High confidence that the field is dead in the inspected repository. It could become useful for future telemetry, but there is no current timestamp-bearing diagnostic API; hypothetical telemetry is not a reason to retain it. This recommendation is deliberately limited to the unread field and makes no change to TTL or reclaim semantics.

#### Reductions considered but not recommended

- **Do not merge WebRTC and WebSocket lifecycle rules merely because they close similar objects.** `packages/transport/src/rendezvous-transport.js:586` already shares host compatibility admission through `attachReliableChannel`; `wireChannels` at line 1412 shares joiner handshake and acceptance handling. The relay-specific setup at line 739 replaces an unfinished RTC placeholder but preserves an existing live path. The joiner teardown at line 1278 clears callbacks before closing, whereas the host adapter close at line 408 intentionally triggers refusal and close handling. A common close loop that ignores these distinctions would risk stale callbacks, duplicate delivery or incomplete eviction. Further extraction here was not shown to produce a net reduction after the required policy arguments and guards.
- **Keep the relay channel pair and service mailbox as distinct mechanisms.** `packages/transport/src/rendezvous-relay.js:155` sheds an outgoing snapshot against the socket buffer and fails an oversized reliable link. `packages/transport/src/service-relay.js:277` manages per-sender queues, sheds the oldest queued snapshots, and records overflow for registry handling. Their similar class checks do not represent duplicated algorithms. Both already share byte measurement through `rendezvous-protocol.js:35`. Removing one does not preserve the other's guarantees.
- **Fresh registration and rotation have similar record literals, but are not interchangeable constructor calls today.** Compare `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:464` and line 698. Registration includes advertised `transports`; the rotation literal does not. Reclaim and fleet takeover additionally preserve different membership, admission and capability state (lines 432 and 597). A constructor could centralize common fields, but counting both literals as wholly removable duplication would overstate savings and hide existing differences. Resolving whether rotation should preserve transport advertisement is a separate behavior question, not a justified LOC-only change. No savings estimated.
- **Keep the join-code shape distinction and language counterparts.** `packages/transport/src/join-code.js:275` determines structured-code shape before suffix canonicalisation because underscore serves two roles. The suffix route and structured route share validation but carry different project/version authority. Replacing them with a single split-and-normalize expression would change malformed, unknown-project and namespace handling. Native implementations must remain independent because phones stay pure JavaScript and native hosts need the same wire contract without importing browser runtime code.
- **Keep compatibility facades and application injection.** `gui/rendezvous-transport.js:30` supplies Phoenix configuration and factories, while Grid imports the reusable module directly at `examples/grid/app.js:1`; `worker-rendezvous/src/registry.js:1` retains the service import path. Deleting these small facades or moving game configuration into the package would undermine the declared reusable boundary. Large explanatory comments were not treated as expendable lines: many record admission, ordering, backpressure and security constraints that a shorter implementation still has to maintain.

#### Coverage limits

This audit does not claim exhaustive control-flow coverage of the 1,857-line transport or 1,277-line registry. Fleet capability issuance/election, all host message-switch branches, URL-override policy branches and every diagnostic callback were not traced end to end. Test inspection concentrated on TTL/rotation and relay mailbox behavior; browser matrices, real ICE/TURN behavior, native interoperability and worker deployment were not executed or independently validated. The findings above rely on source-level caller tracing and preserve those unverified areas rather than recommending broad deletion within them.

The callers need detached row records, not recursively copied object graphs.

The cost occurs on a real path. `gui/fleet-session.js:361` and `:1304` construct owner continuation; `packages/session/src/fleet-owner-continuation.js:154` and `:155` export held tails, `:92` reconciles them, and `:35` replays the result. A qualifying reconciliation row can be serialized and parsed twice in the same loop, once into each map, after already being copied for a local tail. Large retained recovery payloads are explicitly supported: the journal allows an 8 MiB conservative byte budget (`fleet-continuation.js:3`, `:7`), and the wire tests deliberately exercise large escaped strings (`tests/client/fleet-continuation-wire.test.js:5`). The JSON cloning adds traversal and temporary escaped text proportional to payload size without giving these primitive-field records extra isolation.

**Deletion test and concrete opportunity.** Replace the general deep-copy operation with a deliberate copy of the three row fields. Keep detached record objects at the existing ownership boundaries. Do not remove either reconciliation map: the `rows` map checks conflicts across all carriers while `authorizedRows` restricts which carrier may prove an origin (`:121`, `:129`, `:135`, `:147`). Their distinct authority semantics are necessary. Similarly, do not remove copies by retaining arbitrary caller-owned row objects.

**Behavior to preserve.** Tail mutation must not mutate the live journal; a replay plan must not expose mutable retained row objects. Exact raw strings, authenticated origin provenance, duplicate suppression, sequence progression, retained byte accounting, overflow refusal, and missing-versus-unverifiable suffix errors must survive. Explicit field projection would cease preserving unrecognized extra fields on externally supplied rows. No inspected consumer needs those fields: the package's own producers do not emit them and delivery consumes `row.raw`/`row.origin`. Treat that narrowing as a deliberate contract choice, not an incidental spread/deep-copy substitution.

**Verification.** Existing tests cover replay without duplicate effects (`tests/client/fleet-continuation.test.js:13`), compaction (`:37`), missing/conflicting history (`:44`, `:51`), origin/sequence refusal (`:56`), overflow (`:63`), and nontransferable origin proof (`:80`, `:86`). Preserve those. Add a narrow public-boundary regression that mutates an exported tail row and an input replay-plan row after use and verifies retained history remains unchanged. The current inspected tests mutate tails to provoke reconciliation errors, but do not directly assert that such mutation cannot alter the originating journal. No benchmark was run; the avoidable serialization passes are certain, but the actual latency/peak-memory benefit is unmeasured.

**Tradeoff and confidence.** This is a small implementation simplification, not grounds to redesign continuation. A row-specific copy makes its schema explicit and avoids the general JSON-cloning behavior, at the cost of needing to update that copy if the row schema later changes. High confidence in the unnecessary deep-clone mechanism for current producers; medium confidence in material performance benefit. No maintained-line reduction is claimed.

#### 2. Low priority: the owner transaction exposes an unused hold callback and roster accessor

**Observed implementation and callers.** `packages/session/src/fleet-owner-continuation.js:10` accepts `onHeld` with a no-op default and invokes it inside the asynchronous begin transaction at `:147`. Its public object also exposes a copying `participants` getter at `:103`. A search of `gui`, `packages`, `examples`, and `tests/client` found no `onHeld` supplier or `continuation.participants` consumer. Both production construction sites (`gui/fleet-session.js:361`, `:1304`) omit the callback. Actual hold coordination waits for `begin()` and checks `phase` and `epoch` (`gui/fleet-session.js:1446`). Tests similarly await begin/connected rather than observe a hold callback (`tests/client/fleet-owner-continuation.test.js:5`, `:36`).

**Why it matters and deletion test.** These are unused extension points on an already stateful public object. The callback creates another place for caller code to run after the journal becomes held but before `begin()` settles; its exception is caught by the transaction's refusal handler (`fleet-owner-continuation.js:148`). There is no present consumer paying for that semantic choice. Removing the callback parameter/invocation and unused getter leaves the observed production path unchanged and modestly reduces the API readers must understand. The benefit is API clarity, not runtime speed or meaningful code-volume savings.

**Preservation and verification.** Keep `phase`, `epoch`, `owner`, `successor`, `frontier`, and `updateRoster`: these are used by service-authorized reconnect, stream authentication, roster baseline seeding, and promotion. Keep `onCommit` and `onError`; those have live consumers. The existing transaction and handoff suites should continue unchanged after the small deletion. No new behavioral test is necessary solely to assert absence of an unused property. Existing begin/held ordering assertions must remain.

**Tradeoff, confidence and limitation.** The package is private (`packages/session/package.json:4`) and known callers do not use these members, giving high confidence for repository-local deletion. An unindexed external consumer could use wildcard exports; no external usage was audited. Keeping these two tiny members is also defensible if an actual external contract is documented. This is minor cleanup, not evidence that the transaction itself is overengineered.

#### Mechanisms that fail the deletion test and should remain

- **Per-tab identity plus persistent adoption and liveness.** `packages/session/src/session-token.js:76` decides among tab identity, persistent identity, and a new identity; `:143` maintains the lease and `:153` performs best-effort cleanup. Removing the registry and using only localStorage restores the documented shared-token ghost-console failure; using only sessionStorage removes cross-restart identity adoption. Reload handling at `:125` supports reusing the same identity while the previous document's lease expires. These are distinct requirements in `pasm/spec/architecture/sessions-replication.yaml:34`, not speculative branches. The pure helpers have meaningful policy tests (`tests/client/session-token.test.js:62`, `:67`, `:78`, `:89`, `:94`). Those tests do not constitute real multi-tab/background-throttling verification, and no such claim is made here.
- **Injected storage keys and browser facilities.** Grid calls the package directly with three Grid keys (`examples/grid/app.js:4`), while the Phoenix adapter sets its established registry namespace and installs the legacy window entry point (`gui/session-token.js:3`). Deleting configurability merges application identity domains. The adapter and one-line continuation re-exports are compatibility ownership boundaries, not redundant implementations. `gui/browser-save-identity.js:19` also legitimately reuses `mintToken`; its stronger Web Locks/SharedWorker exclusivity contract (`:1`) does not make it a drop-in replacement for synchronous phone-session selection.
- **The journal, transaction, and wire split.** The journal manages delivery frontiers and authenticated history. The transaction waits for Rust hold/replay/commit acknowledgements. The wire helper bounds frames and assembles one ordered message per connection. Removing any one shifts necessary behavior into `gui/fleet-session.js`, rather than deleting it. The explicit single-owner-failure and fail-closed rules are recorded in `pasm/spec/architecture/host-mesh-lockstep.yaml:14`. These JavaScript mechanisms do not duplicate simulation time or admission rules.
- **Pending ingress/egress queues and refusal states.** `packages/session/src/fleet-owner-continuation.js:112` and `:129` handle the interval where some survivors have resumed while the coordinator awaits its own commit acknowledgement. `tests/client/fleet-owner-continuation.test.js:69` exercises that asynchronous gap. Replacing this with immediate delivery would remove the guarantee tested there. Bounds, authenticated-tail restrictions, and permanent refusals are part of the safety contract, not optional defensive layering.
- **Membership synchronization and baseline.** `gui/fleet-session.js:567` updates the journal roster and returns its frontier; `:1317` seeds a newly constructed journal from that baseline. The GM redial contract explicitly requires an owner-seeded stream without a second simulation adoption (`pasm/spec/architecture/host-mesh-lockstep.yaml:16`). Deleting `sync` or baseline support because ordinary startup starts at zero breaks that consumer.
- **Chunking.** The escaped-tail regression (`tests/client/fleet-continuation-wire.test.js:5`) checks relay-frame bounds and exact reassembly. Per-connection receivers at `gui/fleet-session.js:811` and `:1239`, including replacement on reconnect at `:1393`, avoid sharing partial messages between connection generations. A single global assembler or unbounded send is not an equivalent simplification.

#### Audit limits

All package source was inspected; surrounding fleet-session code was traced at relevant construction, roster, stream, reconnect and wire seams rather than audited exhaustively. Rust continuation state machines, native relay internals, browser scheduling behavior, deployed services, the complete Grid application, and unrelated GUI identity systems were not independently audited. No builds, tests, browsers, or network calls were run. Existing tests are source evidence of intended coverage, not freshly verified results. Findings concern small removable mechanisms; the inspected contracts do not support deleting the main identity, liveness, journal, transaction or framing machinery.

### Reuse

Module: Browser Session (`packages/session/`). Source root: `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`; source baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e`, with the documented navigation-only HEAD `2de7d5329fba55e06ad00fbd8ca13f0cb4e8c73c`.

Inspected all four package implementation files and its package manifest: `session-token.js`, `fleet-continuation.js`, `fleet-owner-continuation.js`, and `fleet-continuation-wire.js`. Traced their Phoenix compatibility adapters, Grid token consumer, client identity bootstrap, fleet owner/member construction, and native fleet bridge. Compared the Rust runtime continuation gate and snapshot transfer mechanism, native pane identity minting/seeding, and rendezvous capability minting. Read the session-token and continuation test sources, the Session wiki, layer guide, repository vocabulary and operating instructions, and relevant portions of PASM `sessions-replication.yaml`, `host-mesh-lockstep.yaml`, and `reusable-layers.yaml`. No nested `AGENTS.md` was found in the source inventory; `docs/adr/` does not exist in this worktree. This was a read-only inspection; no tests, builds, browser sessions or network operations were run.

#### 1. The client retains a second, weaker token implementation beside the reusable installer

**Evidence and callers.** `packages/session/src/session-token.js:35` provides the canonical 16-byte-to-32-lowercase-hex mint. Its `decideToken` at line 76 distinguishes a reload from a duplicated tab, adopts an unclaimed persistent token, and avoids overwriting another tab's shared identity. `installSessionToken` at line 103 applies that policy with the registry and heartbeat. `gui/session-token.js:1` imports this implementation and supplies the Phoenix registry key at lines 3–5. `client.html:100` loads that adapter, and its ordinary connection bootstrap calls the installed function at lines 4295–4296.

The alternate branch at `client.html:4297` independently reads `sessionStorage`, repeats the crypto/hex conversion at lines 4300–4301, and stores a token at line 4302. Its comment explicitly says it handles a module that failed to load. That is a real fallback, not dead code proved unreachable by the normal module-loading order. However, it implements only an older subset of the identity contract: it cannot detect another tab holding an inherited token, adopt the persistent identity after a browser restart, or publish a lease to protect the fallback token from adoption by another tab. The branch can therefore reintroduce the same duplicate-tab identity collision the package exists to prevent. This is conditional on the installer being unavailable; it is not a claim that the normal path ignores the package.

**Concrete change opportunity.** Make the existing session installer a required dependency of the client identity bootstrap and handle its load failure as an explicit unavailable state, as the transport bootstrap already does at `client.html:4281`. Delete the independent mint-and-store branch once that failure path is wired and tested. If preserving partial-module-load joining is an explicit product requirement, a plain import inside the failing branch does not solve module unavailability: use a build/bootstrap arrangement that delivers the same implementation to that path, rather than maintaining a second policy. Do not merely replace the two-line hex conversion while leaving the divergent token-selection behavior.

**Invariants.** Preserve 32-hex browser minting, per-tab distinct identity, reload reuse, first-tab persistent adoption, Phoenix's `phoenix-live-tabs` namespace, native preseeded opaque tokens, and pure-JavaScript phones. Do not substitute `crypto.randomUUID()` solely to shorten the mint: it changes the browser token format. This recommendation changes degraded-load availability, so that tradeoff needs explicit consideration during implementation.

**Verification.** `tests/client/session-token.test.js:13` checks mint shape; lines 62, 67, 78, 89, 94 and 99 cover token-selection cases through the adapter's re-exports. Those tests do not exercise the installer or the inline module-failure branch. Add a page-level module-unavailable case demonstrating that no Identify is sent with an independently selected identity, plus normal reload/duplicate-tab coverage through the actual bootstrap. Keep a native preseeded-token case if bootstrap loading is rearranged. Confidence: high that there are two implementations and a policy difference; medium on user-visible incidence because no failing-load browser experiment was performed.

#### Existing reuse and similarities that do not justify consolidation

**2. Browser continuation and Rust continuation are cooperating layers, not interchangeable implementations.** `packages/session/src/fleet-continuation.js:22` owns authenticated transport-stream counters, acknowledgement vectors, retained rows and compaction. `reconcileContinuation` at line 118 merges survivor delivery frontiers, with departed-owner proof restricted to the successor's own history at lines 132–140. These counters are explicitly not simulation ticks. `packages/session/src/fleet-owner-continuation.js:32` replays retained frames and requests the runtime's acknowledgement; its commit at line 44 compares the agreed transport frontier and only releases the journal after the runtime answers `committed` at lines 51–53.

By comparison, `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/continuation.rs:75` validates the frozen runtime roster in `begin`, and `validate_commit` at line 142 checks the simulation watermark and whether simulation time has already passed the loss point. It does not retain or merge JS transport envelopes. `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/continuation.rs:1` is only a compatibility re-export of that runtime implementation; it is not another state machine. The adapter in `continuation_systems.rs:26` establishes the simulation hold and processes replay/commit after ingress has drained. Removing the Rust checks because JS already checks epoch/participants would remove the authoritative simulation gate; replacing JS reconciliation with this Rust module would lose the delivery journal.

The actual callers already reuse the JS transaction for fleet owner (`gui/fleet-session.js:361`) and member (`gui/fleet-session.js:1304`), injecting different routes and roster-update behavior. Native fleet peers also supply request and replay-frame callbacks through `gui/native-fleet-peer.js:95` and line 103; they do not require a duplicate native journal. The native replay callback deliberately returns before runtime validation, with the asynchronous runtime acknowledgement completing the contract. Preserve that difference when changing callback handling.

Existing tests include `tests/client/fleet-continuation.test.js:13` (partial delivery), line 37 (compaction), line 80 (unverifiable owner suffix), line 86 (foreign-carried origin); `tests/client/fleet-owner-continuation.test.js:51` (core replay refusal) and line 69 (delayed commit/resumed egress); and `crates/phoenix-simulation/src/lockstep/continuation_systems_tests.rs:42` and line 243 (runtime frontier/hold). These separate test surfaces are justified. No deduplication change is recommended here. Confidence: high for the inspected responsibilities; complete runtime integration and every native bridge response path were not audited.

**3. Continuation wire chunks are not a substitute for, or an accidental copy of, snapshot chunks.** `packages/session/src/fleet-continuation-wire.js:7` chunks a JSON continuation envelope, including envelopes that retain snapshot-carrying frames. Its receiver at line 18 permits one ordered assembly per authenticated connection, refuses interleaving, and rejects nested chunk messages. It measures JavaScript string lengths with 12,000-character chunks and an 8 Mi-character ceiling.

`crates/phoenix-runtime/src/transfer.rs:71` instead sets 32 KiB UTF-8 snapshot chunks and a 64 MiB total ceiling. `SnapshotChunk` at line 103 carries the transfer identity, source, simulation tick, total, whole-payload hash and chunk checksum needed for a canonical snapshot transfer. Reusing it for the outer continuation envelope would add snapshot semantics and a different wire format, size accounting and validation model to an ordered private control exchange. Conversely, the JS assembler cannot replace snapshot integrity checks. The continuation layer also needs to bound the outer JSON escaping expansion after retaining already framed messages. Existing JS wire tests at `tests/client/fleet-continuation-wire.test.js:5` explicitly exercise escaped tails and relay-frame size; line 16 covers refusal cases. No current same-semantics reusable JS framing helper was identified in the inspected session/transport sources. Retain these distinct mechanisms unless a future concrete consumer demonstrates a shared lower-level need; do not introduce a generic cross-language framing abstraction solely for their similar loops. Confidence: high on incompatibility, limited to the inspected transfer/header and JS paths rather than a full snapshot-transfer audit.

**4. Token-shaped values and thin facades are intentional boundaries.** `gui/fleet-continuation.js:1`, `gui/fleet-owner-continuation.js:1`, and `gui/fleet-continuation-wire.js:1` re-export package implementations. They preserve existing caller/test imports, not competing algorithms. Grid directly calls the package installer with separate storage keys in `examples/grid/app.js:2` and line 4. Phoenix's installer wrapper exists to retain the historical registry name and browser global. Removing those wrappers is import migration, not removal of duplicated implementation.

Native panes mint UUIDv4 identities at `src/native_host/panes/identity.rs:94` and seed the ordinary browser storage key at `src/native_host/panes/pane_boot.js:165`. The browser installer accepts an existing opaque token rather than requiring its own mint shape. Native minting is outside deterministic world identity and must survive console recreation; it cannot be replaced by per-tab storage ownership or seeded world RNG. The native minting comments compare it to browser `randomUUID`, but the inspected browser implementation uses random bytes rendered as hex: that commentary discrepancy is not evidence that runtime token formats must be unified. PASM's session connection rules explicitly allow browser hex and native UUID identities.

Finally, `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:196` independently mints 16-byte hex capabilities, superficially matching `mintToken`. The surrounding contract at lines 177–194 deliberately separates these security-sensitive capabilities from the injectable join-code suffix RNG and describes private reclaim/delegation lifetime. Importing Browser Session into Browser Transport to share three lines would violate the declared independent package direction. No existing common lower-layer JS entropy helper was identified; inventing one for these two short conversions would create a new abstraction rather than reuse an existing one. Keep the distinction and cryptographic entropy. Confidence: high. No additional tests are requested solely for retaining these boundaries.

#### Inspection limits

All package production files were inspected, but comparisons outside it were targeted. This report does not claim exhaustive coverage of `gui/fleet-session.js`, native host pane lifecycle, service takeover authorization, Rust snapshot transfer implementation, all host admission paths, or browser scheduling/storage races. ### Architecture

Module: Browser Session (`packages/session/`). Source inspected in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, at the audit's source baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e` with the documented navigation-only HEAD update.

Scope: all four implementation files and the package manifest; Phoenix's four compatibility adapters; identity use in `client.html`, `gui/browser-save-identity.js` and `examples/grid/app.js`; continuation construction, transmission, reception and connection replacement in `gui/fleet-session.js`; the complete four focused session/continuation Vitest files. Also inspected owner-handoff integration coverage and references to Grid and client smoke coverage. Read the required operating instructions, wiki schema/index, domain vocabulary, reusable-layer guide and relevant Session/host-mesh PASM declarations. No nested AGENTS.md was found by the repository file search; `docs/adr/` is absent. No code changes, test runs, browser sessions or network research were performed.

#### 1. Deepen tab identity around the installer that real callers use, and make that Interface the test surface

**Files:** `packages/session/src/session-token.js:98`, `gui/session-token.js:1`, `client.html:4290`, `examples/grid/app.js:4`, `tests/client/session-token.test.js:2`.

**Problem and observed callers:** The actual reusable Interface is already pleasantly small: Phoenix supplies its registry key through the Adapter at `gui/session-token.js:4`, and Grid supplies its three storage keys at `examples/grid/app.js:4`. Both ask `installSessionToken` to hide token selection, persistence and tab liveness. The Implementation, however, is verified mostly below that Interface. The focused test imports only `mintToken`, `pruneRegistry`, `isClaimedByOtherTab` and `decideToken` (`tests/client/session-token.test.js:2–8`); its final test ends at line 103 without calling the installer. That proves decisions given caller-computed facts, but not the integration that computes and preserves those facts.

There is a concrete example of why this distinction matters. The installer promises an in-memory fallback when storage is unavailable (`packages/session/src/session-token.js:101–110`). It guards obtaining the Storage objects, registry parsing, persistence writes and heartbeat writes, but reads of the tab/shared token are outside a guard (`:120–121`). A Storage Adapter whose getter succeeds but whose `getItem` throws can escape that promised fallback before the token is returned. The pure decision tests cannot reveal it. Similarly, reload detection (`:125–134`), heartbeat installation (`:143–151`) and `pagehide` cleanup (`:153–160`) have no focused installer-level verification. This is a source-observed incomplete error contract, not a claim that a particular tested browser currently exhibits the exception.

Phoenix also retains another identity-selection Implementation in the page's missing-module branch (`client.html:4295–4305`). It reuses/mints a sessionStorage token without shared-token adoption or live-tab checks. The branch is explicitly a module-load fallback; that purpose is real, but its different identity guarantees are knowledge the page still owns. It also does not catch storage failures. Grid always goes through the package. The result is less Locality for the promise that concurrent tabs remain distinct while reload/restart can retain identity.

**Solution:** Keep the existing reusable installer Module and its application-specific storage-key Adapters. Complete its storage-error handling and make tests exercise token resolution through that Interface using supplied window/Storage Adapters and controlled time. Resolve the Phoenix bootstrap fallback's intended guarantee alongside that work: either make package availability part of joining readiness or deliberately define and verify the reduced fallback behavior. Do not silently replace it with a second copy of the heartbeat policy. This is a deepening of the existing Module, not a reason to introduce a new general browser-storage framework.

**Benefits:** The same tests can cover the behavior both consumers actually rely on: first-tab persistent adoption, concurrent tab isolation, reload reuse, corrupted registry, unavailable/throwing storage, custom key separation, and lease cleanup. One storage-error fix benefits both consumers. The Interface's error modes become smaller and better specified, and identity knowledge concentrates in one Implementation rather than in pure helpers plus page boot code.

**Invariants to preserve:** 32 lowercase hexadecimal tokens; per-tab reuse across reload; first/only-tab adoption of persistent identity; no concurrent-tab overwrite of the persistent token; best-effort liveness with expiring entries; Phoenix's existing `phoenix-live-tabs` namespace; Grid's separate keys; native-pane seeding compatibility; host-owned Session and Station authority. Browser identity must not absorb Station entitlement or introduce phone WASM.

**Existing verification and missing verification:** The current pure tests are valuable and should remain where they document selection laws (`tests/client/session-token.test.js:61–102`). Native pane tests separately check seeding the session-token key (`tests/client/pane-scripts.test.js:266`). `tests/smoke/four-player-engineering.spec.js:286` deliberately creates a duplicate-token ghost case; it establishes the consequence of shared identity, not complete installer coverage. Search found no test calling `installSessionToken` directly. Missing targeted verification is the installer-level storage/lifecycle matrix and the chosen missing-module behavior. Real timer throttling and simultaneous browser-process storage access would still require browser evidence; fake Storage tests must not claim to prove atomic cross-tab leasing.

**Deletion test, tradeoffs and confidence:** Deleting the installer would push significant policy into both Phoenix and Grid; it earns its keep. Deleting pure helpers merely to shorten the export list would not solve the caller-level verification gap, and `mintToken` has another real consumer in `gui/browser-save-identity.js:19`. Keeping a fail-open page fallback may be an explicit availability choice, so its deletion is not assumed. Confidence is high in the test-surface mismatch and unguarded reads; medium in the appropriate fallback policy until the intended module-load-failure behavior is decided.

#### 2. Concentrate continuation wire lifetime with its reusable framing Implementation

**Files:** `packages/session/src/fleet-continuation-wire.js:6`, `packages/session/src/fleet-owner-continuation.js:31`, `gui/fleet-session.js:361`, `gui/fleet-session.js:811`, `gui/fleet-session.js:1389`, `gui/fleet-session.js:1452`, `tests/client/fleet-continuation-wire.test.js:5`, `tests/client/fleet-owner-handoff.test.js:9`.

**Problem and observed callers:** Chunking is a real reusable mechanism: the sender bounds fragments and total message characters, while the receiver allows one ordered assembly, refuses gaps/interleaving and rejects nested chunks (`packages/session/src/fleet-continuation-wire.js:7–42`). But the Interface includes a lifecycle rule that is enforced outside the Module: one receiver belongs to one authenticated connection. The owner creates a receiver inside `onConnection` (`gui/fleet-session.js:810–814`), whereas the member keeps a receiver across callbacks and must replace it on a new accepted transport generation (`:1389–1394`). The latter comment explicitly records that fragments must never cross into a replacement connection.

The send side has similarly distributed composition knowledge. `createOwnerContinuation` creates serialized control envelopes (`packages/session/src/fleet-owner-continuation.js:31`), but relies on injected send callbacks to frame them. The Phoenix owner/member Adapters wrap those callbacks (`gui/fleet-session.js:365–366`, `:1308`), and broadcast/relay paths repeat the framing obligation (`:468–469`, `:830`, `:968–969`, `:1676–1677`). This is not all needless duplication: destination lookup and relay fan-out legitimately differ. The architectural opportunity is narrower—the reusable wire mechanism's correct lifetime and mandatory framing remain facts every network Adapter must remember.

The current replacement handling is correct by inspection and has an integration regression. This finding concerns Locality and future change cost, not an assertion that partial frames currently survive reconnect.

**Solution:** Deepen the existing continuation wire Module so its connection lifetime and refusal semantics are owned with assembly and frame sizing, and have the owner/member Adapters use that mechanism consistently. Keep physical destination selection, authenticated source determination, pending-GM Admission and Phoenix frame classification in `gui/fleet-session.js`. The package must continue accepting supplied facilities, without importing Browser transport or Phoenix UI. No replacement Interface design is prescribed by this audit.

**Benefits:** Framing rules, replacement cleanup and refusal behavior become a coherent test surface. Maintainers can change a wire bound or recovery-fragment rule without rediscovering every Adapter's initialization and send path. Owner and member are two real network Adapters, so this is a concrete seam with Leverage, not a speculative abstraction for hypothetical applications.

**Invariants to preserve:** Reliable ordered authenticated connections; bounded assembly; strict gap and interleaving errors; a new connection cannot finish an old fragment; control and stream wire compatibility; commit precedes resumed frames; pending GM candidates cannot enter an admitted journal; origin authentication remains distinct from the carrier of relayed bytes. The Adapter must still close/refuse malformed transport input as it does at `gui/fleet-session.js:814` and `:1453`. The raw private GM recovery lane cannot be indiscriminately wrapped as an admitted continuation stream.

**Existing verification and missing verification:** Wire tests cover escaped-tail chunk roundtrip and relay-frame sizing (`tests/client/fleet-continuation-wire.test.js:5–13`), plus a gap, interleaving and sender overflow (`:16–21`). The latter test title mentions nested chunks, but its body does not construct a nested-chunk payload; malformed/nested receive cases deserve explicit assertions if this Module is deepened. Owner-handoff integration exercises partial-message loss before reconnect for relay and WebRTC (`tests/client/fleet-owner-handoff.test.js:9`, `:35–38`). Preserve that composition coverage rather than replacing it with isolated framing tests. Add direct wire-lifetime tests for abandoning a partial assembly on replacement and keeping one alive across duplicate acceptance for the same generation. Those would verify the package-level behavior that currently depends on the member callback.

**Deletion test, tradeoffs and confidence:** Deleting chunking would relocate necessary size, ordering and assembly policy into callers; the Module earns its keep. Moving all fleet routing into the package would instead violate the declared seam and reduce reuse. A deeper wire Module adds value only if it removes the repeated lifetime/framing obligations; a wrapper that merely renames existing calls would be Shallow. The scope is modest, and current integration coverage lowers urgency. Confidence is high in the distributed Interface obligations, medium that consolidation will simplify both concrete Adapters enough to justify a refactor.

#### Existing depth and intentional seams retained

The journal and transaction Modules should not be flattened just because both describe continuation. `packages/session/src/fleet-continuation.js:22` owns bounded retention, acknowledgement compaction, source checks and frontier reconciliation; `packages/session/src/fleet-owner-continuation.js:9` owns asynchronous hold/replay/commit coordination and buffering. Their tests cross their respective Interfaces. In particular, `tests/client/fleet-continuation.test.js:71–91` verifies why a survivor cannot supply another origin's proof, while `tests/client/fleet-owner-continuation.test.js:69–113` verifies delayed commit and resumed traffic. These are distinct rules with meaningful Depth. Deletion would redistribute complexity into network callbacks.

The retained `beginPromise` and one-owner-loss transaction comment (`packages/session/src/fleet-owner-continuation.js:8`, `:140–149`) should not be reported as an unsupported repeated-failover bug: PASM explicitly specifies bounded single-failure continuation (`pasm/spec/architecture/host-mesh-lockstep.yaml:14`). Likewise, holding on unverifiable history, refusing unsupported membership change and maintaining separate delivery counters are requirements, not excess caution to remove.

The `gui/fleet-*.js` re-export files preserve compatibility import paths; the identity Adapter additionally preserves Phoenix storage naming and its page-global bootstrap. Their apparent shallowness is intentional extraction scaffolding under `pasm/spec/architecture/reusable-layers.yaml:7`. Grid demonstrably uses the identity installer, not the browser continuation transaction. Its existence proves reuse of that identity mechanism, not independent exercise of every export. The Phoenix-prefixed continuation wire marker remains a compatibility value; no evidence here justifies a protocol rename or configurable marker solely for cosmetic generality.

#### Inspection limits

All Browser Session implementation files were read. #### 1. Reuse the existing continuation-envelope encoder for chunk envelopes — a small genuine deletion

It already imports from the owner-continuation module at line 1, so using the encoder introduces no new dependency edge.

Both real directions use this chunk sender: the host's continuation send callback is in `gui/fleet-session.js:361-366`, ordinary owner broadcast at lines 467-469, and the member callback at lines 1304-1308. Receivers are per connection at line 811 and reset on replacement transport generations at lines 1390-1393.

**Opportunity.** Change the wire module's import from the raw discriminator to `continuationEnvelope`, then send `continuationEnvelope('chunk', { index, count, text: ... })`. Delete only the repeated outer-envelope construction. Keep the slicing and chunk-count calculation exactly where they are. This is reuse of an existing encoder, not a new utility or a relocation of the wire protocol.

**Estimated savings.** Approximately one to two physical production lines, depending on ordinary formatting; one fewer independently maintained envelope schema. The benefit is small and is best taken during adjacent work, not presented as a meaningful package-size reduction.

**Invariants.** Preserve `continuation`, `kind`, `body` property ordering and values, the exact version discriminator, 12,000-character slices, maximum message length, synchronous ordered send calls, and the short-message direct-send return value. For the small encoder substitution, an assertion that a generated chunk equals the existing canonical envelope would directly establish serialized compatibility. No tests were executed for this audit.

**Tradeoff and confidence.** High confidence in equivalence for the observed code; negligible LOC impact. There is no case for introducing another abstraction to obtain this saving.

**Evidence.** The journal validates and retains reliable-stream history in `packages/session/src/fleet-continuation.js:22-114`; reconciliation validates a collection of survivor reports at lines 118-152. Their checks overlap in vocabulary but protect different boundaries. Local receive verifies authenticated origin, conflict, contiguous delivery and acknowledgement shape at lines 52-67. A foreign survivor carrying a row is expressly not sufficient origin proof at lines 132-135.

The journal's `rows` and reconciler's `authorizedRows` are not interchangeable redundant maps. The union map identifies conflicting retained bytes and distinguishes an existing-but-unverifiable row from a wholly missing row; the authorized map controls what may actually be replayed. **Disposition, tradeoff and confidence.** No deletion recommended. Sharing generic map/queue machinery or replacing stateful paths with a compact dispatch table is likely to move or compress code while making sequencing harder to inspect. High confidence that the highlighted apparent duplication is required by present behavior. This is not a claim that every line is irreducible; no larger reduction was supported by the inspected callers and tests. Savings: none established.

`gui/fleet-continuation.js:1`, `gui/fleet-owner-continuation.js:1` and `gui/fleet-continuation-wire.js:1` are one-line re-export facades, not extra implementations. `gui/session-token.js:3-7` additionally preserves Phoenix configuration and the legacy browser entry point. This was a static audit with no code changes, builds, browser sessions, network requests or test execution.
