### Overengineering

Module: Cloud Workers — `worker-rendezvous/` and `worker/` together.

Scope: all eight files in the two Worker directories: both entry points, the rendezvous registry/relay compatibility exports, and both development/demo Wrangler configurations. Source inspected in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, against the audit's stated code baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e` and documentation-only HEAD `2de7d5329fba55e06ad00fbd8ca13f0cb4e8c73c`.

Supporting inspection: `packages/transport/src/service-relay.js`; selected registry lifecycle, relay dispatch, and public API sections in `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js`; `gui/connection-manager.js`; rendezvous Worker request-gate tests, relay contract tests and registry lifecycle tests; native protocol endpoint checks; references from the local rendezvous server, smoke fixtures and deployment workflow. Read the repository orientation material, networking/build wiki material and the relevant rendezvous PASM declarations. There is no nested AGENTS.md in these directories and `docs/adr` is absent. No tests, builds, deployments or network requests were run.

#### 1. Candidate: terminal cloud sockets enter a recoverable mailbox state that this adapter can never recover

**Evidence and callers.** `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:107` dispatches the frames already produced by the shared registry. At `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:121`, a missing socket or a socket whose ready state is not OPEN causes `registry.setWritable(to, false)`, followed by skipping the frame. The immediately preceding comment explicitly says both conditions are terminal and there is no re-arming path. The ordinary `drop` callback at `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:170` instead deletes the socket and calls `registry.disconnect`, and both close and error events use it.

The state being entered is more general than this cloud adapter needs. `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:1185` supports making a peer writable again and draining its held frames. `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:1108` enqueues each relay frame and `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:1116` immediately flushes it. While writable, the registry does not accumulate a cross-message backlog. Once unwritable, the hub retains per-pair reliable and snapshot queues, snapshot-drop totals and overflow state (`packages/transport/src/service-relay.js:146`, `:265`, `:310`, `:331`). The Worker has no call restoring writability. The checked local server also does not supply a writability recovery caller.

**Deletion test and current cost.** On this adapter, deleting the *intermediate terminal-but-buffering state* appears preferable to treating it as temporary backpressure: a socket already closing cannot consume the retained bytes. The first frame that discovers the closed socket has already left `relay.drain`; setting writability afterward cannot rescue that frame. Subsequent arrivals may retain payloads, shed snapshots and eventually generate overflow diagnostics until the ordinary close/error cleanup arrives. This is real lifecycle machinery, but on the cloud path it buys no eventual delivery. The common short close-event interval may make its runtime cost small; this is not a measured performance problem.

**Concrete change opportunity.** Investigate routing a positively terminal destination through the existing idempotent socket-departure cleanup immediately, rather than marking it as recoverably unwritable. That would remove one intermediate lifecycle state from the Worker. Do not simply remove the condition and continue dropping output: reliable delivery failure needs to become an explicit departure. Do not delete the shared mailbox hub or its configuration as part of this narrow change. Its reusable contract, sender-side queue advice and independent tests remain relevant outside this one adapter.

**Behavior that must survive.** Cleanup must remain idempotent when close/error arrives afterward, must notify surviving participants, and must retain the registry's existing host reclaim and frozen-fleet continuation behavior. A cloud signalling departure must not imply that an already working peer-to-peer DataChannel has died. Failure of a single client must not take other clients' relays down. Dispatching departure notifications during a batch must not create recursive cleanup loops or deliver stale frames to a removed connection. Neither delivery-class admission nor opaque game payload handling may change.

**Tests and missing verification.** `tests/client/rendezvous-relay.test.js:356` proves immediate delivery while writable; `:371`, `:399`, `:416` and `:433` exercise artificially unwritable destinations, resumed drains and pair-isolated overflow. Those tests justify the shared primitive; they do not demonstrate recovery of a terminal Worker socket. `tests/client/rendezvous-worker.test.js:13` imports only the outer fetch handler and stubs the Durable Object binding; its origin tests do not drive `RendezvousRegistry.dispatch` or socket events. A simplification needs adapter-level tests for missing/CLOSING sockets, close+error duplication, host loss with grace/delegation, and a batch containing multiple frames for the departed peer. The existing registry tests around reclaim (`tests/client/rendezvous-registry.test.js:725`) should remain intact.

**Tradeoffs, confidence and limitation.** Medium confidence as a simplification candidate, not an approved deletion. PASM explicitly describes the current unwritable-mailbox design (`pasm/spec/architecture/rendezvous-transport.yaml:60`); changing the adapter's policy would require updating that design statement, not silently violating it. The finding is that the Worker supplies a terminal condition to a recoverable state machine, not that bounded queues are inherently unnecessary. Cloud runtime event timing and fleet teardown ordering were not executed or exhaustively proved here. Total shared-code savings are not estimated; the initial useful reduction is in reachable lifecycle states, not necessarily file length.

#### 2. Small confirmed deletion: the Durable Object retains environment configuration it never uses

**Evidence and callers.** `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:100` accepts the runtime's constructor arguments and `:101` stores `env` as `this.env`. The constructor creates its registry from imported authored data and initializes the socket map. No method reads the saved environment, and searching the Worker directory finds no other `this.env` reference. Origin checks and the Durable Object binding lookup use the outer fetch handler's own `env` at `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:180` and `:231`.

**Deletion test and opportunity.** Delete the stored field. Preserve whatever constructor signature is useful for the Cloudflare adapter; the runtime's provision of arguments is not itself excess machinery. Removing this one assignment loses no current behavior and makes it clear that the live object does not consult deployment configuration. Its cost today is small: an unnecessary retained reference and misleading instance state. This is housekeeping, not a reason to redesign the Worker.

**Invariants, verification and tradeoffs.** Keep authored join-code data injection, instance registry lifetime and socket ownership unchanged. Outer origin tests should behave identically. No special new test is warranted solely for removing an unread assignment; a future adapter lifecycle test from finding 1 would exercise construction naturally. High confidence within inspected source; no claim about out-of-repository reflective inspection of the object. One maintained statement can be deleted.

#### Machinery checked and justified by current requirements

- **Two independently deployed services pass the deletion test.** The credential Worker makes outbound HTTP requests and owns provider secrets (`worker/src/index.js:26`, `:36`); the rendezvous Worker owns live WebSockets and a Durable Object (`worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:99`). Combining them would couple deployment and lifetime without eliminating their distinct behavior. The stateless/stateful split is expressly recorded in `pasm/spec/architecture/rendezvous-transport.yaml:42`.
- **The two credential sources are implemented behavior, not a speculative provider framework.** `worker/src/index.js:69` invokes the two small source functions concurrently with `Promise.allSettled`; the response keeps the existing flat array and tolerates one provider failing. `gui/connection-manager.js:66` fetches the service and consumes that array. `worker/src/index.js:53` normalizes the Cloudflare response and `:87` keeps upstream diagnostics out of the body. Removing a source, aggregation, or normalization would narrow supported configured behavior. There is no plugin registry or provider-class hierarchy to remove. No direct automated test importing `worker/src/index.js` was found; fixture tests of deployment judgments are not proof of this handler's partial-failure behavior. This is a coverage limitation, not evidence that provider redundancy is excessive.
- **The origin logic has distinct callers.** The rendezvous upgrade path requires an allowed Origin (`worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:219`), whereas health supports the documented bare-curl check (`:192`). The credential Worker emits CORS headers for browser HTTP responses (`worker/src/index.js:97`). Deleting the health distinction, replacing upgrade checks with CORS alone, or forcing both Workers through one identical policy would lose current behavior. The request-gate tests directly cover the meaningful distinction.
- **Development/demo manifests identify separate deployments and secret scopes.** `worker/wrangler.demo.toml:1` and `worker-rendezvous/wrangler.demo.toml:1` select distinct Worker names and origins. The demo workflow consumes its own TURN URL (`.github/workflows/deploy-demo.yml:223`). Similar declarations do not establish unnecessary configuration, and introducing a manifest generator would add machinery at this size. The SQLite class declaration is a platform deployment requirement, not evidence that the application has built an unused database layer; no storage API is used by the object.
- **The one-line registry and relay files are compatibility exports with real consumers.** `worker-rendezvous/src/registry.js:1` and `worker-rendezvous/src/relay.js:1` forward to package-owned mechanisms. Tests and `scripts/rendezvous-dev-server.mjs:65` still import the registry path. Removing these files is not deletion of a second implementation; it would require consumer migration and abandon a deliberate compatibility surface.
- **Record grace, rotation, TTL and fleet ownership cannot be dismissed as generic lifecycle excess.** The registry's lazy expiry at `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:365`, the reclaim tests at `tests/client/rendezvous-registry.test.js:725`, rotation tests at `:900`, and the fleet continuation rationale in `pasm/spec/architecture/rendezvous-transport.yaml:61` tie these states to current reconnect and ownership behavior. The Worker delegates these rules rather than maintaining its own second policy. Removing them merely to shrink the service would change player-visible semantics.

#### Unexplored areas and limits

The complete shared registry's fleet takeover/security transitions, all browser/native reconnect implementations and all smoke-test routing were not exhaustively audited. They were followed where needed to establish Worker consumers and preserve deletion constraints. The exported Worker code and deployment configurations were read in full; shared-package behavior was sampled around lifecycle and relay operations. Actual deployed secret availability, Cloudflare socket semantics beyond the repository's documented contract, provider response variants and production event ordering were not verified. No production usage or performance claims are inferred from test-only writability toggles.

The browser's `gui/connection-manager.js:63` consumes the flattened Worker result, appends STUN entries and chooses OpenRelay only on failure. It does not duplicate provider credential minting. Actual page callers are `client.html:4576` and the host ICE latch at `server.html:6534`. Its use of package `hasRelayServer` at `gui/connection-manager.js:1` is also already reuse. Preserve the flat response shape and partial-provider-success behavior if modifying the Worker: changing the response into a provider envelope would break that consumer contract. The browser tests mentioned above validate the consumer side; provider response normalization and partial-failure aggregation need direct Worker tests before a future credential-layer refactor.

**Cloudflare and local socket adapters are intentionally separate.** `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:144` uses `WebSocketPair`, `accept`, browser-style events and a 101 response carrying the client socket. `scripts/rendezvous-dev-server.mjs:65` reuses the service mechanism but owns Node sockets, RFC 6455 framing and local impairment queues. Both Workers' deployed entry-point source and Wrangler files were inspected. The full registry's fleet takeover/reclaim state machine was not audited branch by branch; it is package-owned, not a second Worker implementation. Rust native transport counterparts, every browser transport transition, the complete deployment workflows, remote provider schemas and live Cloudflare runtime behavior were not exhaustively examined. ### Architecture

Module: Cloud Workers (`worker-rendezvous/` and `worker/`). Source inspected in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, code baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e` (the supplied subsequent HEAD changes documentation only).

Scope: both Worker entry points and deployment configurations; registry/relay compatibility facades; selected implementation and public operations of `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js` and `service-relay.js`; browser credential acquisition and startup callers; local rendezvous and smoke adapters; request-gate, registry, relay, fleet-takeover, credential-client and deployment-check tests. Read project instructions, wiki schema/index, domain vocabulary, reusable-layer guide, networking orientation, the architecture skill and LANGUAGE.md, and the relevant rendezvous/reusable-layer PASM declarations. No nested AGENTS.md was found; `docs/adr/` is absent. No builds, tests, deployments or network probes were run.

1. **Deepen the TURN credential acquisition module so partial provider availability also means bounded completion.**

   **Files:** `worker/src/index.js:26`, `worker/src/index.js:36`, `worker/src/index.js:68`; `gui/connection-manager.js:62`; `client.html:4576`; `server.html:6533` and `server.html:6541`; `tests/client/connection-manager.test.js:77`; `tests/client/rendezvous-checks.test.js:45`.

   **Problem and observed callers:** The Worker has two real provider adapters: Metered returns an array, while Cloudflare returns an `iceServers` envelope that the Worker normalises. Their concurrent calls are joined with `Promise.allSettled`; successful entries are flattened and a nonempty result yields 200 even if the other provider rejects. This already provides useful depth and hides provider secrets from the pure-JavaScript Console. However, neither provider fetch nor the aggregation has an application-controlled deadline. A fulfilled provider cannot produce a response until its sibling settles. The browser's credential module likewise awaits fetch and response decoding without a deadline; the client awaits this before its subsequent diagnostics/join wiring, and host transport initialisation awaits the same module. Thus the effective interface includes an implicit wait for every configured upstream, despite the stated intent that either source suffices. OpenRelay fallback handles rejection/non-OK responses, not a request which remains pending.

   This is a concrete completion-policy gap, not a claim that a provider currently hangs forever: platform/network limits may eventually terminate it. No deployed latency evidence was collected. The architecture friction is that provider latency escapes the credential module and becomes a startup concern for its callers.

   **Solution:** Give the existing credential-acquisition module ownership of bounded provider completion, cancellation and normalisation. Retain concurrent providers and return the successful results after bounded collection. Also bound the browser's acquisition of the Worker response so a stalled Worker/network cannot prevent its existing fallback decision. Keep this within the current credential module and Phoenix adapter rather than adding a generic provider framework or making every page implement timeout logic. This is a behavioural deepening candidate; no replacement interface is proposed here.

   **Benefits:** Better locality for the meaning of “credential acquisition finished”; more leverage from the existing `fetchIceServers` interface for both page callers. Failure tests can cover the delivered behaviour rather than forcing callers to know which provider or network stage stalled.

   **Deletion test and seam:** Deleting provider adapters would move their URL, authentication, response-shape and error knowledge into the fetch handler; deleting the browser acquisition module would move fallback policy into both pages. These modules earn their keep. Deepen their behaviour instead of removing them. Two providers are real adapters at the upstream seam, not speculative extensibility.

   **Preserve:** flat ICE array response; no client-side secrets; independent optional provider configuration; current Cloudflare credential TTL; successful partial responses; no-store/CORS behaviour; STUN base list; existing distinctions among `worker`, `openrelay`, and no-relay diagnostics. Do not turn a successful STUN-only response into OpenRelay fallback incidentally: the client explicitly tests that distinction.

   **Verification:** `tests/client/connection-manager.test.js:78`, `:88`, `:97`, and `:109` exercise network rejection, HTTP failure, successful relay data and STUN-only data through the caller's interface. Deployment-check fixtures validate synthetic Worker responses, not provider execution. Searches of tests/scripts found no direct import of `worker/src/index.js` testing its provider orchestration. Add exported-fetch tests with controlled upstream completion: one provider stalls while another succeeds; both fail or exceed the budget; one is unconfigured; response decoding stalls; cancellation produces no later response mutation. Exercise browser acquisition with a pending Worker request and confirm bounded fallback. These are proposed checks, not executed evidence.

   **Tradeoffs/confidence:** A deadline trades some slowly arriving relay choices for predictable startup; its value must fit existing ICE connection timing rather than being arbitrary. High confidence in the missing application-level bound and real callers; medium confidence in operational priority without field timing data.

2. **Make socket delivery and terminal failure one testable lifecycle inside the rendezvous adapter.**

   **Files:** `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:99`, `:107`, `:121`, `:125`, `:144`, `:165`; `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:992`, `:1037`, `:1185`, `:1248`; `scripts/rendezvous-dev-server.mjs:182`, `:257`, `:368`; `tests/client/rendezvous-worker.test.js:13`; `tests/client/rendezvous-relay.test.js:371`.

   **Problem and observed callers:** The shared registry has a deep interface: connect/receive/disconnect translate transport events into ordered outgoing frames. The Cloudflare adapter separately owns the socket map, JSON decoding, ready-state inspection, refusal-close application and event-driven removal. That is appropriate platform ownership, but its two failure paths differ: a missing/non-open socket reports `setWritable(false)`, whereas a thrown `ws.send` is swallowed and cleanup is left to a future close/error event. Meanwhile `flushRelay` has already drained frames into the outgoing batch before dispatch runs. Reporting unwritable only after inspecting that batch cannot restore already-drained frames. Those details are not proven by tests calling the registry's `setWritable` directly.

   `tests/client/rendezvous-worker.test.js:13` imports only the default HTTP handler and stubs the Durable Object binding; it deliberately never runs `RendezvousRegistry.fetch`. Registry relay tests instead set writability before sending and prove queue behaviour at that seam. The local Node adapter has its own dispatch and explicit `endSocket` path that removes the connection and calls `registry.disconnect`, while the smoke shim also dispatches directly to the pure registry. Therefore broad registry and browser-smoke coverage does not establish the Cloudflare socket-lifecycle behaviour.

   **Solution:** Concentrate send-result handling, terminal connection removal, registry notification and refusal-close sequencing within the rendezvous socket-lifecycle module, and make that actual module exerciseable with controlled sockets. Clarify the policy for a failed send after draining: it must become a visible terminal connection failure where appropriate, rather than leave the abstraction relying indefinitely on an unverified later callback. Keep Cloudflare upgrade mechanics platform-specific. Assess common lifecycle behaviour with the existing Node adapter, but do not merge their socket implementations or impairment machinery merely because both dispatch frames.

   **Benefits:** Locality for the invariant that a failed physical connection is reflected in registry presence and relay lifetime. Better leverage from adapter-level tests: one test can establish that a socket failure actually produces the protocol departure/grace effects promised by the shared registry. This narrows a current testing seam gap without changing the game protocol.

   **Deletion test and seam:** The registry and relay hub pass the deletion test: deleting them would spread typed lookup, admission, fleet continuity, queue-class and resource-limit rules across Cloudflare, local tooling and tests. The socket adapter also cannot simply disappear. The opportunity is to deepen lifecycle ownership rather than create another forwarding wrapper. Cloudflare and local Node sockets are real differing adapters; their differences justify retaining platform-specific implementation.

   **Preserve:** `relay-peer` reaches the host before `relay-ready` reaches its joiner (`service-registry.js:1064`); opaque game payloads and unchanged Admission on the authoritative host; reliable overflow terminates only the offending joiner's relay; snapshot shedding remains oldest-first; close/error cleanup stays idempotent; refusal reason is sent before policy close where possible; explicit teardown differs from socket death and fleet grace; no credentials or private codes enter diagnostics. Preserve the documented limitation that Cloudflare exposes no `bufferedAmount`: extracting a module cannot manufacture real send-buffer backpressure.

   **Verification:** Existing relay tests cover bounded mailboxes, setWritable transitions, reliable overflow isolation and relay teardown (`tests/client/rendezvous-relay.test.js:356`, `:371`, `:399`, `:429`, `:460`). Fleet tests cover owner loss and grant rules (`tests/client/rendezvous-fleet-takeover.test.js:65`, `:94`, `:159`, `:166`). Missing evidence is at the Cloudflare adapter interface: accepted socket→ready; decoded malformed message→refusal; send throws while socket still reports open; closing target during a dispatch batch; duplicate error/close; refusal followed by closure; owner failure delivering expected departure/grace notifications exactly once. A controlled platform adapter or suitable Worker harness should exercise the real event wiring, not just another hand-built registry loop.

   **Tradeoffs/confidence:** Avoid a generic networking abstraction that is larger than the adapter itself. A focused harness and local lifecycle concentration may suffice. High confidence in the coverage gap and distinct failure branches. The hypothesis that a swallowed send error can leave a live registry entry materially longer than expected depends on runtime event guarantees; this audit did not establish Cloudflare's exact callback timing and does not claim a reproduced production loss bug.

**Inspected choices that should remain:**

- Separate Workers are justified. `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:3` and `pasm/spec/architecture/rendezvous-transport.yaml:42` declare separate lifetimes/state models; credential HTTP and live rendezvous sockets do not warrant one combined module merely because deployment is on Cloudflare.
- `worker-rendezvous/src/registry.js:1` and `relay.js:1` are compatibility re-exports of reusable package owners. Real callers include registry/fleet tests and `scripts/rendezvous-dev-server.mjs:65`. Their shallow shape is intentional migration scaffolding; deleting them without caller migration supplies no meaningful behavioural depth.
- A single Durable Object registry is a declared lookup requirement, not automatically a scaling mistake: `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:93` and the PASM rendezvous declaration require distinguishing namespace/version/unknown across the same live set. No measured capacity evidence warrants changing that architecture.
- Similar CORS formatting across the Workers does not justify sharing their admission decisions. Rendezvous explicitly refuses WebSocket upgrades by Origin; the credential Worker supplies browser CORS headers. The health route also permits absent Origin. Consolidation that silently makes these policies identical would change behaviour.
- The relay module's bounded queues are not ornamental merely because Cloudflare cannot inspect buffer length. Its package implementation documents the narrower mailbox role and sender-side backpressure (`packages/transport/src/service-relay.js:43`). Keep this distinction in any lifecycle change.

**Coverage limits:** The Worker entry points were read in full. Shared registry/relay code was inspected around construction, exported operations, lifecycle, relay attach/drain and related tests, not every fleet authorisation branch. Browser transport state machines and native `phoenix-transport` relay handlers were traced as protocol counterparts rather than exhaustively audited; simulation authority, complete fleet recovery, codecs and deployment automation were not independently verified. Local Node RFC6455 parsing and impairment algorithms were sampled only to understand the adapter seam. Existing test definitions were read, not executed. No assertion here establishes live Worker deployment state, provider availability, Cloudflare runtime guarantees or production performance.

#### 1. Delete the unused Durable Object environment field — definite but one-line saving

**Evidence and callers.** `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:100` accepts the Durable Object constructor arguments, then `:101` stores `env` as `this.env`. The remaining constructor initializes the registry with the imported authored format (`:102`) and a socket map (`:104`). Neither `dispatch` (`:107`) nor instance `fetch` (`:144`) reads `this.env`; a search of the Worker source and relevant tests/tooling found no access to that property. The actual request-origin and Durable Object binding configuration is consumed by the separate exported Worker handler, using its own `env` at `:180`, `:219`, and `:231`. Wrangler binds the exported class by name at `worker-rendezvous/wrangler.toml:25` and `worker-rendezvous/wrangler.demo.toml:15`; retaining an instance environment field is not what supplies that binding.

**Opportunity and maintained-line effect.** Remove `this.env = env`. This deletes exactly one executable line rather than relocating logic. The constructor can retain its platform-shaped parameters if that makes the lifecycle recognizable; shortening its parameter list is not another line saving and need not be part of the change. Do not delete the constructor: it initializes the per-object live registry and socket map.

**Invariants.** Keep per-Durable-Object isolation, imported join-code configuration, random connection identities, and the Worker-level origin gate/binding lookup. This change must not attempt to persist registry state or reinterpret socket death as explicit host closure.

**Tests and missing verification.** `tests/client/rendezvous-worker.test.js:13` imports the default Worker handler and `:16` supplies a stub Durable Object binding. Its endpoint cases do not instantiate `RendezvousRegistry`, so they are not direct constructor coverage. The absence of any field read is the deletion evidence; a new test asserting the absence of the property would merely mirror implementation and is unnecessary. Existing request-gate tests remain relevant regression coverage, but a Worker-runtime lifecycle exercise would be separate evidence if later changes touch the constructor's real initialization.

**Tradeoff/confidence.** High confidence; negligible maintenance benefit. Appropriate opportunistic cleanup, not a standalone architectural project. No claim of material size reduction.

#### 2. Consolidate the identical CORS response-header policy only if a small shared source helper remains a net deletion

**Evidence and callers.** `worker/src/index.js:93` and `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:71` each implement the same response policy: parse the comma-separated allowlist, default missing/empty configuration to `*`, trim and discard blank entries, choose wildcard or the matching request origin, otherwise use the first configured entry, and return `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`, `Access-Control-Allow-Methods: GET, OPTIONS`, and `Vary: Origin`. Rendezvous already factors parsing into `allowlist` (`:42`), also used by `originAllowed` (`:63`). TURN duplicates that parsing inline (`worker/src/index.js:94`). TURN calls its helper for OPTIONS (`:61`) and its credential response (`:87`); rendezvous calls its helper across OPTIONS, health, and HTTP refusals (`:184`, `:187`, `:203`, `:208`, `:212`, `:227`).

**Opportunity and maintained-line effect.** One small shared source module could own allowlist parsing and response-header construction, imported by both entry points. Keep rendezvous's upgrade admission decision local. The duplicated header functions occupy 15 and 14 physical lines; factoring out one implementation can remove roughly 10–15 production lines after imports/export overhead, depending on formatting and retaining the existing useful explanations. That is a rough implementation-only estimate, not a promise of repository-wide net savings after tests. Count the helper itself: merely moving both functions, introducing a package/dispatcher framework, or adding a configurable HTTP middleware stack does not satisfy this lens. Placement should respect existing layer policy and must not make the TURN Worker import the rendezvous entry point, authored join data, or registry machinery.

**Invariants and compatibility differences.** Shared headers do not imply shared authorization. TURN currently performs GET credential requests even for an unmatched origin; browser CORS mismatch is its response policy. Rendezvous explicitly refuses unmatched or absent origins before upgrades when a finite allowlist is configured (`worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:219`). Its health endpoint permits absent Origin, and the current wildcard branch allows it for upgrades too (`:65` precedes `:67`). Preserve these actual branches rather than silently tightening them while deduplicating. Likewise preserve TURN's plain 405 response without CORS (`worker/src/index.js:64`) versus rendezvous's JSON 405 with CORS (`worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:186`). Do not combine entire request handlers. Dev allowlists differ intentionally: TURN has port 3911, while rendezvous additionally includes 3000 and 8080 (`worker/wrangler.toml:14`; `worker-rendezvous/wrangler.toml:17`).

**Tests and missing verification.** `tests/client/rendezvous-worker.test.js:35` covers both socket routes with absent, foreign, and allowed origins; `:58` and `:71` cover health's no-Origin behavior and allowed-origin reporting; `:87` covers a plain non-upgrade GET. These do not fully assert the response-header matrix. `tests/client/rendezvous-checks.test.js:125` checks diagnostics against a fabricated mismatched CORS response, not the Worker implementation. No direct test importing `worker/src/index.js` was found in the inspected client tests. Before consolidation, add focused contract coverage shared across the two exported handlers for matched/foreign/missing origins, wildcard/default configuration, whitespace, and preflight headers, preserving endpoint-specific statuses. Keep the existing origin-gate tests independent of the header helper so an incorrect shared policy cannot validate itself.

**Tradeoff/confidence.** High confidence that the response policy is duplicate; medium confidence that consolidation is worth doing for LOC alone. A new shared-file dependency and contract tests may exceed the small production saving. This is a conditional candidate, not a recommendation to add general-purpose infrastructure. Retaining two small local helpers is reasonable if there is no appropriate shared service-support location.

#### Reductions examined and rejected

- **Do not remove the compatibility re-exports.** `worker-rendezvous/src/registry.js:1` and `worker-rendezvous/src/relay.js:1` already contain no duplicate implementation. Callers include `tests/client/fleet-session-harness.js:16`, `tests/client/rendezvous-transport.test.js:24`, and `scripts/rendezvous-dev-server.mjs:65`; Worker `index.js:38` also uses the registry facade. Deleting two lines would require caller churn and abandon a deliberate compatibility path, not remove maintained registry behavior.
- **Do not collapse the two credential providers.** Metered is a GET with a query credential and an array result (`worker/src/index.js:26`); Cloudflare is a POST with bearer authorization, a six-hour TTL, and object/array normalization (`:36`). The parallel `Promise.allSettled` (`:68`) deliberately preserves one-provider success, with a flat array body, diagnostic error header, and 502 only when no servers remain (`:72`–`:88`). `gui/connection-manager.js:59` consumes that contract and falls back on failure; `tests/client/connection-manager.test.js:78`, `:88`, and `:97` distinguish failures from usable Worker credentials. A generic provider table would mostly move these provider differences into callbacks/configuration and create additional machinery. No supported line saving identified.
- **Do not merge the independently deployed Workers or generate the four tiny configurations to remove repeated keys.** Stateless credential minting and the live Durable Object have different lifetimes. Demo/dev configurations select distinct Worker names, origins, and secret scopes; rendezvous additionally declares its binding and SQLite migration (`worker-rendezvous/wrangler.toml:25`, `:29`; demo `:15`, `:19`). The demo TURN deployment explicitly names `wrangler.demo.toml` (`.github/workflows/deploy-demo.yml:374`). A generator would replace a few repeated scalar declarations with another maintained deployment mechanism, while migration identity and secret scopes must remain explicit.
- **Do not shorten socket cleanup and dispatch by removing guards/catches.** The socket-presence/readiness guard and `setWritable(false)` at `worker-rendezvous/src/index.js:121` are the adapter's backpressure input. Registry `packages/transport/src/service-registry.js:1185` connects this to relay draining; `tests/client/rendezvous-relay.test.js:371` and `:399` distinguish lossy snapshot shedding from reliable overflow termination. The idempotent drop closure (`index.js:165`) is installed for both close and error, and registry `disconnect` (`service-registry.js:1248`) preserves the socket-loss lifecycle. Sending a refusal before closing with 1008 (`index.js:125`, `:135`) is observably different from just dropping the socket. These lines encode requirements, not removable boilerplate.

#### Coverage limits

No tests, builds, network probes, provider calls, or deployments were run. Deployed origin values, secret provisioning, Wrangler bundling across a proposed shared module, and real Cloudflare event ordering were not verified. Shared registry takeover/reclaim logic, complete local server WebSocket framing, and Rust/native counterparts were not audited exhaustively. This scope yields one definite one-line deletion and one small conditional deduplication; it does not support a larger maintained-line reduction without moving required behavior elsewhere or reducing verification.
