No confirmed overengineering removal is recommended. The following numbered assessments record the plausible deletion candidates and why they do not pass the deletion test under the current requirements. The distinction matters here: this is an executable proof of reusable foundations, so a mechanism can be necessary even when the smallest playable moving-square game would not need it.

1. **Peer ordering, readiness and digest history are deliberate proof machinery, not an unnecessary game framework.**

   Evidence: `examples/grid/src/lib.rs:32` composes `LockstepSession`, `DigestLedger` and `CommandQueue<OrderedMove>`. `submit` at line 49 assigns canonical order and a delayed application tick; `receive` at line 61 admits valid moves from configured origins; `advance` at line 73 waits at the readiness barrier, drains due commands, clamps the position and records the digest. Native hosting constructs a single-host Grid at `examples/grid/src/main.rs:23`; browser hosting does the same at `examples/grid/src/browser.rs:11`.

   The apparently unused generality has a real second caller: `examples/grid/src/grid_tests.rs:4` creates two differently numbered peers and demonstrates delayed and duplicate delivery, a blocked barrier, and order-sensitive boundary movement. The digest comparison and recovery case at line 70 deliberately creates divergence and then restores agreement. PASM explicitly requires the separate game to demonstrate command ordering, digest divergence and recovery (`pasm/spec/architecture/reusable-layers.yaml:11`).

   Deletion test: removing peer membership, delayed commands or the ledger would simplify the standalone UI but destroy the independent proof. Replacing these with Grid-specific counters or arrays would also stop exercising the extracted runtime mechanisms. Their current maintenance cost is concrete—Grid must retain admission, queue restoration and clock boundaries—but corresponds directly to the stated purpose. Retain the composition; do not introduce a generic game engine interface around it.

   Invariants and verification: preserve tick/order determinism, duplicate suppression, readiness blocking, admission checks and digest agreement after recovery. The cited Rust tests exercise these through the real Grid API. They do not demonstrate networked multi-host Grid lockstep; both executable hosts use no peers. This limits the proof's network scope, rather than making its in-memory peer tests expendable. Confidence: high that this is justified; no deletion candidate.

2. **Checkpoint transfer and checkpoint persistence cover different requirements and should not be collapsed into one minimal save path.**

   Evidence: `examples/grid/src/lib.rs:103` captures version, state, next issuer sequence and pending commands. `recovery_chunks` at line 111 calls the shared chunker, while `recover` at line 152 drives the shared receiver before invoking `restore`. `restore` at line 119 validates and prepares replacement state before assigning it at line 144. The network `Recovery` output instead carries a complete checkpoint string (`examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:20`); the native host returns it at `examples/grid/src/main.rs:44`, and the JavaScript browser host at `examples/grid/app.js:49`.

   The chunk helper methods are exercised by the independent tests, not by the browser console. `examples/grid/src/grid_tests.rs:35` proves pending-command and issuer-sequence continuation; line 53 checks damaged transfer rejection without changing the running checkpoint; line 70 exercises recovery from divergent state. The browser console merely displays the returned checkpoint state (`examples/grid/app.js:20`), consistent with a stateless console. This is an important boundary on what the smoke test proves.

   Deletion test: removing chunk recovery because the wire presently sends a whole checkpoint would remove the independent integration with shared transfer/reassembly. Removing pending commands or the issuer sequence would break checkpoint continuation. Moving these checks into runtime-only tests would no longer prove another game can consume the foundation. Retain both the simple console checkpoint response and the independent chunk-transfer exercise; adding chunk framing to the console solely to make the two paths look alike would add machinery without a demonstrated need.

   Persistence is likewise intentionally platform-specific: native optional saving uses `phoenix_platform::native_file::replace` at `examples/grid/src/main.rs:68`, whereas browser hosting uses localStorage at `examples/grid/app.js:37` and line 58. The optional native path is documented at `examples/grid/README.md:29`; deleting it would remove the example's exercised platform file operation. Sharing those stores would require a new abstraction over unlike environments.

   Verification and limitations: the smoke test covers reliable checkpoint receipt and browser host reload (`tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs:60`, line 75). It starts the native host with only two arguments at line 42, so it does not verify optional native file persistence or process restart. The Rust invalid-recovery test corrupts a chunk; it does not exhaustively test each syntactically valid but semantically invalid checkpoint branch. These are coverage limits, not evidence that defensive restoration code should be deleted. Confidence: high that the mechanisms are justified.

3. **The small codec module and target adapters are shallow but currently useful boundaries.**

   Evidence: `examples/grid/src/codec.rs:3` and line 6 wrap JSON encode/decode and normalize errors to strings. Their actual callers include Grid checkpointing (`examples/grid/src/lib.rs:104`, line 120), native relay protocol encode/decode (`examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:49`, line 59), and browser state export (`examples/grid/src/browser.rs:19`). The repository explicitly requires `serde_json` imports to remain in `codec.rs` (`AGENTS.md:897`).

   Deletion test: inlining these two wrappers would remove a tiny module but spread the same serialization/error handling across three owners and violate the explicit repository rule. This is not speculative codec pluggability: there is no codec registry, injected strategy, or alternate implementation. Keep the eight-line seam. There is also no reason to import Phoenix's game codec, which would defeat independence.

   `GridProtocol` implements the transport's required `Profile` and `RelayProtocol` contracts (`examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:30`), and the native caller selects it concretely at `examples/grid/src/main.rs:29`. `GridHost` exports the handful of methods JavaScript actually invokes (`examples/grid/src/browser.rs:5`; `examples/grid/app.js:36`, line 48, line 53, line 58). These wrappers bridge native relay typing and wasm-bindgen; removing them would require shifting or duplicating the same boundary logic elsewhere. Rust and JavaScript stamp checks at `examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:39` and `examples/grid/app.js:38` are target counterparts, not evidence of an unnecessary abstraction.

   Invariants and verification: preserve the tagged JSON vocabulary, Grid-specific compatibility stamp, error propagation, native-only socket feature selection, and lazy host-only WASM loading. Unit checkpoint tests and the two smoke paths exercise these indirectly; there are no explicit Grid codec round-trip or compatibility-refusal tests in the inspected source. This audit does not claim perfect cross-target validation parity. Confidence: high for retention; no replacement interface recommended.

4. **The browser host/console lifecycle and separate join configuration are proportionate to an independently runnable example.**

   Evidence: `examples/grid/app.js:1` imports reusable browser transport and session mechanisms directly, not Phoenix GUI adapters. Session storage keys are Grid-specific at line 4. The join path at line 27 loads no WASM; the host path loads the generated module dynamically at line 36. `stop` at line 25 closes transport, cancels ticking, clears peers and frees the WASM host. Both modes share rendering and message reception without a frontend framework. `examples/grid/index.html:8` exposes the service address used by both local instructions and the smoke test.

   Deletion test: removing mode switching or its cleanup would alter supported host/join/disconnect behavior. Eagerly loading the host module would break pure-JavaScript console loading. Reusing Phoenix application configuration would compromise the example's independent identity: `examples/grid/join-codes.json:9` supplies separate namespaces, and the smoke test supplies this exact file to the local service at `tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs:40`. Service-address configuration is exercised at smoke-test line 51 and native startup arguments at line 42. These are actual variations, not speculative options.

   Retain this direct implementation. The file has one render function, one message receiver and two connection entry points; introducing host/console controller classes or a generic UI store would increase the conceptual surface. Preserve shared movement semantics, host authority, session continuation and reliable recovery versus snapshot delivery. The smoke test exercises those happy paths, including console reload; it does not exhaustively cover rapid mode changes while the asynchronous WASM import is outstanding, storage failures, or hostile frames. Those untested cases are not proposed as overengineering removals. Confidence: high.

5. **The smoke test's local service/process setup is necessary integration setup rather than an excess test framework.**

   Evidence: `tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs:14` starts and tracks child processes; line 21 waits for startup output; line 25 serves source assets with WASM MIME support and path containment; line 40 starts the existing rendezvous service; line 42 starts the native binary. Small `open` and `join` helpers at lines 49 and 53 serve both host paths. The test drives native movement/recovery/reload at line 57, then browser-host movement/reload at line 67. Cleanup closes the browser and server and kills the children at line 82. CI builds both hosts and invokes the integration proof (`.github/workflows/ci.yml:376`, line 380, line 387).

   Deletion test: mocking transport, removing the static server, or dropping either host would materially weaken the declared native/browser reuse proof. There is no elaborate fixture hierarchy to collapse: the script directly composes three real services and a browser. The configurable base port at line 9 supports isolated local runs; its relay port is derived once. The generated `examples/grid/pkg/` files are build output, ignored by `examples/grid/.gitignore:1` and produced by `scripts/build-grid.mjs:6`; generated glue size is not maintained abstraction overhead.

   Retain the integration setup and assertions. A screenshot at smoke-test line 79 is only a diagnostic artifact, not visual verification; deleting that one call would offer negligible simplification and lose a debugging aid. Existing assertions establish state movement, checkpoint receipt, reload continuity, and absence of console WASM requests during the native phase. The no-WASM assertion occurs at line 64 before the browser-host phase, and there is no explicit assertion on which direct/fallback network route succeeded. These bound the current evidence. No new test harness is proposed merely to address those limits. Confidence: high that the current setup is proportionate.

Unexplored areas: generated WASM machine code and generated binding internals were excluded from maintained-source analysis. Shared runtime, platform, transport, session and rendezvous implementations were followed only far enough to identify the contracts consumed here; this is not an audit of their internal abstractions. Production Phoenix GUI and host implementations were not evaluated for general consolidation opportunities. No behavioral success is claimed from execution in this audit.


Audit 70/72 · Agent: `/root/audit70_grid_reuse` · Lens: reuse

### Reuse

Module: Grid reuse example, Rust and JavaScript together.

Scope inspected: all authored files in `examples/grid/` (`Cargo.toml`, README, HTML, join-code configuration, `app.js`, and the six Rust source/test files), plus `tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs`. Compared these with the reusable Runtime command/session/digest/transfer APIs, Rust Transport protocol/connection/codec mechanisms, Platform atomic-file API, browser Transport adapter, and browser Session token installer. Inspected static-serving implementations in the browser fleet matrix and Workshop launcher, and the matching mixed-matrix implementation/call site. Generated `examples/grid/pkg/` WASM and bindings were identified as build products, not treated as separately maintained implementations.

Audit source: `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`, code baseline `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e`; the recorded HEAD documentation update does not change audited code. Orientation included repository instructions, wiki schema/index, vocabulary, reusable-layer guide, and `pasm/spec/architecture/reusable-layers.yaml`. No nested AGENTS.md was found in the inspected example/test/package/crate trees; `docs/adr` is absent. This was a read-only source audit; no tests, builds, browser sessions or network requests ran.

#### 1. Browser-host admission partially recreates the reusable connection registry, with different identity semantics

**Evidence and callers.** `examples/grid/app.js:40` adds each transport connection to the host's peer set. Lines 42–49 implement admission as a connection-local `identified` boolean, set whenever an Identify payload contains a nonempty string token. A subsequent Move calls `GridHost.move_piece`, and Identify/Recover returns a checkpoint. Neither a token-owner map nor a connection incarnation participates in that path.

The native implementation already delegates these same physical-connection concerns to the foundation. `examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:36` selects `OpaqueIdentity`; its `identity` method at line 52 extracts the Identify token. `examples/grid/src/main.rs:29` instantiates `RelayTransport<GridProtocol>`. That transport binds identity through the registry at `crates/phoenix-transport/src/relay.rs:280` and resolves the current authenticated sender at line 300. The reusable `ConnectionRegistry` at `crates/phoenix-transport/src/connections.rs:59` validates through `validate_token` (line 45), forbids a connection changing identities, and prevents a superseded connection from reclaiming ownership (`bind`, line 109; `sender`, line 133). The token rule rejects whitespace/control characters and more than 64 Unicode characters, whereas the JavaScript boolean accepts all nonempty strings.

**Why this matters for reuse.** This is a partial reinvention of an existing game-independent mechanism, not merely the necessary JavaScript/Rust wire counterpart. The authoritative browser host already loads Rust/WASM (`app.js:36`), and `phoenix-grid` already depends on Transport on both targets (`examples/grid/Cargo.toml:12`). Sharing that existing pure registry is possible without adding WASM to consoles or importing any Phoenix game type. The existing simplified browser gate teaches a different reconnect/identity model than the native example: two physical peers claiming the same token can remain capable of sending browser-host moves, while native ownership transfers to one current connection. This is a semantic difference established by source inspection, not a reproduced browser failure.

**Concrete opportunity.** Extend the host-only adapter in `examples/grid/src/browser.rs:6` to use the existing registry for connection open, identification, sender lookup and close, and let JavaScript retain responsibility for actual channel callbacks and teardown. Use the existing Grid input codec and protocol where inbound messages cross that Rust seam (`examples/grid/src/codec.rs:6`, `examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:11`), rather than creating another generic admission engine in JavaScript. This can also avoid a second interpretation of the Move envelope at `app.js:48`; `GridHost.move_piece` currently exposes numeric arguments rather than the typed protocol (`browser.rs:13`). The exact adapter surface should be chosen during implementation; no new foundation abstraction is needed to justify the reuse.

**Invariants and compatibility.** Preserve the Grid-specific stamp and vocabulary, current movement validation, reliable recovery versus snapshot state delivery, rejection before movement, and opaque identities with no Phoenix reserved-token policy. Map supersession to close of the old physical peer and do not let its later close remove the replacement. Continue importing WASM only from the host button, so `app.js:27` remains a pure-JavaScript console path. Adopting registry validation intentionally tightens currently permissive browser behavior; document and test that parity decision rather than claiming a behavior-neutral substitution.

**Tests and missing verification.** `examples/grid/src/grid_tests.rs:4` proves delayed/duplicate ordered movement but operates on Grid commands, not physical identity. `tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs:62` covers a native client reload, and lines 67–77 cover browser hosting, movement and host persistence. Neither asserts duplicate-token replacement, changed identity on a live link, stale input after replacement, or malformed/oversized tokens across both hosts. Add matched host-adapter cases for those behaviors and retain the no-console-WASM request assertion at smoke line 64. Current tests were inspected, not executed.

**Tradeoff, confidence and limits.** Medium priority; high confidence in the duplication and differing semantics, medium confidence in payoff for this deliberately small example. A registry adapter adds host-boundary plumbing, so this is more about demonstrating the real reusable identity mechanism consistently than reducing line count. Grid intentionally has one shared marker and no Station permissions; do not import Phoenix Session admission to make it look more elaborate. The recommendation applies to physical ownership, not a hypothetical per-player game model.

#### 2. The smoke harness maintains another copy of the repository's static test server

**Evidence and callers.** `tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs:25` implements URL decoding, root containment, index-file selection, a MIME table, file reads and 404 handling in its own HTTP callback. It listens at line 39 and closes at line 83. `scripts/fleet-browser-matrix.mjs:239` implements the same server responsibilities in `serve(directory, port)`, consumed by the fleet runner at line 432. `scripts/fleet-mixed-matrix.mjs:121` has another `serve` implementation, consumed at line 225. This overlap is an actual implementation match, not inferred from similar function names.

**Why this matters for reuse.** These are test/tool mechanisms independent of the spaceship game. Root containment, MIME correctness for WASM, and asynchronous listen failure handling need consistent maintenance. For example, the fleet implementation rejects its startup promise on server error (`fleet-browser-matrix.mjs:251`), while Grid's promise only resolves on successful listen (`grid-smoke.mjs:39`). Sharing the mechanism would make a port-bind failure follow one tested behavior. This is a small maintenance opportunity, not evidence that Grid needs a production web framework.

**Concrete opportunity.** Extract the existing matrix server into a side-effect-free Node tooling helper and consume it from Grid and the two matrix runners. Do not import an entire executable matrix script: its game orchestration is unrelated and its implementation is currently local rather than exported. Keep Grid's rendezvous startup, native process launch, page-driving assertions and lifecycle ownership in its harness. An extracted helper is justified by these existing copies; no external dependency is proposed.

**Compatibility differences and invariants.** Grid serves the repository root because its ES modules import `packages/` directly; matrix runners serve a built distribution. Parameterize the root rather than forcing Grid to build a Phoenix distribution. Grid chooses `index.html` only for a URL ending in `/` (`grid-smoke.mjs:31`); matrix serving detects directories with `stat` (`fleet-browser-matrix.mjs:246`). Grid's containment uses `relative`/`isAbsolute`; matrix uses an absolute prefix with a separator. Choose and test a common root-containment rule while consciously preserving or accepting the directory-URL change. Preserve loopback binding, configurable port, `.wasm` MIME, default octet-stream behavior, 404 handling and explicit server cleanup.

The Workshop launcher is a related but less exact candidate: `scripts/serve-workshop.mjs:27` handles GET/HEAD and rejects other methods, adds no-store and content-length headers, has a broader MIME map, maps `/` to `workshop.html`, and optionally opens a browser at line 59. Those are real policy differences. Do not force it into the extraction unless its options stay simpler than its current dedicated launcher.

**Tests and missing verification.** The Grid browser smoke exercises successful loading of HTML, ES modules, JSON and WASM, but has no assertions for containment refusal, missing files, malformed encoding, directory URL behavior, or address-in-use rejection. Preserve its native/browser behavior assertions and use focused helper tests for file serving and listener failures. The matrix callers also need their served-root behavior preserved; their complete game flows were not reviewed or run.

**Tradeoff, confidence and limits.** Low priority; high confidence in duplication, moderate confidence that extraction is worthwhile independently of other tooling cleanup. The local Grid handler is only about a dozen lines, so a broad configurable server abstraction would defeat the purpose. The benefit is one maintained serving implementation across the existing callers, not merely fewer lines inside Grid.

#### Confirmed reuse and justified separate implementations

- **Movement and command scheduling:** `examples/grid/src/lib.rs:36`, `:55`, `:70` and `:77` use `CommandQueue` for sequence issuance, keyed deduplication and canonical draining. Those mechanisms already live at `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/commands.rs:46`, `:53` and `:62`. Grid's cardinal-move admission (`lib.rs:177`) and 8-by-8 boundary clamp (`:78`) are its own game rules. Replacing them with Phoenix entity movement would break the reason for the example.
- **Readiness and digest history:** Grid uses `LockstepSession::may_simulate` at `lib.rs:74` and `DigestLedger::record` at `:83`, with the real reusable implementations at `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/session.rs:256` and `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/digest.rs:126`. Its explicit state-byte encoding and `grid/1` digest namespace (`lib.rs:87` and `:96`) define its own deterministic state, not a duplicate of Phoenix's authoritative census. The delayed/duplicate-delivery test at `grid_tests.rs:4` and divergence/recovery test at `:70` are valuable integration witnesses even though foundation units test their own algorithms.
- **Recovery:** `lib.rs:111` calls `transfer::chunk`, and `:152` feeds `SnapshotReceiver::accept`; it does not duplicate CRC, chunk ordering or whole-payload hashing. `crates/phoenix-runtime/src/transfer.rs:331` owns these checks. The small `complete` accumulator is legitimate caller glue: `accept` takes completed state and returns `Accepted::Complete` at lines 399–402, so blindly replacing Grid's loop with `receiver.finish()` afterward would not be equivalent. Grid owns checkpoint version, cell bounds and pending-command admissibility (`lib.rs:119`), and stages restoration before mutation. The persistence tests at `grid_tests.rs:35` and corruption check at `:53` should stay.
- **Host persistence:** Native writes already use `phoenix_platform::native_file::replace` with a Grid-specific temporary prefix (`main.rs:69`; helper `crates/phoenix-platform/src/native_file.rs:33`). Browser persistence uses localStorage (`app.js:37`, `:58`). These are intentionally different platform adapters; a shared file API would not eliminate a duplicate algorithm here.
- **JSON seams:** `examples/grid/src/codec.rs:3` and `:6` are tiny serde_json wrappers used by Grid protocol, checkpoint and WASM output. Transport's codec functions (`crates/phoenix-transport/src/codec.rs:3`, `:10`, `:18`, `:25`) accept rendezvous/handshake frame types, not Grid game payloads. They are not an interchangeable generic codec. Retain the application seam instead of importing Phoenix model codecs or making Transport interpret game messages.
- **Browser session and network setup:** `app.js:1` imports the real rendezvous host/joiner implementation, and `:4` calls `installSessionToken` with independent storage keys. The reusable installer at `packages/session/src/session-token.js:103` owns token persistence and tab coordination. Grid's independent join-code JSON supplies a separate namespace; sharing Phoenix's authored identity configuration would undermine independence. The snapshot-channel fallback at `app.js:55` reflects the exported raw-channel adapter contract (`packages/transport/src/rendezvous-transport.js:342`), so it is not evidence of an overlooked foundation send-snapshot helper.
- **Separate test composition:** The smoke harness's native host and WASM host cases test two different adapters and preserve the pure-JavaScript console requirement. Phoenix-specific fixtures such as Station selection and game boot are not appropriate reuse targets merely because they also drive Playwright pages. Grid is intentionally an independent consumer.

#### Coverage limits

The authored Grid implementation and its supplied tests were read in full. Foundation internals were inspected at the actual reuse boundaries and selected relevant implementations, not audited exhaustively. Browser transport's complete signalling/retry state machine, all fleet-matrix scenarios, generated WASM bindings/binary contents, and unrelated production HTTP hosts are outside this report. No execution evidence or measured savings is claimed. The two opportunities above preserve the declared independent second-game boundary; none requires a dependency on Phoenix Model, Content, Simulation or Presentation.


Audit 71/72 · Agent: `/root/audit71_grid_architecture` · Lens: architecture

### Architecture

Module: Grid reuse example (Rust and JavaScript together).

Scope: `examples/grid/` and `tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs`, inspected in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`. Source baseline is `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e`; the audit instructions identify HEAD `2de7d5329fba55e06ad00fbd8ca13f0cb4e8c73c` as a documentation-only navigation update.

Inspected all tracked Grid Rust files, page implementation, HTML, manifest and README, plus the complete smoke script. Traced the consumed command queue, lockstep session, relay protocol and connection ownership implementations, the browser rendezvous interface and session-token interface, and layer checks. Read the architecture skill and LANGUAGE.md, project vocabulary, wiki schema/index/reusable-layer navigation, project instructions, and `pasm/spec/architecture/reusable-layers.yaml`. No nested AGENTS.md was found under the inspected examples/tests/packages/crates trees; `docs/adr` does not exist in this checkout. The large root instructions were consulted for layer, testing and audit rules. No code changes, tests, builds, browser sessions or network research were performed.

#### 1. Deepen the Grid host protocol module so browser hosting does not independently choose admission semantics

**Files:** `examples/grid/app.js:38-57`, `examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:8-60`, `examples/grid/src/browser.rs:13-26`, `examples/grid/src/main.rs:29-61`; relevant reusable implementation in `crates/phoenix-transport/src/relay.rs:272-305` and `crates/phoenix-transport/src/connections.rs:42-54,114-150`.

**Problem.** Native hosting consumes `GridProtocol`: typed JSON decoding, compatibility checks and extraction of the Identify token are provided through the reusable relay interface. The relay binds that token to a physical connection and obtains its current sender identity before yielding `Event::Received`. The reusable registry rejects whitespace/control characters, tokens over 64 characters, identity changes on an already bound connection and superseded connections. This is substantive behavior hidden behind the native transport interface.

Browser hosting does not consume the equivalent game protocol behavior. `app.js:45-49` parses arbitrary JSON, tests only that an Identify token is a nonempty string, and stores a boolean. It neither binds a token nor remembers which token was supplied. Consequently a second Identify can change identity, duplicate-token connections can continue issuing moves, and whitespace or overlong tokens can identify in the browser path even though native refuses them. These are observed differences in implementation, not a claim of a demonstrated exploit. Grid has no per-player ownership or private game state, which limits their current practical impact.

There are additional signs of the same shallow seam. `Input::Identify` requires both `token` and `name` during Rust decoding (`protocol.rs:12`), but browser hosting ignores `name`; raw movement fields are passed directly through the WASM integer interface (`app.js:48`, `browser.rs:13`) instead of being decoded as `Move`. Compatibility stamp/refusal text and the Recovery envelope are also restated in JavaScript. The browser snapshot audience is the physical `peers` set populated before Identify (`app.js:41,54`), whereas native `Target::All` routes through identified owners. The browser transport explicitly hands the caller a connection after its compatibility handshake, leaving Identify to the caller (`packages/transport/src/rendezvous-transport.js:344-350,454-460`); using that transport alone does not supply native identity parity.

**Solution.** Concentrate Grid's game-message decoding, compatibility and response decisions in the Grid host protocol module, used by both host adapters. Keep physical connection ownership in the transport layer, but make the browser adapter's obligations explicit and exercise them against the native behavior. Avoid importing Phoenix Station or Session policy into this independent example. This is a candidate direction, not a proposed replacement interface.

**Benefits.** Better locality for the rules defining a Grid console; greater leverage from the existing typed vocabulary; a single protocol test surface can check movement refusal, Identify and Recover independently of rendering. Native and browser are already two real adapters, so this is not an invented seam for hypothetical consumers.

**Deletion test and tradeoffs.** Deleting the browser's handwritten message decisions without replacing their behavior would remove admission and recovery, so they are not dead code. Moving them behind the Grid host interface concentrates duplicated knowledge rather than merely moving lines. Target-specific connection management still needs adapters; forcing the browser through native relay machinery would be inappropriate. For this tiny public game, a full session framework would cost more than it returns; start with explicit parity and narrow shared decisions.

**Invariants and verification.** Preserve pure-JavaScript consoles, dynamically loaded host-only WASM, the `grid/1` compatibility decision, reliable recovery, snapshot delivery with reliable fallback, and application-owned movement validation. Current Rust tests exercise Grid movement/recovery, not `GridProtocol` or hostile wire messages. The smoke script verifies ordinary movement and reconnect (`tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs:57-77`) but never sends a missing name, changed token, duplicate token or malformed move. Add the same protocol cases across the two host adapters, including verifying that a superseded connection cannot command or receive an owner's updates if identity parity is the intended example contract.

Confidence: high for the differences; medium for requiring every native identity rule in a deliberately small example. The design promises reusable transport admission, but does not spell out a Grid-specific duplicate-player policy. At minimum the example should explicitly define that contract rather than accidentally deriving two contracts from two adapters.

#### 2. Put browser host activation and teardown behind one lifecycle seam

**Files:** `examples/grid/app.js:7,25-38,40-60,62-66`, `examples/grid/index.html:9-14`, `tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs:67-77`.

**Problem.** The page owns transport, WASM Grid and timer in mutable globals. `stop()` tears down only resources currently stored in those globals. Host activation calls `stop()` and then suspends twice while loading and initializing WASM (`app.js:36`). The Host, Join and Disconnect controls remain available during this interval. There is no operation identity or cancellation check after either await.

This leaves a concrete lifecycle gap: a Disconnect or Join during host startup cannot prevent the old startup from later installing a Grid, transport and timer. Two overlapping host starts can each create resources but overwrite the sole global handles; all interval bodies and peer callbacks then read the same mutable `grid`. The exact visible result depends on asynchronous completion order, but the absence of ownership/cancellation is evident in the implementation. The initial `stop()` is not a cancellation mechanism for resources that do not exist yet.

**Solution.** Give each host activation responsibility for its own resources and for deciding whether it is still the active page operation before publishing them. Concentrate mode transitions, eventual initialization failure and teardown in one browser lifecycle module or cohesive local implementation. The DOM handlers should request a transition rather than each participating in resource ownership. Do not introduce a generic application state-machine framework for a page this small.

**Benefits.** Locality for WASM lifetime, transport lifetime and timer lifetime; a small lifecycle test surface can demonstrate that cancelled initialization remains cancelled. The page gains depth through one operation that owns asynchronous setup and its inverse, rather than exposing the temporal ordering to every button handler.

**Deletion test and tradeoffs.** `stop()` itself earns its keep: deleting it duplicates cleanup across three callers and the error path. The problem is that its interface hides synchronous cleanup but not the asynchronous lifecycle that callers assume it controls. Deepen that ownership instead of deleting the helper. Extra lifecycle code is justified by actual user controls and two real modes, but dependency injection for all canvas/DOM operations would be unnecessary.

**Invariants and verification.** Preserve lazy host-only WASM import, immediate disabling of movement on disconnect, checkpoint restoration before hosted simulation begins, and closing all owned peers. Existing smoke coverage exercises a completed host start and a subsequent page reload; it does not hold WASM initialization pending while another operation is requested. Test a deferred initialization followed by Disconnect, Join and a second Host action, verifying observable final mode and absence of stale updates. A browser smoke case can then validate a rapid mode switch without making every timing edge depend on network timing.

Confidence: high for the missing lifecycle ownership; the exact resource-leak and stale-update manifestations are static-analysis predictions, not reproduced failures.

#### 3. Align the cross-target verification seam with the capabilities the example claims to demonstrate

**Files:** `examples/grid/src/lib.rs:103-160`, `examples/grid/src/grid_tests.rs:3-97`, `examples/grid/src/main.rs:23-26,44-51,68-73`, `examples/grid/src/browser.rs:22-26`, `examples/grid/app.js:20-23,37,49,58`, `tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs:42-81`, `examples/grid/README.md:3-6,39-42`.

**Problem.** Grid usefully exposes checkpoint/restore and chunked recovery, but the verification seams currently prove different things without joining them. Rust unit tests call `recovery_chunks()` and `recover()` directly, compare digest histories, and manually advance peer readiness. Both running hosts are single-host sessions (`main.rs:23`, `browser.rs:11`); their network Recovery message is a whole checkpoint string, not the reusable chunk transfer. The console Recovery handler parses and paints the checkpoint but does not restore a host. This is reasonable behavior for a read-only console, but its “Checkpoint received” status is not evidence of host recovery.

The smoke script's reliable checkpoint assertion (`grid-smoke.mjs:60-61`) checks only that status. Its browser reload assertions check coordinates, not pending command/issuer preservation or digest agreement. Native is launched without a checkpoint path (`:42`), so the platform atomic replacement path is not exercised at all. A regression in native save integration can coexist with successful unit recovery and successful smoke movement. This is a gap between individually tested mechanisms and their actual host adapters, not evidence that the mechanisms themselves are broken.

**Solution.** Make the example's verification explicitly distinguish console checkpoint delivery, host persistence restoration, and in-memory multi-peer runtime proofs. Deepen the host-adapter verification around checkpoint/restore behavior shared by the native and browser adapters. Exercise the native optional checkpoint argument with a temporary file and process restart, and compare a known continuation after restoring the same checkpoint on the two targets. Keep the existing direct chunk-transfer unit proof; adding a network chunk protocol merely to make the smoke test use it is not warranted for such small state.

**Benefits.** Greater leverage from the public Grid checkpoint interface and better locality of cross-target expectations. A maintainer can see exactly which reusable-layer behavior is demonstrated by which adapter, and persistence regressions are caught where the real adapter invokes platform storage.

**Deletion test and tradeoffs.** The standalone example and its tests earn their keep: deleting them removes an independent consumer that cannot accidentally inherit Phoenix game dependencies. Combining their assertions with the large Phoenix smoke suite would weaken that evidence. The opportunity is to improve the test surface, not delete the example or invent a second game protocol. Restart coverage adds test time and process cleanup duties; keep it focused rather than creating a general host orchestration framework.

**Invariants and verification.** Preserve delayed canonical ordering, rejection without partial mutation, pending moves and issuer sequence restoration, host-only WASM, and no dependency on Phoenix Model/Content/Simulation/Presentation. The four Rust tests already cover canonical movement, pending move recovery, damaged transfer refusal and divergence repair. Retain them. Missing adapter verification includes native file persistence/restart, cross-target continuation from a checkpoint, and console no-WASM verification after the browser-host phase: `expect(clientWasm).toEqual([])` currently occurs only at line 64, before that phase. These are finite additions at real interfaces, not a request to test private call counts.

Confidence: high for the coverage gap. No tests were run, and no claim is made that native persistence or cross-target determinism currently fails. Networked host-to-host lockstep is not implemented by this example and should not be implied by its ordinary console smoke test.

#### Deliberate seams retained and scope limits

- The Rust `Grid` module is already a useful composition seam: application movement and checkpoints sit over reusable ordering, readiness, digest history and transfer. Its implementation does not need Phoenix game types. The manifest and JavaScript layer checks support that independence (`examples/grid/Cargo.toml:11-25`, `scripts/check-layers.mjs:51-64`). Turning Grid into a consumer of Phoenix simulation would defeat its purpose.
- Native socket/file handling and WASM bindings are real target-specific adapters. Their different mechanics are required, not duplication to eliminate. `GridProtocol` makes reusable relay behavior available through a game-owned vocabulary; it is not a pointless wrapper.
- `codec.rs:3-7` is shallow, but currently concentrates Rust JSON error conversion for protocol, persistence and browser exports. Deleting it would mostly redistribute calls and would not address the browser protocol knowledge identified above. No material architectural improvement is claimed from removing eight lines.
- Generated `examples/grid/pkg/` files are present but untracked; they were not treated as authored modules. No generated WASM was inspected. The full rendezvous state machine, browser networking internals, platform atomic file implementation, all foundational unit tests and other Phoenix host adapters were not exhaustively audited. Reusable implementations were followed only far enough to establish the concrete Grid interface contracts described above.


Audit 72/72 · Agent: `/root/audit72_grid_loc` · Lens: loc

### Reducing maintained lines

Module: Grid reuse example (Rust and JavaScript together).

Scope: all maintained files in `examples/grid/`, plus `tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs`, inspected in `C:/Users/jkeyw/.codex/worktrees/reusable-game-layers/project-phoenix-v2`. Source baseline is `07fc2e025bb2f5385b2fadf4fb468039b77c0b4e`; the inspected HEAD is `2de7d5329fba55e06ad00fbd8ca13f0cb4e8c73c`, whose documented difference is navigation documentation only.

Files inspected: `examples/grid/src/lib.rs`, `examples/grid/src/protocol.rs`, `examples/grid/src/codec.rs`, `examples/grid/src/browser.rs`, `examples/grid/src/main.rs`, `examples/grid/src/grid_tests.rs`, `app.js`, `index.html`, `join-codes.json`, `Cargo.toml`, `.gitignore`, `README.md`, and the complete grid smoke script. Orientation included root `AGENTS.md`, `wiki/SCHEMA.md`, `CONTEXT.md`, `wiki/index.md`, `wiki/concepts/reusable-layers.md`, and `pasm/spec/architecture/reusable-layers.yaml`. No nested `AGENTS.md` was found; `docs/adr/` does not exist in this checkout. Targeted dependency/caller inspection included `scripts/build-grid.mjs`, `scripts/check-layers.mjs`, `scripts/layer-policy.mjs`, runtime command and digest implementations, the browser transport connection adapter, and static-serving portions of three other scripts.

#### 1. Conditional opportunity: consolidate the smoke test's static HTTP handler with existing test-tool copies

**Evidence and current callers.** `tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs:25–36` implements URL parsing, decoding, root containment, directory-index selection, MIME selection, file reading, and HTTP error responses. Line 39 binds that server to loopback; line 83 closes it. The browser opens this server at line 50 to load the example, its shared JavaScript imports, and the host-only WASM package.

The same mechanism occurs in `scripts/fleet-browser-matrix.mjs:239–253`, called at line 432, and `scripts/fleet-mixed-matrix.mjs:121`, called at line 225. Those are concrete additional maintained implementations, not hypothetical consumers. The mixed-matrix function is compressed onto one physical line, but contains a complete third server implementation. `scripts/serve-workshop.mjs:27–50` is another related implementation, with more distinct behavior.

**Change opportunity.** In a coordinated test-tool cleanup, extract the common static-serving mechanism into a small Node-only helper and have the grid smoke and fleet matrix scripts use it. Remove their duplicated request handlers rather than merely moving the grid handler to a new file. Keep the helper outside `examples/grid/`: it is test infrastructure, and the demonstrator's browser imports must remain restricted to the reusable packages. Do not import the fleet runner itself, since it is an executable script with unrelated work and lifecycle.

**Compatibility differences that prevent immediate substitution.** Grid selects `index.html` only when the URL pathname ends in `/`; the fleet handlers inspect filesystem directory metadata, including requests without a trailing slash. Grid's MIME table has four entries; the fleet tables additionally serve CSS and TOML. Grid uses `relative`/`isAbsolute` for containment, whereas the fleet handlers use an absolute-path prefix check. Grid's listen promise has no explicit server-error rejection, whereas the fleet helpers do. The Workshop server also implements GET/HEAD restriction, a special root page, extra response headers, and a broader MIME table. It should not be folded in blindly just to increase the nominal savings.

**Behavior to retain.** Preserve loopback binding, configurable grid ports, repository-root resolution independent of the invoking directory, traversal refusal, JavaScript and WASM MIME types, 404 behavior, and teardown ownership. Preserve Grid's actual request behavior unless an intentional change is separately tested. No helper should start the rendezvous, load the game, or execute a browser: the current smoke must continue driving the real native and WASM hosts with the existing protocol and assertions.

**Existing coverage and missing verification.** `tests/layers/grid-smoke.mjs:57–80` exercises successful native movement, checkpoint receipt, reconnect, pure-JavaScript console requests, browser-host movement, and host restoration. It does not directly test the static handler's error paths. Consolidation needs focused checks for directory URL behavior, encoded/path traversal inputs on Windows and POSIX, missing files, MIME types, and port errors, followed by the existing smoke. The fleet runners' other logic was not fully audited, so their caller assumptions require review before adopting a shared helper.

**Line accounting and tradeoff.** Grid's removable request-handler block is 12 physical lines, plus its one-line listen call; replacing it requires imports and a call, and creating a helper costs lines. Moving only this block is not a maintained-line reduction. Across all three copies there is real duplicate logic, but repository-wide net savings are **not estimated** without resolving their compatibility differences and counting helper verification. Shared setup is worth considering only with multiple callers; a configurable static-server framework would likely cost more than it removes. Confidence is high that duplication exists, medium that a coordinated extraction provides a worthwhile net reduction, and low that a grid-only extraction is useful.

#### 2. Retain the Rust recovery and peer-ordering surface: test-only consumers are the demonstration

**Evidence and callers.** `examples/grid/src/lib.rs:49–85` uses `CommandQueue` and `LockstepSession` to admit and order movement and enforce readiness. `lib.rs:111–118` wraps runtime chunk production; `lib.rs:152–160` receives chunks and restores the checkpoint. The native host submits local moves at `examples/grid/src/main.rs:41–42` and advances at line 57. The WASM wrapper submits and advances at `examples/grid/src/browser.rs:13–17`. The multi-host and chunk-recovery APIs are exercised directly by `examples/grid/src/grid_tests.rs:4–31`, `35–49`, `53–66`, and `70–88` rather than by either page's normal single-host session.

**LOC assessment.** Removing these APIs because the interactive host does not use them would remove required independent proof. `pasm/spec/architecture/reusable-layers.yaml` explicitly requires this separate game to demonstrate ordering, digest divergence, and recovery. Moving that proof into Phoenix simulation tests would also defeat independence. The four Rust tests cover materially different behavior; they are not redundant copies of the smoke.

`recover`'s completion accumulator is likewise not safely replaced with an early return merely to delete a few lines: the existing loop continues feeding later chunks to the receiver and propagates a later error before mutating Grid. The restore path at `lib.rs:119–150` constructs and validates a temporary session and pending queue before assigning running state. Reusing ordinary `receive` by mutating the live Grid during restore would shorten repeated insertion code at the cost of its transactional behavior and different validation rules.

**Required invariants and verification.** Keep canonical ordering under delayed/duplicate delivery, the readiness barrier, issuer-sequence continuity, pending moves, digest-history comparison, and failure without partial state mutation. Existing tests cover those principal examples. The rejection test at `grid_tests.rs:53–66` checks a damaged chunk and an invalid submitted move; it does not directly exercise every checkpoint-validation branch or a corrupt chunk after completion. Such additional verification would be necessary before changing those paths; the current coverage must not be represented as exhaustive.

**Tradeoff, confidence, and savings.** The short game facade makes the reusable runtime usable without exposing Phoenix domain types. Deleting it would reduce lines by reducing the specified demonstration. No deletion recommended; zero qualifying savings. Confidence is high based on all callers within the assigned scope and the declared architecture requirement.

#### 3. Retain target adapters, codec seam, and independent smoke assertions; apparent compression gains are not maintainability gains

**Observed code.** Native hosting at `examples/grid/src/main.rs:28–75` uses typed `RelayTransport`, reliable targeted recovery, snapshot broadcasts, and optional atomic file replacement. Browser hosting at `app.js:33–60` loads the WASM wrapper dynamically, handles browser connections, uses the snapshot channel when available, and persists to localStorage. `examples/grid/src/browser.rs:5–33` is the wasm-bindgen boundary, not a second simulation. `examples/grid/src/protocol.rs:9–61` owns typed vocabulary and transport adaptation; `examples/grid/src/codec.rs:1–8` centralizes JSON serialization/error conversion used by the protocol, checkpoint code, and WASM state export.

**Why these are not deletable copies.** Native filesystem persistence and browser localStorage implement different platform contracts. Rust protocol matching and JavaScript message handling exist in different execution environments. Loading the Rust host into the console to eliminate JavaScript protocol references would violate pure-JavaScript console operation: the only import of `pkg/phoenix_grid.js` is inside the host click handler at `app.js:36`, and `grid-smoke.mjs:48,64` explicitly checks native-console WASM requests. Importing Phoenix's GUI adapters would also violate the independent-example boundary enforced by `scripts/check-layers.mjs:51–62`.

The two snapshot sends in `app.js:55–56` are intentional delivery alternatives. The shared connection adapter exposes a raw `snapshotChannel` (`packages/transport/src/rendezvous-transport.js:348–363`) and a reliable `send` operation (line 388); there is no equivalent public snapshot-send convenience operation in that inspected adapter that allows this branch to disappear unchanged. Replacing it with reliable sends would change behavior.

The eight-line codec file already eliminates repeated serialization/error mapping. Removing it would distribute those operations among its callers. The explicit movement whitelist at `lib.rs:177–181` should not become arithmetic on arbitrary signed input for a shorter expression: `grid_tests.rs:61–66` deliberately submits extreme signed values. The 89-line Rust test file and 85-line smoke test remain proportionate for these separate guarantees. Parameterizing their visibly similar actions would introduce setup options for different host lifecycles without an established net deletion.

**Verification and limitations.** Preserve native and browser host launch, successful movement, reconnect, reliable recovery messages, browser checkpoint reload, and console laziness. The smoke's no-WASM assertion occurs before its browser-host half, so it is not evidence of a second independent no-WASM assertion after that transition. No source change is proposed here, and no tests were run. Confidence is high that the cited target boundaries and tests are required; no qualifying line savings identified.

#### Coverage and exclusions

All maintained Grid source and assigned smoke code were read. Generated `examples/grid/pkg/` output was excluded from maintained-line accounting: `.gitignore:1` ignores it, and `scripts/build-grid.mjs:5–6` regenerates it through Cargo and wasm-bindgen. No generated binding reductions, documentation deletion, minification, test deletion, or formatting compression are proposed. `app.js` and `index.html` already put multiple operations on individual physical lines, so raw line counts particularly understate their maintenance surface.

The transport, runtime, platform, fleet matrix scripts, and Workshop server were inspected only at the dependencies/callers cited above; their full implementations, test suites, and security properties remain outside this audit. No builds, tests, formatting, installations, browser sessions, or network activity were performed. The only written artifact is this report.
